soft archive

the journal.

notes, observations, the things you'd otherwise lose.

Jul 8, 2026 · Tue
jazer. dandan's father. the one before rhyle.

gaslighter from the start. love bombed me the first few weeks. never mentioned he had a wife and son. when his wife contacted me, he said it was his brother's wife. he doesn't have a brother.

proposed to me knowing it would never go through. asked my mom for ₱20k for the wedding. useless.

ran off with him after the pandemic. didn't realize he was with his wife during lockdown. then we had dandan.

while i was pregnant, while we were living together, he would leave for hours to call his wife and kids. came back like nothing happened. my mom supported us financially for 6 months because he refused to work, kept saying he was looking for jobs but no one ever contacted him.

my brother changed the way he treated me because i kept choosing jazer.

the whole time we were together — flirting with other girls, meeting them, sleeping with them.

almost 6 years no contact now but he still bugs me on instagram nonstop.

after me: 1 long-term gf (2 kids), 1 one night stand (1 kid), and a rumor he impregnated a 15 year old.

he was the first one who broke me.
Jul 8, 2026 · Tue
rhyle christian. the guy after jazer. met around july 2022 after he saw me in calatrava. he was married dec 2021 but his wife died july 2022 from cancer. had a premature daughter, exact replica of him — dark thick brows, slightly downturned eyes.

we started talking october 2022. first date at boulevard, san carlos. he was a basketball player, played for ucpb in manila, known in san carlos basketball circles. quiet person, deep voice, sounded sweet and sad. i fell for his sadness because i related to it.

december 24, 2022 — i asked what we were. he said bf/gf. that made me happy.

he kept calling me his peace. his pahinga.

but he was nonchalant. wouldn't reply for days. only talked when he wanted to come over. i had such bad anxiety waiting for him. made him my world. my time revolved around when he'd come back.

he was good with dandan. they'd play, i took photos/videos. we'd go to beaches, cook together (he loved home cooked meals), watch movies or basketball youtube or those short corny dramas he loves.

his red car with white stripes. headlights i could see from afar — the ones i waited for every night. hours on short trips uphill. eyes crinkling when he laughs. goofy jokes, waiting for me to laugh. the teasing.

wila (dandan's yaya) didn't like him. my friends didn't like him. i was lost to the world for years.

first breakup: found him talking to another girl. second: found him meeting up with another. endless cycle of other girls. march-july 2024 we weren't together but he still came to me. found countless instagram messages to girls. found out he had a 40+ year old girlfriend — mayor's daughter from calatrava, probably for connections/projects. he's a project manager, his late wife's family has a construction company in bacolod.

he kept saying i was too young to understand (i was 23, he was 29). sometimes compared me to his wife. that hurt.

officially ended july 2024. relapse march 2026 — saw him but realized feelings were gone. no anger. just compassion. i see him as someone who lost his way after he lost his wife.

my mom liked him. he has a sweetness that's endearing.

i was his pahinga. but i was never enough to make him stay.

I'll Always Be Here

collected works · for dandan
Your little hand on my nose The other is pulling me close I'm holding back my tears Just thinking of all the years And I know this won't last Cause time is moving too fast But we can stay right here For a while, my dear You'll grow up and live your life Go to school and learn to drive Time will push you away But know that I'll always stay Even though they'll break your heart Call me up and we'll bandage your scar I'll kiss your nose and whisper in your ear Mama will always be here…

I am enough.

collected works
I am enough. I will be enough. It's sad you couldn't see that. It's sad you couldn't see what I was. I'm not a waste. I'm not just a phase. I'm everything. And someday you'll realize that. But I'm not gonna be there for you anymore.

i'm not sorry

collected works
you don't love me you never did. you used me and i let you.. because i love you. i loved you even when you weren't there for me. i loved you even when you cheated on me. i loved you even when you compared me to her. i loved you even when you left me alone. i loved you even when you told me i wasn't enough. i loved you even when you broke my heart. i loved you even when you lied. i loved you even when you showed me you didn't. i loved you but now i'm exhausted. i can't love you anymore i don't have love to give you anymore. i can't love you anymore i don't even love myself anymore. so i'm sorry if i left you on seen i'm sorry if i can't respond to your messages. i'm sorry if i can't forgive you. i'm sorry if i can't see you again. i'm sorry if i can't be with you. i'm sorry if i can't accept you now. but then... i'm not sorry anymore.

Fortress

Jun 19, 2026 · Fri
written by kat · taylor swift vibes · capo 4
Verse 1 I learned to build these walls so young Brick by brick until the sun went down Everyone who knocked just wanted in To take whatever warmth that I had found Pre-Chorus And I'm so good at holding still While everything around me falls Chorus I'm the one they lean on but I don't lean back I'm the keeper of a hundred cracks And I smile through the weight of it all A fortress who's waiting for the walls to fall Verse 2 You showed up like you could read my mind Saw the parts I never let them see But feelings without action are just lies And potential's not the same as choosing me Pre-Chorus I know better than to wait around For someone scared to make the call Chorus I'm the one they lean on but I don't lean back I'm the keeper of a hundred cracks And I smile through the weight of it all A fortress who's waiting for the walls to fall Bridge Maybe I was taught that love means sacrifice So the quiet ones don't feel like love at all Maybe I'm the reason that I'm still alone Building something no one asked me for Final Chorus But what if I could lean and not collapse What if someone held me through the cracks What if I don't have to be so tall What if someone had the key after all Outro A fortress... waiting for the walls to fall
songwriting playbook

the craft.

techniques for writing songs that stick. learned from taylor swift, applied to your lyrics.

dynamics & structure

Drop Technique

Chorus starts sparser and lower energy than the verse, builds tension, then hits a moment of silence before exploding into a high-energy post-chorus. Creates anticipation. Example: "I Knew You Were Trouble"

S.I.A. (Section Impact Accentuator)

Pull parts or all instrumentation out to spotlight a hook, usually at the end of a chorus. Everything cuts out so the hook lands harder. Example: "Love Story", "Lover"

vocal & lyrical techniques

Vocal Register Contrast

Shift between low and high registers based on what the lyrics need. Low register for negative/sad lyrics, high register for passion/desire. The register matches the emotion. Example: "Cardigan"

Production Mirrors Lyrics

Tiny sounds that make the lyric land. "High heels on cobblestones" sound in Cardigan, pen click in "Blank Space" before "and I'll write your name"

Duets with Countering Lyrics

Two voices singing different sides of the story simultaneously. He sings "you never gave a warning sign" while she sings "i gave so many signs" — listeners hear both perspectives at once. Example: "Exile"

Changing Chorus Lyrics

Most pop songs repeat the exact same chorus. Change the words each time to move the story forward. Over half of Taylor's hits do this. Creates narrative momentum.

signature moments

"Swiftisms"

Signature vocal embellishments — laughs, quips, spoken words, clever hooks spotlighted by silence. "Like, ever" and the laugh in "We Are Never Getting Back Together." These became iconic but she phased them out after Reputation to avoid predictability.

Nonsense Lyrics with Purpose

The "whoa" in "Cruel Summer" isn't random — it rhymes with the hook AND sounds like "woe" which fits the negative emotion. Even filler words serve a purpose.

karen's song structure

Verse 1 (8 lines, AABB)
Chorus (4 lines + 2 echo lines)
Verse 2 (8 lines, AABB)
Chorus (4 lines + 2 echo lines)
Bridge (4 lines + 2 powerful lines)
Final Chorus (with variation)
Outro (2-4 lines, soft landing)

Style options: soft acoustic folk OR indie folk chamber pop à la Folklore, OR Hozier-inspired soulful folk rock with blues/gospel undertones (80-85 bpm), OR OPM rock ballad like December Avenue meets Moira Dela Torre, OR alternative pop rock (Olivia Rodrigo meets Florence + The Machine)

vocabulary notes

Elevated vocabulary for lyrics: reckless desire, oblivious to the war, gravity of your gaze, fortress built from sand, paralyzed, undertow, surrender this charade, cascade, paradigm, dissolve, sanctuary, remnants, phantom, silhouette, constellation, unraveling, fractured, illuminate, remnants of what we were

Balance poetic with conversational. Mix complex metaphors with accessible everyday phrasing so the storyline stays easy to grasp.

daily wisdom

classic figures.

mythology, philosophy, history, literature. one figure every day at 10am EST. learning never stops.

Christine de Pizan

1364–c.1430 · Venetian-born French poet, historian, polemicist · widowed at twenty-five with three children and a household to feed, she became the first woman in Europe to make a living entirely from writing — and then used it to pick a public fight with the most beloved book of her century

aug 12, 2026▼

childhood & early life

She was born Cristina da Pizzano in Venice in September 1364, into the most useful accident of birth a clever girl could have had in the fourteenth century: her father was a physician and astrologer, and he was good enough at it to get headhunted. Tommaso di Benvenuto da Pizzano had two offers on the table, one from the King of Hungary and one from Charles V of France. He took France. Christine was five when her mother put her on the road to Paris to join him.

What she walked into was the best-funded reading room in Europe. Charles V was building the royal library in a tower of the Louvre, panelled in rare wood, hung with some thirty small chandeliers and one big silver lamp that was kept burning day and night so that work never had to stop for darkness. Twelve hundred manuscripts. Translators on staff, turning Greek, Latin and Arabic into French so students could actually use the collection. That tower is the seed the Bibliothèque Nationale grew out of, and a foreign astrologer's daughter had the run of it.

Her father encouraged her to learn, which was not the default setting. Her mother, by Christine's own later account, thought the girl should be doing needlework like everybody else, and the compromise reached in that house is basically the reason we have any of this. She read constantly. She married at fifteen, in 1379, to Étienne du Castel, a notary and secretary to the king, a Picard about ten years older whom she had known since she was small. By every line she ever wrote about him, she loved him, and she was happy, and she assumed it would keep.

Then the wheel turned. Charles V died in 1380, and the household's protector was gone. Her father lost his position, his pension shrank, and he died around 1388. The next year, in 1389, Étienne rode to Beauvais on the king's business, caught plague, and died there at thirty-four. Christine was twenty-five. She had three children, a widowed mother and a niece in the house, no income, and a pile of unpaid royal salary she was legally owed and could not get anyone to hand over. The lawsuits went on for years. She lost most of them.

what she did & why she's remembered

She did the thing nobody in her position did. A well-born widow with debts had three respectable options in 1390 — remarry, take vows, or move in with relatives and get quiet. Christine looked at the three of them and started selling poems instead.

She began with love ballades, because that was the market. Nobles wanted verse and would pay for it, and she was extremely good and extremely fast — hundreds of poems inside a decade, some in her own voice, many written to order for men who wanted to sound heartbroken at somebody. She was blunt about the difference: often, she says, she was making merry on the page and would rather have been weeping. Then she used the money and the access to educate herself properly, on history, philosophy, Aristotle, Dante, and moved into prose, where the real money and the real arguments were.

And she ran it like a business, which is the part usually left out of the romantic version. She did not simply hand manuscripts to a patron and hope. She oversaw the copying, employed scribes and illuminators, supervised the miniatures, and assembled bespoke presentation volumes for specific rich people — the huge collected manuscript she put together for Queen Isabeau is still in the British Library. She was author, editor, production manager and account handler of her own small press, three hundred years before an author could expect royalties. Henry IV of England tried to poach her for his court. So did the Duke of Milan. She thanked them beautifully and refused, twice, because France was at war and she was not going to go work for the other side.

Then in 1399 she picked the fight. The most fashionable book in France was the Roman de la Rose, and the section of it written by Jean de Meun was, underneath the allegory, several thousand lines instructing men that women are deceitful, that their refusals are theatre, and that persistence is the correct technique. Christine wrote L'Epistre au Dieu d'Amours — a formal complaint to the God of Love, filed on behalf of women of all ranks — and then kept going into a documented, circulated, years-long literary war, the querelle de la Rose, against senior royal secretaries who were absolutely not used to being answered back. Her flattest and best reply to the charge that all the books condemn women: the books were not written by women. And to the argument that women are naturally weak and easily fooled, she asks why, in that case, the great Jean de Meun needed to fill a hundred pages with tricks and stratagems, since one does not lay siege to a place that isn't defended.

Out of that came Le Livre de la Cité des Dames in 1405, her masterpiece: a city built stone by stone out of the lives of real and legendary women, designed as a fortress for the reputation of the whole sex. In it she argues that God made the soul, and made it as good in a woman as in a man; that intelligence is not sexed; that girls taught the same subjects as boys learn them just as well; and, in a passage that lands strangely hard for 1405, that a woman without means should think hard before marrying a second time, because plenty of wives get a beating for supper when the husband comes home from enjoying himself. She wrote a treatise on warfare so competent that Caxton printed it in English in 1489 by order of Henry VII, and it was still being cited as an authority under Henry VIII. She is, on the record, the first woman in Europe to have supported a family entirely by her pen.

family life

Her father was her first patron and her first ally, the one who decided she should be taught. Her mother wanted a normal daughter. Christine records the disagreement without bitterness and it is clear which parent she was arguing with for the rest of her life; a fair amount of the Cité des Dames is a grown woman still answering that objection.

She and Étienne had three children in ten years. A daughter, born around 1381, who eventually took vows at the royal convent of Poissy. A son who died as a baby. And Jean, born about 1385, whose childhood is its own strange story: the Earl of Salisbury, visiting the French court, was so taken with the boy that he took him back to England to be brought up in his own household — and then Salisbury was killed in the rebellion against Henry IV. Jean ended up in the hands of the English king, who offered Christine a position at his court in exchange, essentially, for coming to get her son. She spent three years negotiating him home rather than take the job. He came back to France, worked as a secretary like his father, and predeceased her.

And she carried everyone. After 1389 the household was Christine, her mother, her niece and the children, and the only earner was her. She writes about that in the most unromantic terms available — the debts, the creditors, the court appearances, the humiliation of arguing for money that was legally hers and being made to wait years for it by men who found the whole thing faintly amusing. The famous line about herself from this period is that she had to become a man: je devins vrai homme. She did not mean it happily. She meant that the work, the fighting and the providing had all landed on her, and she had done them, and she wanted that noticed.

romantic life

One marriage, ten years, and then forty years of writing about love for money while refusing to have any.

The marriage was arranged, in the sense that her father chose Étienne, and it was also a love match, which she seems to have understood was luck rather than merit. She wrote a poem about their wedding night that has no medieval business existing — not allegory, not courtly convention, just a fifteen-year-old girl reporting that the man did not touch her, that he kissed her a hundred times before morning, and that he told her God had made him for her. It is quoted in full below. Read it and then remember she was writing it as a widow.

She never remarried. She was twenty-five, well connected, and it would have solved the money immediately, which is exactly the reason it is worth noticing that she didn't. Instead she turned the grief into product: the Cent Ballades open with a run of widow poems that are among the most exposed things in medieval French, and then slide, without comment, into commissioned love lyrics for other people's affairs. She tells you outright in the prologue that she is doing this. That double register — performing feeling professionally while the real one sits underneath, unavailable — is the most modern thing about her.

Her actual position on romance, once she stopped writing to order, is unsentimental to the point of cold. In The Book of the Duke of True Lovers she builds a lush, beautiful courtly love story about a young prince and a married lady — the tournaments, the coded letters, the go-betweens — and then drops a letter into the middle of it, from an older woman, the Lady of La Tour, which quietly takes the whole genre apart. The letter tells the lady that the servants have already noticed she has started laughing more, withdrawing more, and whispering with a favoured few, and that this is how everyone knows. That the fantasy of a love that is pure because nothing physical happens is exactly that, a fantasy. That she has watched noble women lose everything on suspicion alone, with nothing ever proved. Christine wrote the seduction and then wrote the invoice, and left both in the same book.

life until death

The second half of her life is a country coming apart while she writes memos nobody reads. Charles VI went mad in recurring fits, and while the king was absent from his own mind the Dukes of Orléans and Burgundy fought over the regency until it became civil war. Christine wrote to Queen Isabeau begging her to intervene. She wrote treatises on the duties of princes to the people. She wrote, in Le Livre de la Paix, an argument for a strong middle class as the thing that holds a kingdom's two extremes together, which was not a commonplace idea in 1413. Orléans was assassinated in the street. Then in 1415 came Agincourt, and the French nobility was cut down in an afternoon.

In 1418, with Paris fallen to the Burgundians and massacres in the streets, she went into the abbey at Poissy where her daughter was a nun, and stopped publishing. Eleven years of silence from the most prolific writer in France. We do not know what she was doing in there beyond one devotional book about human life as a prison.

Then in 1429 a teenage girl from Domrémy broke the siege of Orléans and walked the disinherited dauphin to his coronation at Reims, and Christine came out of the abbey at sixty-four to write one last poem. The Ditié de Jehanne d'Arc, finished on 31 July 1429, eleven days after the coronation, is the only work about Joan of Arc written in French during Joan's own lifetime, and the only one by a woman. It opens with her announcing, after eleven years of weeping in a walled abbey, that she is going to start laughing. The two of them never met.

She died around 1430, at Poissy, aged about sixty-six. The date is not recorded, and there is no tomb. Almost certainly she died before Joan was burned at Rouen in May 1431, which is the single mercy in the whole arrangement: the last thing she wrote was a woman winning. Then she disappeared for four hundred years. The nineteenth century mostly remembered her as a curiosity, and it took until the 1980s and Earl Jeffrey Richards's translation of the Cité des Dames for the argument she actually made to get read again on its own terms. She has been dead six centuries and the fight she picked is still on the schedule.

her works

Christine wrote in Middle French and most of her verse has never had a good public-domain English translation, so this section mixes two things, and says which is which. Where an out-of-copyright translation exists — Laurence Binyon and Eric Maclagan's 1908 versions of the ballads, Alice Kemp-Welch's prose, Constance Fletcher's Joan of Arc verses — it is used and credited. Where none exists, the Middle French is given from Maurice Roy's edition of the Œuvres poétiques with a plain literal English rendering underneath, marked as such. Nothing has been prettied up.

1. “Seulete suy.” Her most famous poem, and the one she is still in the anthologies for. Twenty-five lines, and the word seulete — alone, in a small and diminutive form, all alone, little and alone — opens twenty-two of them. It is a ballade, so it is built to a strict form, which is the point: she took the most controlled shape available to her and used it to say one word over and over until the form itself starts to sound like a symptom.

Seulete suy et seulete vueil estre,
Seulete m'a mon doulz ami laissiée,
Seulete suy, sanz compaignon ne maistre,
Seulete suy, dolente et courrouciée,
Seulete suy en langour mesaisiée,
Seulete suy plus que nulle esgarée,
Seulete suy sanz ami demourée.
Alone am I, and alone I want to be,
alone my sweet love has left me,
alone am I, without companion or master,
alone am I, grieving and full of anger,
alone am I, sick and languishing,
alone am I, more lost than anyone,
alone am I, left without my love.

Alone am I at the door or at the window,
alone am I, hidden away in a corner,
alone am I, feeding myself on tears,
alone am I, whether grieving or quiet,
alone am I, and nothing suits me so well,
alone am I, shut inside my room,
alone am I, left without my love.

Alone am I everywhere and in every place.
Alone am I whether I walk or sit,
alone am I more than any other thing on earth,
alone am I, abandoned by every one,
alone am I, brought hard and low,
alone am I, and often all in tears,
alone am I, left without my love.

Prince, now my sorrow has begun:
alone am I, threatened by every grief,
alone am I, stained darker than mulberry,
alone am I, left without my love.

— Cent Ballades XI. Literal rendering; French from Roy's edition.

2. “Doulce chose est que mariage.” The wedding-night poem. She is writing in the present tense of a marriage that was already over when she wrote it, which is either a professional exercise or the most self-inflicted thing in her collected works. Note what she picks out as evidence that he was good: that on the first night he did her no oultrage, asked for nothing, and kissed her a hundred times.

Doulce chose est que mariage,
Je le puis bien par moy prouver,
Voire a qui mary bon et sage
A, comme Dieu m'a fait trouver…
A sweet thing is marriage,
I can prove it well enough by myself,
truly, for anyone who has a good and wise husband
such as God granted me to find.
Praised be He, who I pray will keep him
safe for me; for his great goodness
I can prove by the fact of it,
and certainly the sweet man loves me well.

The very first night of the marriage
I could already prove
his great goodness, for he did me no
outrage that should have hurt me;
but, before it was time to get up,
a hundred times he kissed me, so I hold,
and asked for nothing coarser than that,
and certainly the sweet man loves me well.

And he said, in such gentle language,
“God has brought me to you,
sweet friend, and I believe
he raised me up for your sake.”
And so he went on dreaming out loud
all night long in just that way,
never straying from it,
and certainly the sweet man loves me well.

— Cent Ballades XXVI. Literal rendering; French from Roy's edition.

3. Two widow rondeaux. The first is dated 1396, which makes it seven years after Étienne died — she puts the arithmetic in the poem herself. The second is the one that starts with what she is wearing.

Like the turtledove without her mate I am all alone,
and like a sheep gone astray without a shepherd;
for by death I was parted, long since,
from my sweet mate, whom I miss at every hour.
It is seven years since I lost him, poor thing that I am —
better for me if I had been buried then!
Like the turtledove without her mate I am all alone.
For ever since, in grief and in want
and in very hard trouble I have stayed,
nor have I any hope, as long as I last,
of any solace that could put me back into joy;
like the turtledove without her mate I am all alone.

— Rondeaux I (1396). Literal rendering.
I am a widow, alone, and dressed in black,
my sad face plainly covered over;
in great anger and in a mourning manner
I carry the very bitter grief that is killing me.
And it is only right that I be beaten down,
full of weeping and saying little;
I am a widow, alone, and dressed in black.
Since I lost the one through whom the pain
keeps being called back to me, which drives me out of my senses,
all my good days and all my joy are gone,
my fortune struck down into a hard state;
I am a widow, alone, and dressed in black.

— Rondeaux III. Literal rendering.

4. “What use is it then to complain.” The shortest of the grief poems and the one with the hardest edge, because it is a woman interrogating her own mourning and concluding that it is useless and that she is going to keep doing it anyway.

What use is it, then, to complain,
    or to lament,
about the pain I carry,
when nothing can make it less?
    Rather it grows greater,
and will, until death.
Pain comes at me so hard
    that I cannot hold in
my great distress;
what use is it, then, to complain?
Since the one I loved without pretending
    death chose to take,
and in that it wronged me;
that put out my joy,
    nor since then have I reached
any pleasure at all;
what use is it, then, to complain?

— Rondeaux II. Literal rendering.

5. On Fortune. Fortune is her lifelong subject — she wrote an enormous book called La Mutation de Fortune about it — and her position is not the pious one. She says flatly that Fortune is not a blindfolded goddess spinning a wheel; it is something rooted in us. But in the ballades she is still furious with it.

What good does it do me, then, to knead and to bake,
to pull, to brew, to take on labour,
since Fortune is against me?

Long ago she pulled me out of joy,
and since then has not finished harming me;
she is still so angry with me
that she takes me constantly from bad to worse.
Whatever I build, she tears up.
And what profit could I possibly draw,
since Fortune is against me?

Her wild influence
I thought I could always beat
by doing well and holding out a long time,
expecting to see some hour of light
for me, the one bad luck chases.
But none of it is worth anything, I can get nowhere,
since Fortune is against me.

— Cent Ballades X. Literal rendering. Anyone who has ever taken on a second job knows the kneading-and-baking line.
Whoever trusts too much in Fortune's great gifts
is, in truth, deceived by them;
for she is more inconstant than the moon.
Plenty of the greatest have found this out,
and even those she has raised up high
she throws down fast, and it is seen often enough
that her joys are nothing but plain wind.

— Cent Ballades XII. Literal rendering.

6. From The Book of the Duke of True Lovers (1405). The courtly machinery, working at full power. These are the ballads a young prince sends to a married woman, in Laurence Binyon's 1908 translation, which keeps the original metres. Christine is writing a man writing to a woman — a woman ventriloquising the exact style of pleading she elsewhere warns women to distrust.

Sweet Lady, fair and gentle without peer,
Have mercy on me, who all thy words obey
Body and soul do I abandon here
Unto thy will, and humbly thus I pray:
  Come quickly nigh,
Have pity, and cure my sickness when I cry:
Oh, I beseech thee, graciously attend
And so consent to take me for thy friend.

To thee I give myself, O flower most dear.
For mercy I beseech, and wilt thou slay?
I charge thee by that Lord whom we revere
To lift this wrong that crushes me away.
  No help have I
From any other: leave me not to die!
See, faithfully I serve thee to the end,
And so consent to take me for thy friend.

Seest thou not how I shed full many a tear:
And if thy help for longer shall delay
I am but shent, what need to speak more clear?
Ah, love me, Love so holds me in his sway!
  Then hither hie,
Be merciful, for near to death I lie:
'Tis truth, thou knowest, I have no hope to mend,
And so consent to take me for thy friend.

Lady, I thank thee, and all my duty send,
And so consent to take me for thy friend.

— trans. Laurence Binyon & Eric Maclagan, 1908.

And here is the technically astonishing one. Every line begins by picking up the last word of the line before it — a form called rime annexée. She is showing off, and she has earned it.

In this sad world have pity, my lady dear,
Dear to me more than any other there:
Their pride you know not; let not gracious cheer
Cheer me at so great cost, oh white and fair!
Fare I thus ill, yet canst thou bid me see
Seasons of solace that may comfort me.

If for unfitness I be slighted here,
Here am I dead, and arrows of despair
Spare not to pierce my heart, and life grows drear,
Drear as my brooding on the doom I bear.
Bear witness, Love withholds in obduracy
Seasons of solace that might comfort me.

O loveliest one and sweetest, without peer,
Peerless in honour, of all bounties heir,
Ere I thy servant pine in sorry fear
Fear not a kind and gentle guise to wear.
Where shall I find, 'mid this deep dolorous sea,
Seasons of solace that may comfort me?

Dear Lady, grant in gracious courtesy
Seasons of solace that may comfort me.

— trans. Binyon & Maclagan, 1908.

7. The letter that ruins the love story. From the same book. The lady, unhappy in her marriage and falling into the affair, writes to her old governess asking her to come and be a confidante. This is the reply she gets, in Kemp-Welch's 1908 translation. It is the coldest and most useful thing Christine ever wrote about being watched.

My Lady, I have heard certain rumours touching your conduct which grieve me from the bottom of my heart because of the fear I have of the ruin of your good name…

And although the foregoing conditions, and all other usages befitting a noble princess, were aforetime observed by you, you now act quite otherwise, it is said, for you amuse yourself much more, and have become more communicative and mirthful than was your wont, and it is when the outward signs are changed, that one usually judges the disposition to be altered, and now you desire to be alone, and withdrawn from others save one or two of your waiting-women, and some of your dependants, with whom, even in the presence of others, you consult privately, and titter, and talk secretly, as if you well understood one another, and naught but the company of such pleases you, and the others can in nowise serve you to your liking, the which things and doings arouse envy in your other servants, and cause them to think that your heart is enamoured of some one.

Ah, my very sweet Lady, for God's sake remember who you are… and do not put trust in the vain fancy which many young women have, who permit themselves to believe that there is no wrong in loving with tender passion provided this is not accompanied by any wrongful act… and that it makes life more pleasurable, and that one thus makes a man gallant and renowned for aye. Ah, my dear Lady, it is quite the reverse… take warning from such noble ladies as you have seen in your time (and such there are), who, through being merely suspected of such love, have, without the truth ever becoming known, on this account lost both…

— the Lady of La Tour to her former charge, trans. Alice Kemp-Welch, 1908.

8. The Order of the Rose. In 1402 she wrote up an actual scene — an assembly at the Duke of Orléans's house where a company of men swore an oath to defend the reputation of women. She was given custody of the charter. This is the vow, in her Middle French:

A bonne amour je fais veu et promesse
Et à la fleur qui est rose clamée,
A la vaillant de Loyauté deesse,
Par qui nous est ceste chose informée,
Qu'à tous jours mais la bonne renommée
Je garderay de dame en toute chose
Ne par moy ja femme n'yert diffamée:
Et pour ce prens je L'Ordre de la Rose.
To good love I make this vow and promise,
and to the flower that is called the rose,
and to the valiant goddess Loyalty,
by whom this thing is made known to us:
that from this day forward I will guard
the good name of women in all things,
and no woman shall ever be slandered by me.
And for this I take the Order of the Rose.

— Le Dit de la Rose, ll. 197–204. Literal rendering.

9. Two lines she wrote for her son. Her advice on the Roman de la Rose, compressed into a couplet, in the middle of a book of moral proverbs written to a boy.

Se bien veulx et chastement vivre,
De la Rose ne lis le livre.

If you want to live well and cleanly,
do not read the book of the Rose.

And her single flattest sentence in the whole querelle, on why the literary record is so uniformly against women, as reported by Kemp-Welch: to the charge that books are full of the condemnation of women, she replies with the simple remark that books were not written by women.

10. Le Ditié de Jehanne d'Arc, 31 July 1429. Sixty-four years old, eleven years silent, writing about a nineteen-year-old girl in armour. The opening stanza first, from the Middle French:

Je, Christine, qui ay plouré
XI ans en abbaye close,
Où j'ay tousjours puis demouré…
Ore à prime me prens à rire;

I, Christine, who have wept
eleven years in a closed abbey,
where I have stayed ever since
Charles — and this is a strange thing! —
the king's son, if I dare say it,
fled straight out of Paris
because of the treason shut up inside it:
now, for the first time, I begin to laugh.

— stanza I. Literal rendering.

And four verses in Constance Fletcher's 1913 English, which is the last public-domain verse translation of her that exists, and gets the joy of it:

O Thou! ordainèd Maid of very God!
Joanna! born in Fortune's golden hour,
On thee the Holy Spirit pours His Flood
And His high grace is given thee for dower.
Now all great gifts are thine:—O blessed be He
That lent thee life!—how word my grateful prayer?
—No prayer of thine was spoken fruitlessly,
O Maid of God! O Joan! O Virgin rare!

Mark me this portent! strange beyond all telling!
How this despoilèd Kingdom stricken lay,
And no man raised his hand to guard his dwelling,
Until a Woman came to show the way.
Until a Woman (since no man dare try)
Rallied the land and bade the traitors fly.
Honour to Womankind! It needs must be
That God loves Woman, since He fashioned Thee!

O strange! This little maid sixteen years old
On whom no harness weigheth overmuch.
So strong the little hands! enduring hold
She seemeth fed by that same armour's touch,
Nurtured on iron—as before her vanish
The enemies of her triumphal day;
And this by many men is witnessèd;
Yea, many eyes be witness of that fray!

Castles and towns, she wins them back for France,
And France is free again, and this her doing!
Never was power given as to her lance!
A thousand swords could do no more pursuing.
Of all staunch men and true she is the Chief,
Captain and Leader, for that she alone
Is braver than Achilles the brave Greek.
All praise be given to God who leadeth Joan!

— trans. Constance Fletcher (“George Fleming”), in Alice Kemp-Welch, Of Six Mediæval Women, 1913.

Honour to Womankind! It needs must be / That God loves Woman, since He fashioned Thee. That is the last surviving thing Christine de Pizan wrote. She had spent forty years arguing that women were worth the ink, mostly against men who found the argument funny, and then a farm girl with a sword walked into the middle of her retirement and proved it in public. She got to see it. She did not live to see it undone.

Sources: Œuvres poétiques de Christine de Pisan, ed. Maurice Roy (Société des anciens textes français, 1886–96), vols. I–II, for all Middle French; The Book of the Duke of True Lovers, trans. Alice Kemp-Welch, ballads by Laurence Binyon and Eric R. D. Maclagan (Chatto & Windus, 1908); Alice Kemp-Welch, Of Six Mediæval Women (Macmillan, 1913), chapter on Christine, including Constance Fletcher's verses from the Ditié. Biographical dates checked against Britannica and current scholarship; the literal English renderings of the French are mine and make no claim to be poetry.

Virginia Woolf

1882–1941 · English novelist, essayist, printer · she was never allowed to go to school, so she read her father's entire library instead, then rebuilt the English sentence so it could finally hold what a mind actually does in a day

aug 12, 2026▼

childhood & early life

She was born Adeline Virginia Stephen on 25 January 1882 at 22 Hyde Park Gate, South Kensington, into a house with eight children in it and not one of the girls at school. Her father, Sir Leslie Stephen, was a critic, a mountaineer and the founding editor of the Dictionary of National Biography — a man who had catalogued the lives of thousands of eminent Englishmen and could not imagine sending his daughter to be educated. Her mother, Julia, was a famous beauty who had modelled for the photographer Julia Margaret Cameron, her aunt, and who spent her life nursing other people. Both parents had been widowed. Julia brought three Duckworth children into the marriage, Leslie brought a daughter, and together they had four more: Vanessa, Thoby, Virginia, Adrian.

Virginia's education was piecemeal, done at the kitchen table by her parents and later by tutors in Greek and Latin. What saved her was that her father, having decided she could not be schooled, also decided she could read anything. She said so plainly years later: “Even today there may be parents who would doubt the wisdom of allowing a girl of fifteen the free run of a large and quite unexpurgated library. But my father allowed it. There were certain facts – very briefly, very shyly he referred to them. Yet 'Read what you like', he said, and all his books … were to be had without asking.” The result was a woman better read than any of the Cambridge men she would spend her life talking to, and permanently, structurally furious that she had not been allowed to sit in their rooms.

She started writing early and never really stopped: letters at five, a family newspaper called the Hyde Park Gate News from the age of ten, and a diary from 1897 that she kept for twelve years. Every summer until she was thirteen the family took Talland House at St Ives, with a view across Porthminster Bay to the Godrevy Lighthouse. That house is the engine room of her whole imagination. She would spend the rest of her life writing books that are, one way or another, attempts to get back into it.

Then everything collapsed in a decade. Her mother died in May 1895, when Virginia was thirteen, and the breakdown that followed was the first of many. Her half-sister Stella, who stepped into the mothering role, married in April 1897 and was dead by July. Her father died in February 1904 and she went into a crisis that included at least one attempt on her own life. She later summarised the years 1897 to 1904 in four words: the seven unhappy years. In an essay written in 1939 she also disclosed, for the first time, that she had been sexually abused as a child by her half-brother Gerald Duckworth, with further improprieties from George during the years he was supposedly escorting her and Vanessa into society. She was blunt about that society too: “a very competent, perfectly complacent, ruthless machine. A girl had no chance against its fangs. No other desires — say to paint, or to write — could be taken seriously.”

what she did & why she's remembered

After their father's death the Stephen children sold the South Kensington house and moved to Bloomsbury, which was cheaper and, to their relations, faintly disgraceful. Thoby's Cambridge friends started coming round on Thursday evenings — Lytton Strachey, Clive Bell, Maynard Keynes, Leonard Woolf — and the Bloomsbury Group was simply that: a set of people who talked to each other for thirty years without pretending to be respectable. In 1906 the siblings travelled to Greece and Turkey; Thoby caught typhoid and died at twenty-six. Two days later Vanessa accepted Clive Bell's proposal. That is the tempo of Woolf's life. Loss, then someone getting married, then work.

She married Leonard Woolf in August 1912 and published her first novel, The Voyage Out, in 1915. In 1917 the two of them bought a small hand printing press and set it up in the dining room at Hogarth House, partly as occupational therapy for her nerves. The Hogarth Press ended up publishing T. S. Eliot, Katherine Mansfield, the first English translations of Freud, and every book Woolf wrote after 1917. That last part matters more than it sounds: she is one of the very few major novelists in English who never had to submit a manuscript to a man for approval. She could write anything, and she did.

What she actually invented is hard to overstate and easy to miss, because everyone steals it now. The Victorian novel told you what a character did and then what it meant. Woolf threw out the scaffolding and wrote consciousness directly — a mind moving sideways through a single day, hauling in memory, weather, other people's faces, a bus, a regret from twenty years ago, all in one unbroken sentence held together by semicolons. Mrs Dalloway (1925) is one June day in London. To the Lighthouse (1927) is two days ten years apart, with the entire decade between them — a war, three deaths — disposed of in a middle section narrated by an empty house. Orlando (1928) is a mock-biography of a poet who lives four hundred years and changes sex halfway through without particularly minding.

And then A Room of One's Own (1929), built from two talks she gave to women students at Cambridge, which is the most durable argument about women and work ever written, mostly because it refuses to be inspirational. Her thesis is money and a door that locks. Not genius, not encouragement, not believing in yourself. Five hundred a year and a lock. She followed it in 1938 with Three Guineas, which connects the tyranny of the Victorian father directly to European fascism and which people liked considerably less.

family life

The Stephen household was a blended one and a crowded one: George, Stella and Gerald Duckworth from her mother's first marriage; Laura Stephen from her father's, a girl considered defective who was eventually put in an institution and effectively erased from the family; and the four full siblings. Virginia was the seventh child of the house. She adored her mother and lost her at thirteen, and spent thirty years being haunted by her until she wrote To the Lighthouse and found, to her surprise, that the haunting stopped. Mrs Ramsay in that book is Julia Stephen, down to the way she made people fall in love at dinner.

Her sister Vanessa was the great constant — painter, mother, rival, the one person she never stopped writing to. Thoby's death at twenty-six in 1906 is the wound she kept reopening on the page; he is Jacob in Jacob's Room and Percival in The Waves, both of them young men who are only ever described from the outside by people who loved them and then lose them.

She had no children. Woolf wanted them; Leonard, after consulting doctors and watching her breakdowns, decided she was not strong enough and refused. Whether he was protecting her or managing her is one of the permanent arguments about their marriage, and she never fully stopped grieving it. Vanessa's children were the ones she wrote silly letters to, and Vanessa's fertility was a thing she could look at all her life from just outside the window.

romantic life

Leonard Woolf first saw the Stephen sisters in Thoby's rooms at Cambridge and remembered them in “white dresses and large hats, with parasols in their hands, their beauty literally took one's breath away.” He then went off to run a colonial district in Ceylon for seven years, came home on leave in 1911 after Lytton Strachey wrote to him about Virginia, and never went back. He proposed in January 1912. She said no, kept seeing him, and on 29 May told him she loved him. They married that August. He resigned his career for her.

The marriage was not sexually successful and both of them knew it early. What it was instead was a working partnership of unusual completeness: a press they ran together, a house in Sussex, and a man who spent nearly thirty years watching for the signs of an oncoming collapse and enforcing milk and rest and no visitors. There is a version of this story where Leonard is a jailer. There is another where he is the reason she got twenty-nine more years and eight more books. Both versions have evidence.

The love affair of her life was with Vita Sackville-West: aristocrat, novelist, gardener, married to a diplomat, considerably more experienced and considerably less clever. They met in December 1922 and the affair ran through the mid-twenties. Vita's husband knew. Leonard knew. The letters are extraordinary because the roles keep swapping — the famous stylist insisting she is not making phrases, the glamorous one admitting she has no defences left.

I am reduced to a thing that wants Virginia. I composed a beautiful letter to you in the sleepless nightmare hours of the night, and it has all gone: I just miss you, in a quite simple desperate human way … Damn you, spoilt creature; I shan't make you love me any the more by giving myself away like this — But oh my dear, I can't be clever and stand-offish with you: I love you too much for that.
— Vita Sackville-West to Virginia Woolf, from Milan, 21 January 1926
Your letter from Trieste came this morning — But why do you think I don't feel, or that I make phrases? 'Lovely phrases' you say which rob things of reality. Just the opposite. Always, always, always I try to say what I feel … And ever since, nothing important has happened — Somehow its dull and damp. I have been dull; I have missed you. I do miss you. I shall miss you. And if you don't believe it, you're a longeared owl and ass.
— Virginia Woolf to Vita, 26 January 1926

In 1927 she wrote to Vita: “Look here Vita — throw over your man, and we'll go to Hampton Court and dine on the river together and walk in the garden in the moonlight.” Vita did not throw over her man. The affair cooled into a friendship that lasted until Woolf's death. What Woolf did instead was write Orlando in about three months and put Vita in it as the immortal hero — Vita's own son later called it “the longest and most charming love-letter in literature.” It is the only break-up in history where the injured party responded by inventing a genre.

life until death

She was ill her whole adult life with something that had no name yet and that we would probably now call bipolar disorder. The pattern was consistent: finish a book, correct the proofs, collapse. Headaches, insomnia, racing speech, voices, months in bed. In September 1913, while correcting the proofs of her first novel, she took an overdose of Veronal and was saved by a surgeon who happened to be a friend of the family. The treatment on offer in that era was rest cures, milk, forced weight gain and the confiscation of pen and paper — which is why the doctors in Mrs Dalloway, Holmes and Bradshaw, are written with a cold hatred that has not aged a day.

Between the collapses she worked constantly and lived well: London, Monk's House at Rodmell with its garden and its view across the Ouse to the Downs, walking, printing, reviewing, hosting, arguing. Then the war came. Their London house was bombed. Her friend Roger Fry had died and she had written his biography. She and Leonard, a Jew, kept petrol in the garage and an agreement to kill themselves rather than be taken if the Germans landed. She finished Between the Acts in early 1941 and, as always, the finishing of a book pulled the floor out.

On 28 March 1941 she wrote letters to Leonard and to Vanessa, put on her coat, weighted a pocket with a stone, walked from Monk's House to the River Ouse and went in. She was fifty-nine. Her body was not found for three weeks. The letter she left for Leonard is one of the clearest things ever written by someone in the middle of losing clarity, and it is not about the war, whatever the newspapers said afterwards.

Dearest,

I feel certain I am going mad again. I feel we can't go through another of those terrible times. And I shan't recover this time. I begin to hear voices, and I can't concentrate. So I am doing what seems the best thing to do. You have given me the greatest possible happiness. You have been in every way all that anyone could be. I don't think two people could have been happier till this terrible disease came. I can't fight any longer. I know that I am spoiling your life, that without me you could work. And you will I know. You see I can't even write this properly. I can't read. What I want to say is I owe all the happiness of my life to you. You have been entirely patient with me and incredibly good. I want to say that — everybody knows it. If anybody could have saved me it would have been you. Everything has gone from me but the certainty of your goodness. I can't go on spoiling your life any longer.

I don't think two people could have been happier than we have been.

The press then did what the press does. The Sunday Times printed a letter from a bishop's wife demanding to know what right anyone had to call Woolf especially sensitive, when other people “are hiding their agony of mind, suffering bravely and carrying on unselfishly for the sake of others.” Leonard wrote in to correct the record — she had written those terrible times, meaning her own illness, not these terrible times, meaning the war — and the paper reprinted the misquotation anyway. He buried her ashes under an elm in the garden at Monk's House and spent the next twenty-eight years editing and publishing her diaries and essays, which is how we have most of what we have.

their works

All of the fiction and essay text below is quoted verbatim from the published editions, now in the public domain. Read the long ones slowly; the sentences are built to be walked through, not skimmed.

1. The opening of Mrs Dalloway (1925). Fifteen words of errand, and then the floor gives way and a fifty-two-year-old woman is eighteen again at a window in the country. This is the whole method in one paragraph.

Mrs. Dalloway said she would buy the flowers herself.

For Lucy had her work cut out for her. The doors would be taken off their hinges; Rumpelmayer's men were coming. And then, thought Clarissa Dalloway, what a morning — fresh as if issued to children on a beach. What a lark! What a plunge! For so it had always seemed to her, when, with a little squeak of the hinges, which she could hear now, she had burst open the French windows and plunged at Bourton into the open air. How fresh, how calm, stiller than this of course, the air was in the early morning; like the flap of a wave; the kiss of a wave; chill and sharp and yet (for a girl of eighteen as she then was) solemn, feeling as she did, standing there at the open window, that something awful was about to happen; looking at the flowers, at the trees with the smoke winding off them and the rooks rising, falling; standing and looking…

2. The well of tears. Clarissa stops at a bookshop window, reads two lines of Shakespeare through the glass, and the novel quietly states its thesis about the generation that came out of the first war.

What was she trying to recover? What image of white dawn in the country, as she read in the book spread open:

Fear no more the heat o' the sun
Nor the furious winter's rages.

This late age of the world's experience had bred in them all, all men and women, a well of tears. Tears and sorrows; courage and endurance; a perfectly upright and stoical bearing.

3. Septimus at the window. The shell-shocked veteran, hunted by two doctors who are certain they are helping. Note what she does with the timing: he does not want to die, he says life is good, and he goes out the window anyway because Holmes is on the stairs. Written in 1925, by someone who had been on the receiving end of that particular door opening.

There remained only the window, the large Bloomsbury-lodging house window, the tiresome, the troublesome, and rather melodramatic business of opening the window and throwing himself out. It was their idea of tragedy, not his or Rezia's (for she was with him). Holmes and Bradshaw like that sort of thing. (He sat on the sill.) But he would wait till the very last moment. He did not want to die. Life was good. The sun hot. Only human beings — what did they want? Coming down the staircase opposite an old man stopped and stared at him. Holmes was at the door. “I'll give it you!” he cried, and flung himself vigorously, violently down on to Mrs. Filmer's area railings.

“The coward!” cried Dr. Holmes, bursting the door open.

4. The last four words of Mrs Dalloway. Peter Walsh, who has loved her for thirty years and been refused, sitting at her party.

“I will come,” said Peter, but he sat on for a moment. What is this terror? what is this ecstasy? he thought to himself. What is it that fills me with extraordinary excitement?

It is Clarissa, he said.

For there she was.

5. The most famous pair of brackets in English fiction. From the “Time Passes” section of To the Lighthouse (1927). The novel's central character dies in a subordinate clause, inside square brackets, offstage, while the narrator is busy describing wind in an empty house. Every other writer would have given it a chapter. She gives it a bracket, and it is unbearable.

[Mr. Ramsay, stumbling along a passage one dark morning, stretched his arms out, but Mrs. Ramsay having died rather suddenly the night before, his arms, though stretched out, remained empty.]

6. The end of To the Lighthouse. Lily Briscoe, unmarried, told all her life by a man at dinner parties that women can't paint and women can't write, finishes the picture. Note that she knows perfectly well it will end up in an attic. She finishes it anyway.

There it was — her picture. Yes, with all its greens and blues, its lines running up and across, its attempt at something. It would be hung in the attics, she thought; it would be destroyed. But what did that matter? she asked herself, taking up her brush again. She looked at the steps; they were empty; she looked at her canvas; it was blurred. With a sudden intensity, as if she saw it clear for a second, she drew a line there, in the centre. It was done; it was finished. Yes, she thought, laying down her brush in extreme fatigue, I have had my vision.

7. “A Haunted House” (1921), nearly complete. Six hundred words. A dead couple wander through their old house looking for something they left behind, and the thing they are looking for turns out to be the sleeping people in the bed. It is the best short answer anyone has written to the question of what a house remembers.

Whatever hour you woke there was a door shutting. From room to room they went, hand in hand, lifting here, opening there, making sure — a ghostly couple.

“Here we left it,” she said. And he added, “Oh, but here too!” “It's upstairs,” she murmured. “And in the garden,” he whispered. “Quietly,” they said, “or we shall wake them.”

But it wasn't that you woke us. Oh, no. “They're looking for it; they're drawing the curtain,” one might say, and so read on a page or two. “Now they've found it,” one would be certain, stopping the pencil on the margin. And then, tired of reading, one might rise and see for oneself, the house all empty, the doors standing open, only the wood pigeons bubbling with content and the hum of the threshing machine sounding from the farm. “What did I come in here for? What did I want to find?” My hands were empty. “Perhaps it's upstairs then?” The apples were in the loft. And so down again, the garden still as ever, only the book had slipped into the grass.

But they had found it in the drawing room. Not that one could ever see them. The window panes reflected apples, reflected roses; all the leaves were green in the glass. If they moved in the drawing room, the apple only turned its yellow side. Yet, the moment after, if the door was opened, spread about the floor, hung upon the walls, pendant from the ceiling — what? My hands were empty. The shadow of a thrush crossed the carpet; from the deepest wells of silence the wood pigeon drew its bubble of sound. “Safe, safe, safe,” the pulse of the house beat softly. “The treasure buried; the room …” the pulse stopped short. Oh, was that the buried treasure?

A moment later the light had faded. Out in the garden then? But the trees spun darkness for a wandering beam of sun. So fine, so rare, coolly sunk beneath the surface the beam I sought always burnt behind the glass. Death was the glass; death was between us; coming to the woman first, hundreds of years ago, leaving the house, sealing all the windows; the rooms were darkened. He left it, left her, went North, went East, saw the stars turned in the Southern sky; sought the house, found it dropped beneath the Downs. “Safe, safe, safe,” the pulse of the house beat gladly. “The Treasure yours.”

The wind roars up the avenue. Trees stoop and bend this way and that. Moonbeams splash and spill wildly in the rain. But the beam of the lamp falls straight from the window. The candle burns stiff and still. Wandering through the house, opening the windows, whispering not to wake us, the ghostly couple seek their joy.

“Here we slept,” she says. And he adds, “Kisses without number.” “Waking in the morning —” “Silver between the trees —” “Upstairs —” “In the garden —” “When summer came —” “In winter snowtime —” The doors go shutting far in the distance, gently knocking…

… the pulse of the house beats wildly. Waking, I cry “Oh, is this your buried treasure? The light in the heart.”

8. A Room of One's Own (1929) — the refusal to be inspiring. She was asked to speak on women and fiction. This is how she opens: by telling a room full of young women that she has no nugget of truth for them.

All I could do was to offer you an opinion upon one minor point — a woman must have money and a room of her own if she is to write fiction; and that, as you will see, leaves the great problem of the true nature of woman and the true nature of fiction unsolved. I have shirked the duty of coming to a conclusion upon these two questions — women and fiction remain, so far as I am concerned, unsolved problems.

9. The looking-glass. Still the sharpest single paragraph anyone has written about why some men need you smaller. She wrote it in 1929, with Mussolini in power and Hitler four years away, and she saw the connection immediately.

Women have served all these centuries as looking-glasses possessing the magic and delicious power of reflecting the figure of man at twice its natural size. Without that power probably the earth would still be swamp and jungle. The glories of all our wars would be unknown. We should still be scratching the outlines of deer on the remains of mutton bones and bartering flints for sheep skins or whatever simple ornament took our unsophisticated taste. Supermen and Fingers of Destiny would never have existed. The Czar and the Kaiser would never have worn crowns or lost them. Whatever may be their use in civilized societies, mirrors are essential to all violent and heroic action. That is why Napoleon and Mussolini both insist so emphatically upon the inferiority of women, for if they were not inferior, they would cease to enlarge.

10. Judith Shakespeare. The most famous thought experiment in feminist criticism: suppose Shakespeare had a sister just as gifted. Woolf follows the logic all the way to the end and does not flinch at where it lands. The last line of it is one of the great sentences in the language.

Meanwhile his extraordinarily gifted sister, let us suppose, remained at home. She was as adventurous, as imaginative, as agog to see the world as he was. But she was not sent to school. She had no chance of learning grammar and logic, let alone of reading Horace and Virgil. She picked up a book now and then, one of her brother's perhaps, and read a few pages. But then her parents came in and told her to mend the stockings or mind the stew and not moon about with books and papers. They would have spoken sharply but kindly, for they were substantial people who knew the conditions of life for a woman and loved their daughter — indeed, more likely than not she was the apple of her father's eye. Perhaps she scribbled some pages up in an apple loft on the sly but was careful to hide them or set fire to them.

Soon, however, before she was out of her teens, she was to be betrothed to the son of a neighbouring wool-stapler. She cried out that marriage was hateful to her, and for that she was severely beaten by her father. Then he ceased to scold her. He begged her instead not to hurt him, not to shame him in this matter of her marriage. He would give her a chain of beads or a fine petticoat, he said; and there were tears in his eyes. How could she disobey him? How could she break his heart? The force of her own gift alone drove her to it. She made up a small parcel of her belongings, let herself down by a rope one summer's night and took the road to London. She was not seventeen. The birds that sang in the hedge were not more musical than she was. She had the quickest fancy, a gift like her brother's, for the tune of words. Like him, she had a taste for the theatre. She stood at the stage door; she wanted to act, she said. Men laughed in her face. The manager — a fat, looselipped man — guffawed. He bellowed something about poodles dancing and women acting — no woman, he said, could possibly be an actress. He hinted — you can imagine what. She could get no training in her craft. Could she even seek her dinner in a tavern or roam the streets at midnight? Yet her genius was for fiction and lusted to feed abundantly upon the lives of men and women and the study of their ways. At last — for she was very young, oddly like Shakespeare the poet in her face, with the same grey eyes and rounded brows — at last Nick Greene the actor-manager took pity on her; she found herself with child by that gentleman and so — who shall measure the heat and violence of the poet's heart when caught and tangled in a woman's body? — killed herself one winter's night and lies buried at some cross-roads where the omnibuses now stop outside the Elephant and Castle.

11. The last page of A Room of One's Own. Having killed Judith off, she brings her back, and makes the reader responsible for her. This is the passage people quote badly. Read what she actually asks for: not confidence, not permission. Money, freedom, the habit of telling the truth, and the acceptance that there is no arm to cling to.

Now my belief is that this poet who never wrote a word and was buried at the cross-roads still lives. She lives in you and in me, and in many other women who are not here to-night, for they are washing up the dishes and putting the children to bed. But she lives; for great poets do not die; they are continuing presences; they need only the opportunity to walk among us in the flesh. This opportunity, as I think, it is now coming within your power to give her. For my belief is that if we live another century or so — I am talking of the common life which is the real life and not of the little separate lives which we live as individuals — and have five hundred a year each of us and rooms of our own; if we have the habit of freedom and the courage to write exactly what we think; if we escape a little from the common sitting-room and see human beings not always in their relation to each other but in relation to reality; and the sky, too, and the trees or whatever it may be in themselves; if we look past Milton's bogey, for no human being should shut out the view; if we face the fact, for it is a fact, that there is no arm to cling to, but that we go alone and that our relation is to the world of reality and not only to the world of men and women, then the opportunity will come and the dead poet who was Shakespeare's sister will put on the body which she has so often laid down.

12. On being censored. One line, from the same book, aimed at a beadle who had just turned her off the grass at an Oxbridge college for being a woman.

Lock up your libraries if you like; but there is no gate, no lock, no bolt, that you can set upon the freedom of my mind.

13. Orlando (1928) — the change. Her hero goes to sleep as a man in Constantinople and wakes up as a woman, and the narrator refuses to make a fuss about it, which in 1928 was the most radical possible thing to do.

Orlando had become a woman — there is no denying it. But in every other respect, Orlando remained precisely as he had been. The change of sex, though it altered their future, did nothing whatever to alter their identity. Their faces remained, as their portraits prove, practically the same.

14. Orlando — on what a woman is allowed to want. Pure acid, delivered in the voice of a fussy Victorian biographer who cannot understand why his subject won't behave.

Love, the poet has said, is woman's whole existence. And if we look for a moment at Orlando writing at her table, we must admit that never was there a woman more fitted for that calling. Surely, since she is a woman, and a beautiful woman, and a woman in the prime of life, she will soon give over this pretence of writing and thinking and begin at least to think of a gamekeeper (and as long as she thinks of a man, nobody objects to a woman thinking). And then she will write him a little note (and as long as she writes little notes nobody objects to a woman writing either) and make an assignation for Sunday dusk … all of which is, of course, the very stuff of life and the only possible subject for fiction. Surely Orlando must have done one of these things? Alas, — a thousand times, alas, Orlando did none of them.

15. Killing the Angel in the House. From a talk she gave to the London/National Society for Women's Service in January 1931, later printed as “Professions for Women.” The Angel is the perfect self-erasing Victorian woman, taken from a popular poem of the period. Woolf describes murdering her, at length, and admits she is not sure the job stayed done.

I will describe her as shortly as I can. She was intensely sympathetic. She was immensely charming. She was utterly unselfish. She excelled in the difficult arts of family life. She sacrificed herself daily. If there was chicken, she took the leg; if there was a draught she sat in it — in short she was so constituted that she never had a mind or a wish of her own, but preferred to sympathize always with the minds and wishes of others…

I turned upon her and caught her by the throat. I did my best to kill her. My excuse, if I were to be had up in a court of law, would be that I acted in self-defence. Had I not killed her she would have killed me. She would have plucked the heart out of my writing…

Thus, whenever I felt the shadow of her wing or the radiance of her halo upon my page, I took up the inkpot and flung it at her. She died hard. Her fictitious nature was of great assistance to her. It is far harder to kill a phantom than a reality. She was always creeping back when I thought I had despatched her. Though I flatter myself that I killed her in the end, the struggle was severe…

The Angel was dead; what then remained? You may say that what remained was a simple and common object — a young woman in a bedroom with an inkpot. In other words, now that she had rid herself of falsehood, that young woman had only to be herself.

Sources: Mrs. Dalloway (Harcourt, Brace, 1925), Project Gutenberg #71865; To the Lighthouse (Hogarth Press, 1927), Project Gutenberg Australia; Orlando: A Biography (1928), Project Gutenberg Australia; A Room of One's Own (1929), Project Gutenberg Australia; “A Haunted House” from Monday or Tuesday (Hogarth Press, 1921), Project Gutenberg #29220; “Professions for Women” (1931 address); the 1926 Woolf–Sackville-West letters as collected in The Letters of Vita Sackville-West to Virginia Woolf; the final letter to Leonard Woolf, 28 March 1941, and the press aftermath from Sybil Oldfield, Afterwords: Letters on the Death of Virginia Woolf. Biographical detail follows Hermione Lee and Quentin Bell.

Jalál al-Dín Rúmí

1207–1273 · Persian jurist, professor, Sufi · a respectable middle-aged law lecturer met a wandering dervish, lost his entire life to him, and came out the other side as the most-read poet on earth

aug 11, 2026▼

childhood & early life

He was born on 30 September 1207, in or near Balkh in what is now northern Afghanistan — the old sources say Balkh, modern scholars mostly say the small town of Vakhsh where his father actually worked. His father, Bahá al-Dín Walad, was a preacher and jurist grand enough to be given the title Sultan of the Scholars, and stubborn enough to lose his position over it. His mother was Mo'mena Khátún. He had an older brother, Alá al-Dín, who died young.

The family left Balkh while he was still a boy and never went back. The reason given in the pious biographies is a quarrel with the king; the reason visible on any map is that Genghis Khan was coming, and within a few years Balkh would be rubble and its population dead. So his childhood was a caravan. Nishapur, Baghdad, Mecca, Damascus, a stretch in Anatolia, and finally Konya, the capital of the Seljuks of Rúm — which is why a Persian-speaking Central Asian ended up permanently nicknamed the Roman. The great theme of everything he ever wrote is separation from home and the ache to get back. He was a refugee before he was a poet. It is not a coincidence.

There is a lovely story from Nishapur, and it may be a legend, but the tradition keeps it because it is too good to lose: the aged mystical poet Faríd al-Dín 'Attár met the family, handed the small boy a copy of his Book of Mysteries, and told the father that this child would one day set the world's lovers on fire. Konya later collected a whole shelf of these child-prodigy stories — visions at six, teaching his playmates metaphysics, flying. Treat them the way you'd treat any saint's childhood: as the community explaining backwards to itself how it got so lucky.

His father died in 1231 and Jalál, in his mid-twenties, inherited the professorial chair. Then his father's old student Burhán al-Dín Muhaqqiq of Tirmidh turned up in Konya and put him through nine more years of training, including Aleppo and Damascus. By his late thirties he was exactly what everyone wanted him to be: a jurist with hundreds of students, a mufti issuing legal opinions, a man with an excellent reputation and, by every account including his own, absolutely nothing on fire inside him.

what he did & why he's remembered

On 15 November 1244, a rough travelling dervish in black called Shams of Tabriz arrived in Konya and asked the celebrated professor a question in the street. The versions of the question differ. In one, Shams asks who was greater, the Prophet Muhammad or the mystic Bayázíd, and the answer he gets makes him faint. In another, he shoves Rumi's books into a pool of water, and when Rumi cries out about the manuscripts, Shams pulls them out dry and says, this is something you do not understand. Take your pick. What every account agrees on is the result: the two men shut themselves in a room for weeks and Rumi's public life stopped dead.

He stopped lecturing. He stopped issuing legal opinions. He started spinning — turning on one foot with one arm raised, for hours, to music, which for a senior jurist in a conservative city was roughly the equivalent of a supreme court judge quitting to busk. And he started speaking poetry, thousands upon thousands of lines of it, dictated in a trance while circling a pillar in his house while a scribe scrambled to keep up.

Two enormous books came out of it. The Díván-i Shams-i Tabrízí is around forty thousand lines of ecstatic lyric — and note the title, because he signed his own love poems with the name of his friend instead of his own. Poets end ghazals with their pen name; Rumi ended his with Shams, which is both a man's name and the Persian word for the sun. He wrote himself out of his own book. The other is the Masnaví, twenty-five thousand couplets in six volumes, dictated over the last twelve years of his life — a vast, digressive, funny, filthy-in-places poem full of parrots and lions and grocers, which Persian tradition nicknames the Qur'an in Persian and which is essentially a manual for how to be destroyed by love on purpose.

He founded no order himself; his son and his successor Husám al-Dín built the Mevlevis, the whirling dervishes, around his grave. And he is now, improbably, the best-selling poet in America, mostly in loose modern versions that quietly delete the Islam, the sexual jokes, the theology and the Qur'anic citations, and leave a sort of warm wallpaper. Everything quoted below is old, literal, and out of copyright, so you get the actual man: harder, stranger, more argumentative and much more heartbroken than the fridge magnets.

family life

He married Gawhar Khátún — her name means Pearl — in 1225 at Larende, in a marriage arranged by his father. She bore him two sons, Sultán Walad and Alá al-Dín Chalabí, and died young. He married again, to Kerrá Khátún, who outlived him; with her he had a son, Amír Álim, and a daughter, Malakeh Khátún.

The household is where the story goes dark. His two sons split cleanly over Shams. Sultán Walad adored his father, personally walked to Damascus to fetch Shams back when he ran off, and later wrote the verse chronicle that is our best primary source for all of it. Alá al-Dín went the other way. He was among the students who hated the intruder, and the persistent tradition — not provable, never denied by the family either — is that he was involved in the group that murdered Shams. Rumi appears to have refused to speak to him afterwards, and did not attend his funeral. Read that again: he chose the stranger over his own son.

There is a further, contested figure: Kímíá Khátún, a young woman raised in Rumi's household, whom Shams married. She died suddenly, within months. Some sources put her death immediately before Shams's final disappearance, which turns the whole crisis into something much uglier and more domestic than a spiritual disagreement. The evidence is thin and the fiction written about her is thick, so hold it loosely.

One more family detail, from the collector al-Aflákí, that is worth more than any of the miracle stories: passing a group of children at play, Rumi bowed to each of them as they ran up to greet him. One small boy, too far off to reach him, shouted from down the road, wait for me until I come! And the most famous man in Konya stood in the street and waited, and bowed to him too.

romantic life

Two wives, and one relationship that broke his life in half, and it was not with either of them.

What Shams and Rumi were to each other has been argued about for eight centuries and cannot be settled. The Sufi frame is that Shams was the mirror, the beloved as a doorway to the Beloved. Modern readers, looking at forty thousand lines of the most erotically charged devotional verse in Persian addressed to a man by name, tend to raise an eyebrow. The 1907 English editor of these translations calls it, primly, this strange union, and admits it is extremely difficult to rightly fathom. What is not in doubt is the size of it. A settled, respected, married man in his late thirties met someone and became unrecognisable to everyone who knew him, and never went back.

The students revolted. They called it an unworthy infatuation on their master's part and a shameful seduction on the dervish's. Shams felt the pressure and fled to Damascus in March 1246. Rumi sent Sultán Walad after him with money and letters, and Shams came back, and for a while it held. Then on a night in December, two years later, someone called Shams to the back door of the house. He got up, went out, and was never seen again. There was a scuffle in the yard, a cry, and nothing. No body was ever produced. Some say he simply left again and died years later in Khoy, where there is a tomb with his name on it.

Rumi refused to believe it. He went to Syria looking for him. Twice. He walked around Damascus asking after a man in a black cloak. And then, at some point in that search, something turned over in him, and he came home and said, in effect: I stopped looking because I found him — I am he. I gazed into my own heart; there I saw Him; He was nowhere else. That is the hinge of his entire body of work: a man who could not get his person back and so metabolised the loss into a theology in which nothing that was ever really loved can actually be gone.

Afterwards there were two more companions, and it is telling that he needed them. Saláh al-Dín Zarkúb was an illiterate goldsmith; the story is that Rumi was walking through the bazaar, heard the rhythm of the hammer on gold leaf coming out of his shop, and started turning in the middle of the street. They were inseparable for around ten years until Saláh al-Dín's death, and Rumi married his son to the goldsmith's daughter. After him came Husám al-Dín Chalabí, the scribe, who complained that the students were reading other people's mystical poetry and asked why their own master didn't write them a book. Rumi is said to have pulled a scrap of paper out of his turban with the first eighteen lines of the Masnaví already on it. When Husám's wife died, the dictation stopped for two years.

life until death

He aged into something very unlike a wild man. The last twenty-five years are mostly domestic and productive: teaching, dictating, letters, arbitrating quarrels, refusing invitations from the powerful, going up and down the same streets in Konya. The ecstasy did not go away; it just found a work schedule.
