Whole nights went to the Masnaví, Rumi reciting and Husám writing, sometimes singing the couplets back to him in a good voice while the ink dried.

In December 1273 he fell ill with a fever that the doctors could not name. He was calm about it in a way that annoyed some of the people around him. When friends came in worried, the tradition says he told them not to fuss: the earth is hungry, and it is about to get a fat morsel. He is said to have composed a ghazal on his deathbed that opens by refusing to be pitied — how dost thou know what sort of king I have within me as companion? Do not cast thy glance upon my golden face, for I have iron legs.

He died on 17 December 1273, at sunset. His followers do not call it his death day. They call it Shab-i Arús, the wedding night, because on his own account death was the moment the separation ended, and it has been kept as a festival in Konya every December since. He had said it plainly years earlier, in a poem about his own funeral: when thou seest my hearse, cry not “Parted, parted!” Union and meeting are mine in that hour.

The funeral was chaos and it was the best argument for him. Muslims, Christians, Jews and Greeks jammed the streets and the procession took most of the day because the crowd kept stopping it to touch the coffin. When someone asked a weeping Christian what business he had at a Muslim's grave, he answered that they esteemed the man as the Moses, the David and the Jesus of their time, and were all his disciples. He was buried beside his father under a fluted turquoise dome that still stands in Konya. The epitaph traditionally attached to him fits the man who wrote the reed-flute prologue: when we are dead, seek not our tomb in the earth, but find it in the hearts of men.

their works

All translations below are Victorian and out of copyright, which is the point: they are literal rather than improved. The Masnaví passages are E. H. Whinfield's 1898 rendering; the lyrics from the Díván-i Shams-i Tabrízí are R. A. Nicholson's, with three by William Hastie, as collected by F. Hadland Davis in 1907. Nothing here has been smoothed out for you.

1. The Song of the Reed. The opening of the Masnaví, and the most famous thirty lines in Persian literature. A reed has been cut out of the reed-bed to be made into a flute. Every note it plays is the sound of the hole where its home used to be. Remember that the man dictating this had lost a city, a country, and a person.

Hearken to the reed-flute, how it complains,
Lamenting its banishment from its home:
“Ever since they tore me from my osier bed,
My plaintive notes have moved men and women to tears.
I burst my breast, striving to give vent to sighs,
And to express the pangs of my yearning for my home.
He who abides far away from his home
Is ever longing for the day he shall return.
My wailing is heard in every throng,
In concert with them that rejoice and them that weep.
Each interprets my notes in harmony with his own feelings,
But not one fathoms the secrets of my heart.
My secrets are not alien from my plaintive notes,
Yet they are not manifest to the sensual eye and ear.
Body is not veiled from soul, neither soul from body,
Yet no man hath ever seen a soul.”
This plaint of the flute is fire, not mere air.
Let him who lacks this fire be accounted dead!
'Tis the fire of love that inspires the flute,
'Tis the ferment of love that possesses the wine.
The flute is the confidant of all unhappy lovers;
Yea, its strains lay bare my inmost secrets.
Who hath seen a poison and an antidote like the flute?
Who hath seen a sympathetic consoler like the flute?
The flute tells the tale of love's bloodstained path,
It recounts the story of Majnun's love toils.
None is privy to these feelings save one distracted,
As ear inclines to the whispers of the tongue.
Through grief my days are as labour and sorrow,
My days move on, hand in hand with anguish.
Yet, though my days vanish thus, 'tis no matter,
Do thou abide, O Incomparable Pure One!
But all who are not fishes are soon tired of water;
And they who lack daily bread find the day very long;
So the “Raw” comprehend not the state of the “Ripe;”
Therefore it behoves me to shorten my discourse.
Arise, O son! burst thy bonds and be free!
How long wilt thou be captive to silver and gold?
Though thou pour the ocean into thy pitcher,
It can hold no more than one day's store.
The pitcher of the desire of the covetous never fills,
The oyster-shell fills not with pearls till it is content;
Only he whose garment is rent by the violence of love
Is wholly pure from covetousness and sin.
Hail to thee, then, O LOVE, sweet madness!
Thou who healest all our infirmities!
Who art the physician of our pride and self-conceit!
Who art our Plato and our Galen!
Love exalts our earthly bodies to heaven,
And makes the very hills to dance with joy!
O lover, 'twas love that gave life to Mount Sinai,
When “it quaked, and Moses fell down in a swoon.”
Did my Beloved only touch me with his lips,
I too, like the flute, would burst out in melody.
But he who is parted from them that speak his tongue,
Though he possess a hundred voices, is perforce dumb.
When the rose has faded and the garden is withered,
The song of the nightingale is no longer to be heard.
The BELOVED is all in all, the lover only veils Him;
The BELOVED is all that lives, the lover a dead thing.
When the lover feels no longer LOVE's quickening,
He becomes like a bird who has lost its wings. Alas!
How can I retain my senses about me,
When the BELOVED shows not the light of His countenance?
LOVE desires that this secret should be revealed,
For if a mirror reflects not, of what use is it?
Knowest thou why thy mirror reflects not?
Because the rust has not been scoured from its face.
If it were purified from all rust and defilement,
It would reflect the shining of the SUN of GOD.
O friends, ye have now heard this tale,
Which sets forth the very essence of my case.

2. Description of Love. Also Masnaví Book I. A theologian trained to define things for a living, admitting on the page that this one breaks the instrument.

A true lover is proved such by his pain of heart;
No sickness is there like sickness of heart.
The lover's ailment is different from all ailments;
Love is the astrolabe of God's mysteries.
A lover may hanker after this love or that love,
But at the last he is drawn to the KING of love.
However much we describe and explain love,
When we fall in love we are ashamed of our words.
Explanation by the tongue makes most things clear,
But love unexplained is clearer.
When pen hasted to write,
On reaching the subject of love it split in twain.
When the discourse touched on the matter of love,
Pen was broken and paper torn.
In explaining it Reason sticks fast, as an ass in mire;
Naught but Love itself can explain love and lovers!

3. Shams interrupts the poem. A few pages into his masterpiece, Rumi breaks off to argue with his dead friend, who is in the room demanding he stop hiding. The exchange is one of the strangest things in the book — a poet resisting his own subject in real time, and losing.

The sun (Shams) of Tabriz is a perfect light,
A sun, yea, one of the beams of God!
When the praise was heard of the “Sun of Tabriz,”
The sun of the fourth heaven bowed its head. …
That precious Soul caught my skirt,
Smelling the perfume of the garment of Yusuf;
And said, “For the sake of our ancient friendship,
Tell forth a hint of those sweet states of ecstasy.” …
I said, “O thou who art far from 'The Friend,'
Like a sick man who has strayed from his physician,
Importune me not, for I am beside myself;
My understanding is gone, I cannot sing praises. …
Can I describe my separation and my bleeding heart?
Nay, put off this matter till another season.”
He said, “Feed me, for I am an hungered,
And at once, for 'the time is a sharp sword.'” …
He said, “Without veil or covering or deception,
Speak out, and vex me not, O man of many words!
Strip off the veil and speak out, for do not I
Enter under the same coverlet as the Beloved?”
I said, “If the Beloved were exposed to outward view,
Neither wouldst thou endure, nor embrace, nor form. …
If the sun that illumines the world
Were to draw nigher, the world would be consumed.”
… He said, “Thy words are endless. Now tell forth
All thy story from its beginning.”

4. Complaint against the Beloved. Here is the Rumi the paperback anthologies leave out. He calls God That Fickle Fair One and files a formal grievance about being toyed with, then immediately withdraws it, then doesn't.

Let me then, I say, make complaint
Of the severity of That Fickle Fair One.
I cry, and my cries sound sweet in His ear;
He requires from the two worlds cries and groans.
How shall I not wail under His chastening hand? …
His bitters are very sweets to my soul,
My sad heart is a lively sacrifice to my Beloved.
I am enamoured of my own grief and pain,
For it makes me well-pleasing to my peerless King.
I use the dust of my grief as salve for my eyes,
That my eyes, like seas, may teem with pearls.
The tears which are shed because of His chastening
Are very pearls, though men deem them mere tears.
'Tis “The Soul of souls” of whom I am making complaint;
Yet I do not complain; I merely state my case.
My heart says, “He has injured me,”
But I laugh at these pretended injuries. …
I gave Him leave to shed my blood, if He willed it;
I only said, “Is it right?” and He forsook me.
Why dost Thou flee from the cries of us on earth?
Why pourest Thou sorrow on the heart of the sorrowful? …
But, for God's sake, leave off telling of the Rose;
Tell of the Bulbul who is severed from his Rose.

5. The old harper. A singer's voice goes; nobody hires him; he takes his harp to the graveyard, plays for God alone, and falls asleep. The Caliph is sent to find him. When the old man starts sobbing about his wasted years, the answer he gets is the most useful thing in the whole poem, and it is not comfort.

“Sobriety savours of memory of the past;
Past and future are what veil God from our sight.
Burn up both of them with fire! How long
Wilt thou be partitioned by these segments as a reed?
So long as a reed has partitions 'tis not privy to secrets,
Nor is it vocal in response to lip and breathing.
While circumambulating the house thou art a stranger;
When thou enterest in thou art at home. …
Thy wailing contrition is worse than thy sin. …
O thou who seekest to be contrite for the past,
How wilt thou be contrite for this contrition?”
… Like a soul he was freed from weeping and rejoicing,
His old life died, and he was regenerated.

6. On women, from the tale of the Arab and his wife. Written in the thirteenth century, in the middle of a passage that is otherwise cheerfully unreconstructed about nagging wives, which makes the turn at the end more startling, not less.

The Prophet said that women hold dominion
Over sages and over men of heart,
But that fools, again, hold the upper hand over women,
Because fools are violent and exceedingly froward.
They have no tenderness or gentleness or amity,
Because the animal nature sways their temperament.
Love and tenderness are qualities of humanity,
Passion and lust are qualities of animality.
Woman is a ray of God, not a mere mistress,
The Creator's self, as it were, not a mere creature!

7. “Thou and I.” From the Díván. Two people in a garden who are also in two countries at once, which is what it feels like to be with someone and to have lost them at the same time.

Happy the moment when we are seated in the Palace, thou and I,
With two forms and with two figures but with one soul, thou and I.
The colours of the grove and the voice of the birds will bestow immortality
At the time when we come into the garden, thou and I.
The stars of heaven will come to gaze upon us;
We shall show them the moon itself, thou and I.
Thou and I, individuals no more, shall be mingled in ecstasy,
Joyful, and secure from foolish babble, thou and I.
All the bright-plumed birds of heaven will devour their hearts with envy
In the place where we shall laugh in such a fashion, thou and I.
This is the greatest wonder, that thou and I, sitting here in the same nook,
Are at this moment both in Iráq and Khorasan, thou and I.

8. The Finding of the Beloved. The search poem. He goes everywhere, methodically, and comes back with the only answer available to a man whose friend has vanished.

Cross and Christians, from end to end,
I surveyed; He was not on the Cross.
I went to the idol-temple, to the ancient pagoda;
No trace was visible there.
I went to the mountains of Herát and Candahár;
I looked; He was not in that hill-and-dale.
… I gazed into my own heart;
There I saw Him; He was nowhere else.

9. A Cry to the Beloved. He sends messages to a star, then to the sun, then shows his wounds as evidence, then rocks his own heart like a baby to get it to stop crying. It is the most childlike thing he wrote and the most exact.

Yestereve I delivered to a star tidings for thee:
“Present,” I said, “my service to that moon-like form.”
I bowed, I said: “Bear that service to the sun
Who maketh hard rocks gold by his burning.”
I bared my breast, I showed it the wounds:
“Give news of me,” I said, “to the Beloved whose drink is blood.”
I rocked to and fro that the child, my heart, might become still;
A child sleeps when one sways the cradle.
Give my heart-babe milk, relieve us from its weeping,
O Thou that helpest every moment a hundred helpless like me.
The heart's home, first to last, is Thy City of Union:
How long wilt Thou keep in exile this heart forlorn?

10. This is Love. Eight lines. No decoration.

This is Love: to fly heavenward,
To rend, every instant, a hundred veils.
The first moment, to renounce Life:
The last step, to feel without feet.
To regard this world as invisible,
Not to see what appears to one's self.
“O heart,” I said, “may it bless thee
To have entered the circle of lovers,
To look beyond the range of the eye,
To penetrate the windings of the bosom!
Whence did this breath come to thee, O my soul,
Whence this throbbing, O my heart?”

11. The Water of Eternal Life. The consolation poem, and the one to keep. He is not saying the thing you lost didn't matter. He is saying the spring it came out of is still running.

Every form you see has its archetype in the placeless world;
If the form perished, no matter, since its Original is everlasting.
Every fair shape you have seen, every deep saying you have heard,
Be not cast down that it perished; for that is not so.
Whereas the Spring-head is undying, its branch gives water continually;
Since neither can cease, why are you lamenting?
Conceive the Soul as a fountain, and these created things as rivers:
While the Fountain flows, the rivers run from it.
Put grief out of your head and keep quaffing this River-water;
Do not think of the Water failing, for this Water is without end.

12. Life in Death. His own funeral, written in advance, with stage directions for the mourners.

When my bier moveth on the day of Death,
Think not my heart is in this world.
Do not weep in the devil's snare: that is woe.
When thou seest my hearse, cry not “Parted, parted!”
Union and meeting are mine in that hour.
If thou commit me to the grave, say not “Farewell, farewell!”
For the grave is a curtain hiding the communion of Paradise,
After beholding descent, consider resurrection;
Why should setting be injurious to the sun and moon?
To thee it seems a setting, but 'tis a rising;
Tho' the vault seems a prison, 'tis the release of the soul.
… Shut thy mouth on this side and open it beyond,
For in placeless air will be thy triumphal song.

13. Thou didst go to the Rose-Garden. An elegy for someone who has died — a falcon kept caged by an old woman, hearing the drum that calls it back.

At last thou hast departed and gone to the Unseen;
'Tis marvellous by what way thou wentest from the world.
Thou didst strongly shake thy wings and feathers, and having broken thy cage
Didst take to the air and journey towards the world of Soul.
Thou wert a favourite falcon, kept in captivity by an old woman:
When thou heard'st the falcon-drum thou didst fly away into the Void.
Thou wert a love-lorn nightingale among owls:
The scent of the Rose-Garden reached thee, and thou didst go to the Rose-Garden.

14. The Journey to the Beloved. A caravan striking camp at night, which is a thing he had actually watched as a child on the road out of Balkh.

O lovers, O lovers, it is time to abandon the world:
The drum of departure reaches my spiritual ear from heaven.
Behold, the driver has risen and made ready his files of camels,
And begged us to acquit him of blame: why, O travellers, are you asleep?
These sounds before and behind are the din of departure and of the camel-bells;
With each moment a soul and spirit is setting off into the Void.
From these inverted candles, from these blue awnings
There has come forth a wondrous people, that the mysteries may be revealed.
A heavy slumber fell upon thee from the circling spheres:
Alas, for this life so light, beware of this slumber so heavy!
O soul, seek the Beloved, O friend, seek the Friend,
O watchman, be wakeful: it behoves not a watchman to sleep.

15. The Moon-Soul and the Sea. What the experience itself was like, described by someone who plainly did not enjoy having to use words for it.

At morning-tide a moon appeared in the sky,
And descended from the sky and gazed on me.
Like a falcon which snatches a bird at the time of hunting,
That moon snatched me up and coursed over the sky.
When I looked at myself, I saw myself no more,
Because in that moon my body became by grace even as soul.
When I travelled in soul, I saw naught save the moon,
Till the secret of the Eternal Theophany was revealed.
The nine spheres of heaven were all merged in that moon,
The vessel of my being was completely hidden in the sea.
The sea broke into waves, and again Wisdom rose
And cast abroad a voice; so it happened and thus it befell.
Foamed the sea, and at every foam-fleck
Something took figure and something was bodied forth.
Every foam-fleck of body, which received a sign from that sea,
Melted straightway and turned to spirit in this Ocean.

16. The Divine Friend. Two stanzas that are, depending on the day, either the most comforting or the most unnerving thing in the book.

Look on me, for thou art my companion in the grave
On the night when thou shalt pass from shop and dwelling.
Thou shalt hear my hail in the hollow of the tomb: it shall become known to thee
That thou wast never concealed from mine eye.
I am as reason and intellect within thy bosom
At the time of joy and gladness, at the time of sorrow and distress.

17. Earthly love and the Love Divine. The hardest thing he ever said about attachment, and he said it as an instruction, not a comfort.

'Twere better that the spirit which wears not true Love as a garment
Had not been: its being is but shame. …
Without the dealing of Love there is no entrance to the Beloved. …
'Tis Love and the Lover that live to all Eternity;
Set not thy heart on aught else; 'tis only borrowed.
How long wilt thou embrace a dead beloved?
Embrace the Soul which is embraced by nothing.
What was born of spring dies in autumn,
Love's rose-plot hath no aiding from the early spring.

18. Two short ones, to finish. He used Khámúsh — Silent — as one of his pen names, which for a man with forty thousand lines of verse is either a joke or the whole doctrine.

I am silent. Speak Thou, O Soul of Soul of Soul,
From desire of whose Face every atom grew articulate.
Be silent that the Lord who gave thee language may speak,
For as He fashioned a door and lock, He has also made a key.

Sources: E. H. Whinfield, Masnavi i Ma'navi: The Spiritual Couplets of Maulána Jalálu-'d-Dín Muhammad Rúmí (Trübner's Oriental Series, 1898); R. A. Nicholson, Selected Poems from the Díváni Shamsi Tabríz (Cambridge, 1898); William Hastie, The Festival of Spring (1903); all as gathered in F. Hadland Davis, The Persian Mystics: Jalálu'd-Dín Rúmí (John Murray, 1907). Biography from Davis, from Sultán Walad's own verse chronicle as reported by later scholarship, and from al-Aflákí's Manáqib al-Árifín, which should be read as devotion rather than as record.

Brynhildr

Norse legend · valkyrie, daughter of Buðli, ringed in fire · she was demoted by Odin for giving victory to the wrong king, and she took an entire dynasty down rather than be quietly married off

aug 10, 2026▼

childhood & early life

First, the honest problem with her. Brynhildr is two women welded together by centuries of retelling, and the seam still shows. In the oldest layer she is a valkyrie asleep in a ring of fire on a mountain called Hindarfjoll, no father, no politics, nothing but a suit of mail grown into her skin and a body of rune-lore she hands to the man who wakes her. In the later layer she is Brynhildr Buðladóttir, a king's daughter with a marriageable body, a brother named Atli who is Attila the Hun with the serial numbers filed off, and a foster-father called Heimir in a place called Hlymdalir. The compilers of the Poetic Edda did not reconcile these. They just put them next to each other and let the reader do the work, which is why she is the most psychologically loaded character in the whole northern canon: she is a supernatural being who has been forced into a domestic arrangement and never stops noticing the difference.

Her own account of her childhood exists, and it is short and furious and delivered from a wagon on the road to hell. She says she was called Hild the helmed in Hlymdalir by everybody who knew her. She says a king took the swan-robes of eight sisters from under an oak — the old motif in which a mortal steals a valkyrie's feather-coat and thereby owns her — and that she was twelve winters old when she gave her oaths to a young king. Twelve. That is the age at which the story has already begun happening to her.

Her job, before any of the romance, was administrative and enormous: she was a chooser of the slain. Odin decided who won; valkyries carried it out and collected the dead for Valhalla. She had a battle in front of her with two kings in it, Hjálmgunnar, old and mighty, whom Odin had promised the victory, and Agnar, who was younger and had asked her for help. She looked at the promise and she looked at the man and she killed Hjálmgunnar. She gave the win to the wrong side on purpose, knowing exactly whose order she was overriding.

Odin's punishment is worth reading closely because it is a punishment designed by someone who understands her. He does not kill her. He pricks her with a sleep-thorn and rules that she will never win victory in battle again, and that she will be married. That is the sentence: you will be a wife. For a woman whose entire identity was deciding the fates of men, being handed over to one is the specific, tailored humiliation. And her response, immediately, still inside the sentence, is to insert a clause of her own. She vowed she would only ever marry a man who did not know how to be afraid. She could not refuse marriage. So she raised the entry requirement until it was almost certainly impossible, and put a wall of fire around herself to enforce it. Everything that follows is what happens when someone actually clears the bar and then it turns out he was a fraud.

what she did & why she's remembered

She is the reason the Nibelung cycle exists. Every corpse in the back half of that legend — Sigurðr stabbed in his bed, Gunnar dropped into a snake pit, Atli murdered by his own wife, a hall burned with everyone inside it, a girl trampled to death by horses two generations later — traces back through a single unbroken causal chain to one woman finding out she had been deceived about who rode through her fire. Guthrun's revenge, the fall of the Gjukungs, the whole Germanic apocalypse of oath-breaking: she starts it, and she predicts all of it out loud while dying, correctly, in order.

She is also, in the Sigrdrífumál, the great teacher of the cycle. Sigurðr cuts her mail off, she sits up, and the first thing she does is not weep or thank him — she greets the daylight, the gods and the earth, hands him a horn of mead, and starts teaching. Victory-runes, ale-runes, birth-runes, wave-runes, branch-runes, speech-runes, mind-runes. Then eleven numbered counsels on how to live: be clean toward your kin, swear no oath you cannot keep, do not argue with fools, do not trust the son of the man you killed, wash and comb the dead you find and pray they rest. The Norse tradition puts its practical wisdom literature in the mouths of exactly two figures. One is Odin. The other is her.

And the second counsel she gives him is to swear no oath he does not know to be true, because bitter is the fate of the breaker of troth. He then breaks his oath to her, under a spell, and she has him killed for it. She taught him the rule she executes him under. Nobody in the poems comments on this. It just sits there.

Downstream, she becomes Wagner's Brünnhilde, which is how most people meet her, softened into a redeemer who dies for love. Further downstream, and much more quietly, she is the sleeping woman behind a barrier of fire and thorn who can only be woken by the one man brave enough to reach her, which is Sleeping Beauty, stripped of everything that made the original interesting. The thing the fairy tale loses is that she was not cursed for being beautiful and passive. She was sentenced for insubordination.

family life

Father: King Buðli, who at the last parting gave her a gold ring and then made her a straightforward commercial offer — marry whoever I say, or lose your inheritance and my protection. She weighed whether to fight him and decided the war was not winnable, which she reports later in the driest possible terms: she let the bond be made, because she wanted the hoard more than she wanted the fight. It is one of the few moments in myth where a woman admits, in plain language, that she was bought and knew it at the time.

Brother: Atli, the Hun-king, who was the one who took her aside privately and asked whether she wanted the man on Grani. She said yes. He is also the man who eventually throws her widower Gunnar into a pit of snakes, and who is himself knifed in bed by Guthrun. Sister: Bekkhild, the domestic one, who stayed home doing needlework, which is exactly the fate the giantess later tells Brynhildr she should have accepted. Another sister in the tradition, Oddrún, gets her own poem of grief, weeping over Gunnar in the snake pit years afterward. Foster-father: Heimir at Hlymdalir, who raised her and who is the only person she confides in when she works out that she has been tricked.

She had one daughter. In the Völsunga saga, on the very day she discovers the deception, before she says anything to anyone else, she tells Heimir: Aslaug the daughter of me and Sigurd shall be nourished here with thee. A child, from the mountain, hidden with the foster-father, arranged for in one sentence while her life is collapsing. Aslaug survives everything, is smuggled out in a harp, and becomes the wife of Ragnar Loðbrók, which means half the legendary kings of Scandinavia claim descent from Brynhildr. She never mentions the child again.

And then the part that no retelling can rinse. When she demanded Sigurðr's death she also demanded the death of his son by Guthrun, a boy of three, and gave a coldly practical reason for it: let not long the wolf-cub live; lighter to pay is the vengeance-price after the deed if the son is dead. The child was killed and burned on the pyre beside his father. She is not a wronged innocent. She is a woman who did the arithmetic of a blood feud out loud and then had it carried out.

romantic life

One love, one wedding to the wrong man, and eight nights with a sword in the bed.

Sigurðr came through the fire on Hindarfjoll, cut the mail off her with the sword Gram, and they swore themselves to each other on the mountain. Then he rode down into the world and into the hall of the Gjukungs, where the queen Grimhild — the actual villain of this story, and the one everyone forgets — gave him a drink of forgetting so that her own daughter could have him. He married Guthrun without remembering that Brynhildr existed. Note what the poison does: it does not make him love someone else. It deletes a name.

Then Gunnar, Grimhild's son, went to woo the woman inside the fire, and his horse would not go in. He borrowed Grani; Grani would not carry him either. So Grimhild's magic swapped the two men's shapes, and Sigurðr in Gunnar's body rode the flames a second time, for someone else, without knowing he had ever ridden them for himself. He stayed three nights in her hall as her husband, or eight, depending on which poem you believe, and laid his naked sword between them in the bed every night, which is the detail Brynhildr later throws in everyone's face as proof of what kind of man he was. He took the ring from her hand and gave her another, and rode out and changed back.

She married Gunnar knowing something was wrong and unable to name it. What broke it open was two women bathing in a river. Brynhildr waded further out, upstream, and said the water running off Guthrun's hair should not touch her, because her own husband was the braver man. Guthrun let her finish and then produced the ring: and Fafnir he slew, yea, and he rode thy flaming fire, whereas thou didst deem that he was Gunnar the King, and by thy side he lay, and took from thine hand the ring Andvari's-loom;—here mayst thou well behold it. The saga says Brynhildr saw the ring, knew it, went as white as a dead woman, walked home and did not speak for the rest of the evening.

What she does next is the part I find impossible to look away from. She goes to bed for seven days without food or drink or a word to anyone. Gunnar comes, Hogni comes, they get nothing. Then Sigurðr comes in, pulls the covers off her and tells her the sun is shining and she has slept enough. And she talks. He offers her everything — gold, his life, all his wealth if she will not die, and finally, in the most extraordinary line he speaks in the whole cycle, he offers to put away Guthrun and marry her instead. It is the only thing she has wanted since the mountain. She has him. And she says no. I will not have thee, nay, nor any other. Because she will not be a second arrangement, and she will not have two kings in one hall, and above all because she made a vow and the vow has already been broken and the woman she was on that mountain cannot be reassembled out of an apology. Sigurðr walks out with his heart swelling so hard that the iron rings of his mail shirt burst apart down his sides.

The truest line about the two of them is the one she says to the giantess at the very end, describing those nights: Happy we slept, one bed we had, as he my brother born had been. It is not a love story about consummation. It is a story about two people who recognised each other exactly once and then spent the rest of their lives being administered by other people's plans.

life until death

She told Gunnar to kill him, and she used the only leverage she had: herself. Lose me, lose my lands, lose your standing as the best of heroes; I will go back to my own people and sleep my life away if you do not dare it. Gunnar sat bowed over it for a whole day, because he loved Sigurðr. Hogni refused outright — not out of tenderness but because they had sworn blood-oaths and because he could see the size of the mistake. So they woke up the youngest brother, Gotthorm, who had sworn nothing, fed him wolf-meat and snake to make him savage, and sent him in. The sword went through Sigurðr in his bed with Guthrun asleep beside him. He woke enough to throw Gram after the killer and cut him in half at the door, then said his last words, which were, in effect, this was Brynhild's doing, and I never once betrayed Gunnar.

Guthrun clapped her hands together so hard the cups rang in the cupboard and the geese screamed in the yard. And lying in the next room, Brynhildr heard her and laughed. Once. With her whole heart. It is the single most famous laugh in Norse literature and both poems that record it stop to say that it was the only time she ever laughed like that.

Then, before dawn, she woke up and started weeping for the thing she had asked for with laughter, and nobody around her could follow the change — the poet says plainly that they were all silent and none of them knew her heart. She put on her gold mail. She had eight of her thralls and five of her women killed to go with him. She gave away all her jewellery, telling the remaining women to come and take gold, and they refused, and she said, fine, then nobody dies unwilling for my sake — a flash of something almost decent in the middle of an atrocity. Then she took a sword and put it in under her own armpit, lay back on the pillows, and spent her last breath prophesying the future of everyone in the room with total accuracy: Guthrun will be given to Atli against her will, Gunnar will love Oddrún in secret, Atli will throw Gunnar into a snake pit, Guthrun will kill Atli in his bed, Svanhild will be born and will be destroyed by the slander of Bikki, and the line will end. All of it happens.

Her last request was staging. Build one pyre wide enough for all of us. Cover it in cloth dyed red. Burn me on one side of him with my slaves at his head and feet, hawks and hounds with him, and lay the drawn sword between us again the way it lay when we were called man and wife. And — this is the line — nor shall the door swing to at the heel of him as I go behind him. The door of the hall of the dead will not close on him before she is through it. She has spent the entire story being arranged by other people and she uses her last five minutes to direct the one scene she controls, down to the props.

Then comes the strangest surviving piece of her, the Helreið Brynhildar, the Hell-Ride. She is dead, riding a covered wagon down the road to the underworld, and a giantess comes out of a rock house and blocks her and calls her a whore and a home-wrecker and tells her she should have stayed at her weaving. And Brynhildr, dead, on her way to hell, stops and argues her case. She lays out the whole biography — the stolen swan-robes, the oath at twelve, Hjálmgunnar, Odin's fury, the shields at Skatalund, the fire, the man on Grani, the eight chaste nights, the betrayal — and finishes by telling the giantess to sink into the ground, because she and Sigurðr are going to be together now. It is a defence speech delivered to a hostile audience with nothing left to gain. She is not asking to be forgiven. She is correcting the record.

their works

Brynhildr wrote nothing; she is a character in anonymous poems that were sung for centuries before anyone wrote them down in Iceland around 1270. But she is given more direct speech than almost any woman in early Germanic literature, and the words below are hers within the text. Verse is Henry Adams Bellows's translation of the Poetic Edda (American-Scandinavian Foundation, 1923); prose is William Morris and Eiríkr Magnússon's Völsunga Saga (1888). Both long out of copyright. The wide gaps inside the lines are the caesura of the original metre.

1. The waking, from the Sigrdrífumál. The prose frame first, because it matters that he does not know what he has found.

Sigurth rode up on Hindarfjoll and turned southward toward the land of the Franks. On the mountain he saw a great light, as if fire were burning, and the glow reached up to heaven. And when he came thither, there stood a tower of shields, and above it was a banner. Sigurth went into the shield-tower, and saw that a man lay there sleeping with all his war-weapons. First he took the helm from his head, and then he saw that it was a woman. The mail-coat was as fast as if it had grown to the flesh. Then he cut the mail-coat from the head-opening downward, and out to both the arm-holes. Then he took the mail-coat from her, and she awoke, and sat up and saw Sigurth, and said:
“What bit through the byrnie? how was broken my sleep?
Who made me free of the fetters pale?”

He answered:
“Sigmund's son, with Sigurth's sword,
That late with flesh hath fed the ravens.”

Then she stands up and blesses the entire visible world before she says one word about herself.

“Hail, day! Hail, sons of day!
And night and her daughter now!
Look on us here with loving eyes,
That waiting we victory win.

Hail to the gods! Ye goddesses, hail,
And all the generous earth!
Give to us wisdom and goodly speech,
And healing hands, life-long.

Long did I sleep, my slumber was long,
And long are the griefs of life;
Othin decreed that I could not break
The heavy spells of sleep.”

Three stanzas, and the third one turns. Long are the griefs of life. She has been unconscious for years and she wakes up already knowing how this ends.

2. The sentence and the loophole. The prose that follows, where the annotator explains why she was asleep, and where she quotes her own counter-vow.

Sigrdrifa slew Hjalmgunnar in the battle, and Othin pricked her with the sleep-thorn in punishment for this, and said that she should never thereafter win victory in battle, but that she should be wedded. “And I said to him that I had made a vow in my turn, that I would never marry a man who knew the meaning of fear.”

3. The teaching. She hands him a horn of mead and begins. This is the oldest surviving rune-lore in the language, and it is in a woman's mouth.

“Beer I bring thee, tree of battle,
Mingled of strength and mighty fame;
Charms it holds and healing signs,
Spells full good, and gladness-runes.

Winning-runes learn, if thou longest to win,
And the runes on thy sword-hilt write;
Some on the furrow, and some on the flat,
And twice shalt thou call on Tyr.

Birth-runes learn, if help thou wilt lend,
The babe from the mother to bring;
On thy palms shalt write them, and round thy joints,
And ask the fates to aid.”

And then the counsels. Eleven of them, numbered. Here are four, including the second one, which is the one that kills him.

“Then first I rede thee, that free of guilt
Toward kinsmen ever thou art;
No vengeance have, though they work thee harm,
Reward after death thou shalt win.

Then second I rede thee, to swear no oath
If true thou knowest it not;
Bitter the fate of the breaker of troth,
And poor is the wolf of his word.

Then ninth I rede thee: burial render
If thou findest a fallen corpse,
Of sickness dead, or dead in the sea,
Or dead of weapons' wounds.

A bath shalt thou give them who corpses be,
And hands and head shalt wash;
Wipe them and comb, ere they go in the coffin,
And pray that they sleep in peace.”

The lesson ends with the two stanzas that most scholars think belong to the original poem, before anyone bolted the rune-lists on. She tells him, flatly, what the price of her is.

Brynhild spake:
“Now shalt thou choose, for the choice is given,
Thou tree of the biting blade;
Speech or silence, 'tis thine to say,
Our evil is destined all.”

Sigurth spake:
“I shall not flee, though my fate be near,
I was born not a coward to be;
Thy loving word for mine will I win,
As long as I shall live.”

Our evil is destined all. She warns him at the moment of greatest happiness that the whole thing is already doomed, and he says he doesn't care. That is the entire relationship in eight lines.

4. The river. From the Völsunga Saga. Two queens bathing, and the most consequential argument in Norse literature. Note that Brynhildr is not lying about anything, and that Guthrun's weapon is a piece of jewellery.

On a day as the Queens went to the river to bathe them, Brynhild waded the farthest out into the river; then asked Gudrun what that deed might signify.

Brynhild said, “Yea, and why then should I be equal to thee in this matter more than in others? I am minded to think that my father is mightier than thine, and my true love has wrought many wondrous works of fame, and hath ridden the flaming fire withal, while thy husband was but the thrall of King Hjalprek.”

Gudrun answered full of wrath, “Thou wouldst be wise if thou shouldst hold thy peace rather than revile my husband … and little it beseems thee of all folk to mock him who was thy first beloved: and Fafnir he slew, yea, and he rode thy flaming fire, whereas thou didst deem that he was Gunnar the King, and by thy side he lay, and took from thine hand the ring Andvari's-loom;—here mayst thou well behold it!”

Then Brynhild saw the ring and knew it, and waxed as wan as a dead woman, and she went home and spake no word the evening long.

The next morning Guthrun tries to make peace and Brynhildr will not take it. Her answer is one of the coldest sentences in the saga.

“Ask such things only as are good for thee to know—matters meet for mighty dames. Good to love good things when all goes according to thy heart's desire! … For this shalt thou pay, in that thou hast got Sigurd to thee,—nowise can I see thee living in the bliss thereof, whereas thou hast him, and the wealth and the might of him.”

5. The bower. Seven days face down in bed, no food, no speech. She tears her needlework in half and has the doors thrown open so the whole household can hear her. Then Sigurðr walks in and pulls the blanket off her and tells her to cheer up.

“Awake, Brynhild! The sun shineth now over all the house, and thou hast slept enough; cast off grief from thee, and take up gladness!”

She said, “And how then hast thou dared to come to me? in this treason none was worse to me than thou.”

… “Never did Gunnar ride through the fire to me, nor did he give me to dower the host of the slain: I wondered at the man who came into my hall; for I deemed indeed that I knew thine eyes; but I might not see clearly, or divide the good from the evil, because of the veil that lay heavy on my fortune.”

I deemed indeed that I knew thine eyes. She recognised him through the disguise and did not trust herself. Then he asks what angers her most, and she tells him.

“This is the sorest sorrow to me,” she said, “that the bitter sword is not reddened in thy blood.”

“Have no fear thereof!” says he, “no long while to wait or the bitter sword stand deep in my heart; and no worse needest thou to pray for thyself, for thou wilt not live when I am dead; the days of our two lives shall be few enough from henceforth.”

And then he offers her the thing she has wanted since the mountain, and she refuses it.

“This is truer,” says Sigurd, “that I loved thee better than myself, though I fell into the wiles from whence our lives may not escape …”

Brynhild answered, and said, “Too late thou tellest me that my grief grieved thee: little pity shall I find now.”

… Said Brynhild, “Such words may nowise be spoken, nor will I have two kings in one hall; I will lay my life down rather than beguile Gunnar the King.” … “But now is all changed, and I will not live.”

“Rather than thou die, I will wed thee, and put away Gudrun,” said Sigurd.

But therewithal so swelled the heart betwixt the sides of him, that the rings of his byrny burst asunder.

“I will not have thee,” says Brynhild, “nay, nor any other!”

Then Sigurd got him gone.

The saga then quotes an older song for the exit, which is the only physical description of grief I know of in Old Norse that works like a wound.

“Out then went Sigurd,
The great kings' well-loved,
From the speech and the sorrow,
Sore drooping, so grieving,
That the shirt round about him
Of iron rings woven,
From the sides brake asunder
Of the brave in the battle.”

6. The demand. From the Sigurðarkviða hin skamma, the Short Lay of Sigurth. This is her thinking it through alone, and then saying it to Gunnar. The last stanza here is the one about the child.

By herself at the end of day she sat,
And in open words her heart she uttered:
“I shall Sigurth have, the hero young,
E'en though within my arms he die.

The word I have spoken; soon shall I rue it,
His wife is Guthrun, and Gunnar's am I;
Ill Norns set for me long desire.”

Oft did she go with grieving heart
On the glacier's ice at even-tide,
When Guthrun then to her bed was gone,
And the bedclothes Sigurth about her laid.

“Now Gjuki's child to her lover goes,
And the Hunnish king with his wife is happy;
Joyless I am and mateless ever,
Till cries from my heavy heart burst forth.”

In her wrath to battle she roused herself:
“Gunnar, now thou needs must lose
Lands of mine and me myself,
No joy shall I have with the hero ever.

Back shall I fare where first I dwelt,
Among the kin that come of my race,
To wait there, sleeping my life away,
If Sigurth's death thou shalt not dare.

The son shall fare with his father hence,
And let not long the wolf-cub live;
Lighter to pay is the vengeance-price
After the deed if the son is dead.”

Read the second stanza again. The word I have spoken; soon shall I rue it. She knows, in the moment of saying it, that she will regret it, and she says it anyway, and then she executes it. That is not a woman losing control. That is a woman watching herself choose.

7. The laugh. Two separate poems preserve it. First the Brot af Sigurðarkviðu, then the Short Lay.

Then Brynhild laughed— and the building echoed—
Only once, with all her heart;
“Long shall ye joy in lands and men,
Now ye have slain the hero noble.”
Then Brynhild, daughter of Buthli, laughed,
Only once, with all her heart,
When as she lay full loud she heard
The grievous wail of Gjuki's daughter.

And then, before dawn, the turn. This is the stanza that makes her a person instead of a monster.

Brynhild awoke, the daughter of Buthli,
The warrior's daughter, ere dawn of day:
“Love me or hate me, the harm is done,
And my grief cries out, or else I die.”

Silent were all who heard her speak,
And nought of the heart of the queen they knew,
Who wept such tears the thing to tell
That laughing once of the men she had won.

8. The account of herself. Still the Short Lay. Gunnar has just called her a mother of ill. She answers with her entire life, and the third stanza here is the transaction with her father.

“To thee I say, and thyself thou knowest,
That all these ills thou didst early shape;
No bonds I knew, nor sorrow bore,
And wealth I had in my brother's home.

Never a husband sought I to have,
Before the Gjukungs fared to our land;
Three were the kings on steeds that came,—
Need of their journey never there was.

To the hero great my troth I gave
Who gold-decked sat on Grani's back;
Not like to thine was the light of his eyes,
Though kingly both ye seemed to be.

And so to me did Atli say
That share in our wealth I should not have,
Of gold or lands, if my hand I gave not …

Oft in my mind I pondered much
If still I should fight, and warriors fell,
Brave in my byrnie, my brother defying;
That would wide in the world be known,
And sorrow for many a man would make.

But the bond at last I let be made,
For more the hoard I longed to have …

One-alone of all I loved,
Nor changing heart I ever had;
All in the end shall Atli know,
When he hears I have gone on the death-road hence.”

9. Dying. The sword is already in. Note that she pauses, in the middle of a suicide, to release her women from following her.

Her byrnie of gold she donned, and grim
Was her heart ere the point of her sword had pierced it;
On the pillow at last her head she laid,
And, wounded, her plan she pondered o'er.

… Long the woman, linen-decked, pondered,—
—Young she was,— and weighed her words:
“For my sake now shall none unwilling
Or loath to die her life lay down.

Sit now, Gunnar! for I shall speak
Of thy bride so fair and so fain to die;
Thy ship in harbor home thou hast not,
Although my life I now have lost. …

Well I remember how me ye treated
When ye betrayed me with treacherous wiles;
Lost was my joy as long as I lived.”

Then the staging instructions, and the last four lines she ever speaks.

“Yet one boon I beg of thee,
The last of boons in my life it is:
Let the pyre be built so broad in the field
That room for us all will ample be …

Let between us lie once more
The steel so keen, as so it lay
When both within one bed we were,
And wedded mates by men were called.

The door of the hall shall strike not the heel
Of the hero fair with flashing rings,
If hence my following goes with him;
Not mean our faring forth shall be. …

Much have I told thee, and more would say
If fate more space for speech had given;
My voice grows weak, my wounds are swelling;
Truth I have said, and so I die.”

Truth I have said, and so I die. Not sorry, not absolved. Accurate.

10. The Hell-Ride, complete. The Helreið Brynhildar is only fourteen stanzas and it is the whole woman in miniature: accused, dead, still arguing. Here it is entire.

After the death of Brynhild there were made two bale-fires, the one for Sigurth, and that burned first, and on the other was Brynhild burned, and she was on a wagon which was covered with a rich cloth. Thus it is told, that Brynhild went in the wagon on Hel-way, and passed by a house where dwelt a certain giantess.
The giantess spake:
“Thou shalt not further forward fare,
My dwelling ribbed with rocks across;
More seemly it were at thy weaving to stay,
Than another's husband here to follow.

What wouldst thou have from Valland here,
Fickle of heart, in this my house?
Gold-goddess, now, if thou wouldst know,
Heroes' blood from thy hands hast washed.”

Brynhild spake:
“Chide me not, woman from rocky walls,
Though to battle once I was wont to go;
Better than thou I shall seem to be,
When men us two shall truly know.”

The giantess spake:
“Thou wast, Brynhild, Buthli's daughter,
For the worst of evils born in the world;
To death thou hast given Gjuki's children,
And laid their lofty house full low.”

Brynhild spake:
“Truth from the wagon here I tell thee,
Witless one, if know thou wilt
How the heirs of Gjuki gave me to be
Joyless ever, a breaker of oaths.

Hild the helmed in Hlymdalir
They named me of old, all they who knew me.

The monarch bold the swan-robes bore
Of the sisters eight beneath an oak;
Twelve winters I was, if know thou wilt,
When oaths I yielded the king so young.

Next I let the leader of Goths,
Hjalmgunnar the old, go down to hell,
And victory brought to Autha's brother;
For this was Othin's anger mighty.

He beset me with shields in Skatalund,
Red and white, their rims o'erlapped;
He bade that my sleep should broken be
By him who fear had nowhere found.

He let round my hall, that southward looked,
The branches' foe high-leaping burn;
Across it he bade the hero come
Who brought me the gold that Fafnir guarded.

On Grani rode the giver of gold,
Where my foster-father ruled his folk;
Best of all he seemed to be,
The prince of the Danes, when the people met.

Happy we slept, one bed we had,
As he my brother born had been;
Eight were the nights when neither there
Loving hand on the other laid.

Yet Guthrun reproached me, Gjuki's daughter,
That I in Sigurth's arms had slept;
Then did I hear what I would were hid,
That they had betrayed me in taking a mate.

Ever with grief and all too long
Are men and women born in the world;
But yet we shall live our lives together,
Sigurth and I. Sink down, Giantess!”

Ever with grief and all too long are men and women born in the world. It is the flattest, widest line in the poem and she says it two stanzas before the end, having just narrated her own destruction without asking anyone for sympathy. Then she orders a giantess out of her way and keeps driving.

Marina Tsvetaeva

Moscow & exile, 1892–1941 · the most ferocious poet Russia produced, who buried a daughter she couldn't feed, wrote her own tombstone at twenty, and was right about everything except how long she'd have to wait

aug 9, 2026▼

childhood & early life

She was born in Moscow on 8 October 1892, in a house on Trekhprudny Lane that she would spend the rest of her life writing about and that would be demolished before she was forty. Her father, Ivan Tsvetaev, was the son of a village priest, had grown up genuinely poor, and had clawed his way to a professorship in art history at Moscow University. He spent thirty years raising money for a museum of casts and antiquities that opened in 1912 and is now the Pushkin Museum. He was a decent, distracted, overworked man who was in love with his dead first wife.

Her mother, Maria Mein, was twenty-one when she married him. He was forty-five, widowed, with two small children already. Maria was a pianist good enough for a concert career and had been forbidden one, because respectable women did not perform. She had also been forbidden the man she actually loved. So she married a kind widower who could not love her back properly, and poured the entire unspent force of her life into her children like water into a narrow glass. She wanted a son. She had decided on the name Alexander. What arrived was Marina, and the family story is that her mother's first response to the baby was, essentially, at least she will be a musician.

That is the household. A father married to a ghost, a mother married to a thwarted ambition, and a child assigned a career before she could focus her eyes. Marina was at the piano before she could read. She could read at four. She was writing verse at six, in Russian, German and French. Her mother's method was total: music was not a hobby, it was a moral condition, and idleness was a sin against the instrument. Tsvetaeva later said her mother flooded her with music and that the flood is why she became a poet instead of a pianist. She had to find something her mother had not already occupied.

In 1902 Maria was diagnosed with tuberculosis and the family became nomads for her lungs. Italy, Switzerland, Germany. Marina and her sister Asya were parked in Catholic boarding schools in Lausanne and Freiburg while their mother coughed in hotels, which is how a Russian girl ended up trilingual and permanently unable to feel at home anywhere. Her mother died in the summer of 1906, at thirty-seven. Marina was fourteen. The music stopped being compulsory that day and she stopped playing almost immediately.

What followed was three years of being expelled from Moscow schools for insolence, reading everything, worshipping Napoleon and Sarah Bernhardt with the intensity other teenagers reserve for religion, and going alone to Paris at sixteen to take a summer course in French literature at the Sorbonne. Then, at seventeen, she gathered her poems, walked into a printer's without telling her father or anyone else, and paid out of her own pocket for five hundred copies of a book called Evening Album. This was not how debuts happened. Three of the most important critics in Russia reviewed it anyway. Maximilian Voloshin liked it so much he came to the house to find out who she was, and stayed her friend for life.

what she did & why she's remembered

She is one of the four poets who define Russian poetry in the twentieth century, alongside Akhmatova, Mandelstam and Pasternak, and she is the one who broke the most equipment. Akhmatova is a scalpel; Tsvetaeva is a hammer thrown at a wall. She wrote in a compressed, dash-riddled, syntactically violent Russian, sentences amputated mid-word, stresses hammered onto syllables that shouldn't carry them, meaning delivered by rhythm and rupture as much as by vocabulary. Translators describe her as close to impossible, because Russian can pack case, gender, direction and emotional register into a single ending, and English has to unpack it into a paragraph and lose the punch.

She wrote roughly a thousand lyric poems, seventeen long narrative poems, verse dramas on Greek subjects (Theseus, Phaedra, Ariadne), some of the finest literary prose in the language, and thousands of pages of letters that are arguably her real masterpiece. Her subject, more or less always, is the collision between an unlimited interior and a world with a fixed capacity. Desire that exceeds its object. Love that is really a demand to be met at full voice, which almost nobody could survive.

She was also structurally incapable of being on a side. In 1917 the revolution arrived and she wrote a book of poems glorifying the White Army while living in Bolshevik Moscow, which was suicidal. Then in exile she praised Mayakovsky, the Soviet poet, at a Paris reading in 1928, and the émigré press dropped her, which removed her only income. She had contempt for orthodoxy of any kind and no talent whatsoever for the political self-preservation that everyone around her was practising. The Russians abroad thought she was a Bolshevik. The Soviets thought she was a White. She was neither; she was a poet, and she kept saying so, as though that were a passport.

The result is that she was almost entirely unread for most of her life and for two decades after her death. Her work was not published in the Soviet Union until the 1960s. Then it detonated. She is now on school syllabuses in the country that starved her, there are museums in the houses she was thrown out of, and there is a monument in the town where nobody would give her a job washing dishes. She had predicted this precisely, in a poem written at twenty, and it is in the works section below, and it is unbearable to read with the dates attached.

family life

Father: Ivan Tsvetaev, museum builder, died 1913, a year after his museum opened, having never quite noticed his daughter had become famous. Mother: Maria Mein, dead at thirty-seven of tuberculosis when Marina was fourteen. One full sister, Anastasia (Asya), who outlived her by nearly fifty years and survived the camps. Two half-siblings from her father's first marriage, Valeria and Andrei, raised in the same house with a completely different mother in it, which is its own quiet war.

Her own three children: Ariadna, called Alya, born September 1912. Irina, born April 1917, four days before the first free elections Russia would have for seventy-four years. Georgy, called Mur, born February 1925 in a Czech village, adored by his mother with an intensity that was probably terrible to be on the receiving end of.

Alya was the great love of Tsvetaeva's life in a way no adult ever was. She was a startling child who kept a diary from the age of six that her mother published passages from, and who functioned as confidante, secretary and emotional peer for a woman who had no peers. Then Alya grew up, wanted a normal life, became enthusiastically Soviet, went back to Russia in 1937 ahead of the rest of the family, and was arrested in August 1939, two months after her mother followed her home. She was tortured, signed a statement against her own father, and served eight years in the camps and another nine in Siberian exile. Rehabilitated in 1955. She spent the last twenty years of her life editing and publishing her mother's work, which is the only reason a great deal of it exists.

Mur was sixteen when his mother killed herself, and he was, by his own diary, cold about it. He was also a child who had been dragged from France into a war he did not choose, whose father had been shot and whose sister was in a camp. He was drafted at eighteen and killed at the front in 1944, at nineteen. There is no grave for him either. Of the five people in that family, two were shot or died in uniform, one hanged herself, one did sixteen years for a crime that did not exist, and one starved to death at two.

romantic life

In the summer of 1911, at Voloshin's house in Koktebel on the Black Sea, she met a seventeen-year-old boy on the beach. Sergei Efron: orphaned, tubercular, beautiful in an enormous-eyed way, the son of revolutionaries, gentle and not remotely her equal in force. She decided on the spot, half-joking, that if he found and gave her a carnelian — a particular reddish stone scattered on that beach — she would marry him. He found one the same day, without looking, and put it in her hand. She married him in January 1912. She was nineteen.

She loved him for thirty years and was faithful to him almost never. The pattern established itself early: an overwhelming attachment, usually conducted at least half on paper, burning at a temperature the other person could not sustain, then collapse, then a poem cycle. In 1914 she met the poet Sophia Parnok at a literary salon and fell into a year and a half of the first open love affair between two women in Russian literature. She was twenty-two, married, with a toddler; Parnok was thirty and known. The seventeen poems of the Girlfriend cycle are the record, and they are astonishingly modern — not romantic haze but forensic self-interrogation, who was the hunter and who was the prey, did I win or was I beaten. Efron, humiliated, went off to the front as a medical orderly. The affair ended in 1916 with Tsvetaeva walking out.

Then Osip Mandelstam, briefly and mostly in poems. Then the civil war separated her from Efron for five years with no word at all — she did not know whether he was alive until 1921. Then, in Prague in 1923, the one that actually took the roof off: Konstantin Rodzevich, a former Red Army officer, Efron's friend, not an intellectual, not remotely a poet. Three months, September to mid-December. He was the only lover who wanted the ordinary woman in her rather than the volcano, which is precisely why it destroyed her when he married someone else. She produced Poem of the Mountain and Poem of the End out of it, two of the greatest long poems in the language, and she was writing them while helping Rodzevich's fiancée pick out a wedding dress.

And then the strangest one. In 1926 Pasternak introduced her by letter to Rainer Maria Rilke, who was dying of leukaemia in Switzerland. Over one summer the three of them conducted a correspondence at a pitch no ordinary relationship reaches, and Tsvetaeva fell completely in love with a man she never met and never would. She told him what to dream. She proposed meeting; he deflected; she was wounded and went quiet. He died on 29 December 1926. She wrote him a long poem as a New Year's greeting, addressed to his new address, which was death. She kept writing to him for years. It is the most honest relationship she ever had, because the other party could not disappoint her by being a person.

Through all of it, Efron. He waited. He was the one constant, and he is also the reason she died: in Paris in the 1930s he was recruited by the Soviet secret police, took part in the operation that led to the assassination of the defector Ignace Reiss in 1937, and fled to Moscow when the French police came. The émigré community, which already disliked her, now treated her as the wife of a murderer. Nobody would print her. Nobody would speak to her. She followed him home in 1939 because there was nowhere else on earth left, and she wrote to a friend that she was going as a dog goes, following.

life until death

The worst thing happened first, and everything after it was aftershock. Moscow, winter of 1919. Efron was somewhere with the White Army, possibly dead. There was no food, no fuel, no money and no work she could hold; she tried an office job and lasted weeks. She was burning furniture, trading her possessions for millet, writing all night by a smoking lamp with two small children asleep in the kitchen. In November she heard that the state orphanage at Kuntsevo was being supplied through American relief, and she put both girls in it. It was not, and they were not fed. She visited, found Alya dangerously ill, and took her home to nurse her. She left Irina, who was two, because she could only carry one of them, and because Alya was the one she could not lose.

Irina died there of hunger in February 1920, a few weeks short of three. Tsvetaeva did not go to the burial. She wrote, later, with the terrible accuracy she used on herself and nobody else, that she had grabbed the elder one out of the darkness with both hands and failed to protect the younger. She never stopped explaining it and never once forgave herself, and she was also entirely aware that she had chosen, and that a choice is different from an accident.

In 1922 she got out: Berlin, then three years in villages outside Prague, which were the poorest and the most productive years she had, hauling water, scrubbing floors, writing on her knees. Then fourteen years in Paris, getting steadily more isolated, sewing, cleaning, cadging small subscriptions from friends, writing prose because prose paid and poetry did not. She said the thing that explains her whole exile in one sentence: she had left Russia in 1922 and her reader had stayed behind, so she was abroad without readers and in Russia without books.

She went back in June 1939. Two months later Alya was arrested. Two months after that, Efron. She spent 1940 standing in prison queues with parcels, the same queues Akhmatova stood in, doing the same arithmetic about whether a parcel accepted meant the person was still alive. She had no flat, no residence permit, no publisher. She survived on translation work. In June 1941 she and Akhmatova finally met, twice, in Moscow — two women who had circled each other in print for twenty-five years, in a borrowed room, both destroyed, and neither of them left a satisfying account of it.

Germany invaded that same month. On 8 August 1941 she and Mur were evacuated east. The Writers' Union families went to Chistopol; she was sent on to Yelabuga, a small town in Tatarstan where she knew no one and there was no work of any kind. On 26 August she travelled to Chistopol and submitted a written application to the Literary Fund asking to be taken on as a dishwasher in a canteen that had not yet opened. It survives. It is four lines long and it is signed M. Tsvetaeva. She was not given the job.

She went back to Yelabuga. Mur wrote in his diary on 27 August that his mother lived in a suicidal fog and talked of nothing else. On Sunday 31 August 1941 the family she was lodging with went out, and Mur went out, and she was alone in the entryway of a stranger's wooden house. She wrote three notes: one to whoever found her, asking them to get her son to Chistopol; one to the poet Nikolai Aseev, begging him to take Mur in and to accept a trunk containing her notebooks; and one to Mur. Then she hanged herself from a nail. She was forty-eight. Aseev did neither of the things she asked.

Sergei Efron was shot in October 1941, six weeks later. She never knew. Mur was killed at the front in 1944. Alya came back from the camps in 1955 and spent the rest of her life getting her mother into print. Nobody recorded where the grave was; there is now a stone in the Yelabuga cemetery that says, honestly, that Marina Tsvetaeva is buried somewhere in this part of it.

her works

Most of the poems below are in A. S. Kline's translations, free to reproduce non-commercially, from his two Tsvetaeva collections at poetryintranslation.com. Where another translator is used the name is on the poem. Read them in order; they are arranged by her life, and the arc is the point.

Start with the prophecy. She wrote this in Koktebel in May 1913, aged twenty, with two thin self-published books to her name and no reason on earth to believe it. Her poems were not properly published in Russia until the 1960s, more than twenty years after she was dead. Every word of this came true, just too slowly to save her.

For my poems, written so young
I'd not dreamed I was a poet,
Like drops from the fountain flung,
Like sparks from a rocket,

That burst into the realm of true
Sleep, and incense, like tiny demons,
For my poems about death and youth,
– For my unread sermons! –

Scattered in dusty stores, in line,
Where, un-purchased, they are dumb,
For my poems, like precious wine,
A time will come.

Same month, same beach, aged twenty: she writes her own grave and talks to the stranger standing over it. She was buried in an unmarked plot twenty-eight years later and nobody knows which one. This poem is now carved on the memorial stone.

Walking, you're just like me,
Your eyes are on the ground.
I used to lower mine, you see.
Stop passer-by, at this mound!

When you've picked a cluster
Of buttercups, poppies, a few –
Read, I was named Marina,
And how old I was, too.

Don't think, this is – a grave,
That I'll appear – too scary! …
I myself when I shouldn't have,
Loved to laugh much too loudly!

And the blood rushed to my face,
And my hair was curly…
Passer-by, I held your place!
Passer-by, stop: and read me!

Break a bramble, and after
Pluck from it a berry,
No strawberry's larger, sweeter
Than one from a cemetery.

But don't stand there gloomy,
Your head on your chest!
Think about me lightly,
Think of me, and forget.

Ah, how the sun shines on you!
Golden dust all round…
– Don't let it upset you,
My voice from underground.

Now Sophia Parnok. October 1914, the second poem of the Girlfriend cycle, written the morning after. Nobody in Russian had written this before and it is not soft; it is a woman conducting an audit of her own defeat. Note that the only calm participant is the cat.

Under the plush rug's caress
I summon yesterday's dream.
What was it? — Whose conquest? —
Who was conquered?

I reconsider everything again,
Am tortured by everything anew.
In that, for which I don't know the word,
Was there love?

Who was the hunter? — Who, the prey?
Everything so devilishly topsy-turvy!
What did the Siberian cat understand,
Constantly purring?

In that self-willed duel
Which of us was being played with like a ball?
Whose heart — was it yours, or mine
That flew at a gallop?

And all the same — what was that?
What I so want and regret?
Even now I don't know: did I conquer?
Was I conquered?
— 23 October 1914, trans. Karina McCorkle

The end of that affair, in two pieces. The first is what leaving felt like from inside. The second is eight lines and contains the single most Tsvetaeva sentence ever written about betrayal.

No thought, no complaint, no argument.
No sleep.
No longing for the sun, the moon, the sea.
Nor a sail.

No sense of the heat of these four walls,
The garden's greenness.
No longing for the gift desired,
No expectation.

No pleasure in the morning, in the tram's
Jingling course.
Not seeing the day, forgetful, I live,
Nor yet the date, nor the century.

I seem to tread a fraying tightrope,
I – a little dancer,
I – a shadow, of another's shadow,
I – a sleepwalker,
Under two dark moons.
A gypsy passion's separation!
Met – we're already torn apart.
I let my head drop in my hands,
And brood: staring at the night.

No one, leafing through our letters,
Could comprehend the depths of them,
How treacherous we were, that is –
How true we were to ourselves.

How treacherous we were, that is, how true we were to ourselves. She is not apologising. She is telling you that fidelity to another person and fidelity to your own nature are two different debts and she has decided which one she pays.

April 1916, for Alexander Blok, whom she never dared speak to. Six syllables of pure sound-work: in Russian his name is five letters, and she spends the whole poem putting it in her mouth. Watch the temperature drop line by line until the last word is ice.

Your name – a bird in the hand,
Your name – ice on the tongue.
One swift movement of the lips.
Your name – four letters long.
A ball – that's caught in flight,
In the mouth jingling silver.

A stone, thrown in a quiet pond,
No sigh, like your name ever.
At night a light clatter of hooves,
Your name a noisy rumble.
We name your noble brow
With that loud click of a trigger.

Your name is – oh, impossible! –
A kiss on the eyes – your name,
In this tender, chill, frozen time.
Your name is – a kiss in the snow.
At the core is a blue gulp of ice.
Sleep soundly – with your name.

And here is Irina. Easter week, 1920, weeks after the child died in the orphanage. Sixteen lines. She does not reach for a single metaphor of consolation, she just counts her own hands, and the last stanza is the sound of a mind refusing to complete a thought.

Two hands, lowered, touching lightly,
Resting on an infant's head!
Two small heads were once beside me —
One for every hand.

But from utter darkness – grueling —
Fierce — and not to be outdone! —
I, with both, the eldest pulling —
Couldn't save the younger one.

My two hands — to stroke — to pat
Locks of hair, so light and airy.
My two hands — and, just like that,
One became unnecessary.

Golden — on her skinny neck —
Like a dandelion crowned!
I have not learned to accept
That my baby's in the ground.
— Easter week 1920, trans. Andrey Kneller

One became unnecessary. She is describing her own hand as surplus equipment. That is a woman who has decided that the correct punishment for surviving is precision.

Three weeks later, May 1920, still in the same starving flat, she wrote this. Her name means of the sea. Read it as what she does with grief, which is refuse to be solid.

Some are made of stone; some made of clay –
But I am made of silver and sunlight!
Treason is – my trade; my name – Marina,
I am the mortal foam of the sea.

Some are made of clay; some made of flesh –
Theirs the coffin and the tombstone…
I was baptised in the sea's font – and
In flight – I am eternally breaking!

Through every heart, through every net,
My headstrong will must penetrate.
From me – do you see these wilful curls? –
No earthly salt, ever, will be gained.

Pounding hard on your granite knees,
With each wave – I shall resurrect!
Long live the foam – the playful foam –
The high, the rising foam of the sea!

Prague, 1923. Rodzevich has left her for a woman who is easier to be in a room with. This is the most famous poem she ever wrote, and it is twenty-four stanzas of a woman asking her ex-lover how the downgrade is going. It is savage, and it is also, underneath, a poem about a woman who knows she is unlivable-with and is furious about being correct.

What's it like with another woman –
Simpler? – a flash of the oar! –
Did the memory of me
Soon fade off-shore,

Like the beach of a floating island,
(In the sky – not in the sea!)
Souls, souls! You'll be sisters,
Not lovers – that's what you'll be!

What's life like with an ordinary
Woman? Now that you've dethroned
Your idol (renounced the throne).
Without the divinity?

What's your life like – occupation –
Shrivelled? Getting up – what's it like?
What do you pay, poor man,
For endless triviality – the price?

'I'm through with hysteria, convulsions!
I'll rent a place, have done!'
What's it like with a common
Woman, my chosen one?

More suitable and edible –
The food? Boring? – Don't complain…
What's it like with an imitation –
You who climbed the holy Mount? A strain?

What's your life like with a stranger,
A worldly soul. Well? – Is it love?
Like the god's whip, does shame
Not lash your head from above?

What's it like – your health –
How is it? How do you sing?
How do you cope, poor man,
With the festering sore of endless conscience?

What's life like with a marketable
Purchase? The price – terrible?
What's it like with crumbling plaster of Paris
After the finest Carrara marble?

(The Goddess made from stone –
And smashed to bits!)
What's your life like with one of millions,
You, who've known Lilith?

Does the marketable purchase meet
Your needs? Now magic's dead,
What's your life like with a mortal
Woman, neither using the sixth sense?

Well, swear, are you happy, then?
No? What's your life like in a pit
With no depth, my love? Harder,
Or just like mine with another man?
— 19 November 1924

Twenty-three stanzas of contempt and then she breaks in the last four words and admits she is not fine either. That turn is why the poem is immortal.

From Poem of the End, the long poem about the same man, in Elaine Feinstein's translation. She is on a bridge in Prague, being left, and her mind attacks the vocabulary instead of the man.

– What are we doing? – We are separating.
– That's a word that means nothing to me.
It's the most inhumanly senseless
of words: sep-arating. (Am I one of a hundred?)
It is simply a word of four syllables…
— trans. Elaine Feinstein

1922, and separately 1924, for Boris Pasternak, the one correspondent who could take her at full volume for seventeen years. First she rewrites the Orpheus myth so that Eurydice tells him to go away, which is the most she ever admitted about her own appetite for love at a distance. Then, two years later, twelve lines of the plainest thing she ever said to anyone.

Those abandoning their last ragged
Covering (no speech, no breath…!)
Oh, do you not exceed your powers,
Orpheus, in descending into Hades?

…As far as the Lethean upper reaches,
Beloved – it is peace I need,
Forgetfulness…for in the house of spirits,
Here – your ghost exists, yet is real –

I am dead…what can I tell you, but:
'You must depart now, and forget!'
I'll not be stirred! Not be drawn to you,
No hands, here! – No mouth, no lips

Meeting. Snake-bitten, immortality
Has put an end to female passion.
I have paid – remember my moans! –
For this final spaciousness.

No need for Orpheus to follow Eurydice,
Nor for a brother to disturb a sister.
In a world, where all
Are stooped and sweating,
I know – you alone
Mirror me.

In a world, where so many
Go wanting,
I know – you alone
Are my peer.

In a world, where all –
Is slime and spit,
I know: only you –
Are a match for me.

Meudon, June 1928. Paris, no money, no readers, no publisher. She writes a dialogue with the thing inside her that makes her keep going, and the thing inside her turns out to be completely merciless. This is the one to reread on a week when nothing works.

A weight, on my brow
The laurel of praise.
'I can't sing, anyhow,'
'But you will.' 'The way,

Sound (Transform me
To sawdust, at best!)
Like milk, you see is –
Gone from my breast.

Dry and empty,
At spring's height:
Feeling's dead twig.'
'– An ancient cry!

Hush, don't babble!'
'I'd be better at –
Pounding gravel!'
'Sing, even that!'

'Am I a bullfinch,
Day in day out
To sing?'
'And if you can't,
Even then, my bird, sing!
To spite the enemy!'

'If I can't make one
Line come to me?'
'When could, anyone?'
'It's torment' – 'Then suffer!'

'A field of dry chaff –
My throat.' – 'Then splutter:
That's sound too – cough!'

'It's a lion's task, not for
A woman' – 'A child's no doubt:
Though dismembered –
Orpheus still sang out!'

'Even at death?'
– 'Under a stone.'
'I can't sing a breath!'
– 'Then make that your moan.'

Then make that your moan. She is arguing with her own gift about whether she is allowed to stop, and the gift wins, and the terms are: keep making the sound even if the sound is only the noise of you failing.

1934, Paris, twelve years into exile. Everyone quotes the last line about the rowanberry, and they are right to, because the whole poem is a forty-line performance of not caring that collapses in the final two lines into a woman ambushed by a shrub.

Homesickness! That long
Exposure to misery!
It's all the same to me –
Where I'm utterly lonely

Or what stones I wander
Home by, with my sacks,
Home that's no more mine
Than a hospital, a barracks.

It's all the same to me, what
Faces I bristle among, a lion
Captive, what human crowd
– as it must do – thrusts me on,

Into myself, individual feeling,
From the pole, a Kamchatka bear;
Where I fail to fit (and won't try!),
Where I'm debased: I don't care.

I won't let the milky call
Of my native language tempt me.
It's all the same to me in what
Tongue they misunderstand me!

(By what readers swallowing
Newsprint tonnage, gossip's grime…)
They belong to the twentieth century
While I'm – before my time,

Petrified, like a log left
From an avenue, let fall.
They're all the same – it's all
The same – perhaps most of all –

What was native to me – of all.
All the signs and tokens, there,
All the dates – a hand erased:
The soul once born – somewhere.

My land cares so little for me
That even the keenest sleuth
Could traverse my whole – spirit!
And find no birthmark, in truth!

Houses alien, churches empty,
