All – one and the same – to me:
Yet if by the side of the road
A particular bush shows – rowanberry…

Also 1934, nine lines, on what it costs to write like this. She is describing the production of poetry as an unstoppable haemorrhage that the available containers are the wrong size for.

Cut veins: irrecoverably
Irreplaceably, life whips out.
Bring out basins and bowls!
Though the bowl's – too low,
The basin's – too shallow.

Over the lip, watch it flow,
To black earth, to feed the reeds.
Irreplaceably, verse will go,
Irrevocably, irrecoverably.

January 1940. Alya has been in prison five months, Efron four. Eight short lines. No abstractions, no politics, no grief-language at all — just bread and snow and the fact that both have stopped working.

He's gone – I don't eat.
Stale – the taste of bread.
Everything – like chalk.
Whatever I reach for.

…Mine was the bread,
And mine the snow.
The snow's not white.
The bread unpleasant.

And the last poem she ever wrote, 6 March 1941, five months before she died. She had read a poem by Arseny Tarkovsky — whom she had met in a prison queue — that begins I laid the table for six. She wrote back in verse to point out that he had miscounted, because she was still alive and nobody had set her a place. It is a poem about being the uninvited seventh at every table in the world, and she sent it to a man she barely knew, because there was no one else left to send it to.

Everyone repeats the first line,
Everyone passes on the word:
'I laid the table for six…'
Yet you forget one – the seventh.

Mirthless the six of you.
Down your faces – the rain streams…
How could you, at such a table,
Forget the seventh one – the seventh?

Mirthless your guests,
The crystal decanter idle.
Disconsolate – are they, disconsolate – am I.
The unnamed the most disconsolate of all.

Mirthless and mirthless again.
Ah, they fail to eat and drink!
– How could you forget their number?
How could you err in the sum?

How could you, dare you, not know
That the six (two brothers, a third –
You yourself, wife, father, mother)
Are seven – since I am here on earth?

You laid the table for six,
Though the sixth is not yet dead.
As a scarecrow among the living,
I long to be a ghost – with you,

(With them) …timid as a thief
Oh – not touching a soul! –
Like an out of place implement,
I sit, the uninvited seventh.

In prose she was, if anything, more alarming. A handful of lines that get quoted forever, and deserve to be. On what exile actually did to her career:

I went abroad in 1922, and my reader remained in Russia where my poems no longer penetrate… And thus, I am here without readers; in Russia, without books.

On being alive at all, in a letter to Rilke, 1926: "To me the strangest thing is to be alive and not know who you are." And in the same correspondence, telling a dying man how to haunt her: "You are what I'm going to dream about tonight, what will dream me tonight… I never await you; I always awake you." After he died she went on writing to him, and in January 1927 she asked him for the only thing she ever asked anyone for: "Beloved, come to me often in my dreams — no, not that. Live in my dreams."

And the one that summarises the whole file, from Poem of the End: "life is the place where it's forbidden to live." Also, because she was funny as well as doomed, on her own temperament: "Next time I will be born not on a planet, but on a comet."

The last two documents. On 26 August 1941 she wrote out an application by hand and handed it in to the Literary Fund in Chistopol. It is the shortest thing she ever wrote and it is in a museum now.

To the Council of the Literary Fund. Please hire me as a dishwasher in the Litfond canteen that is due to open.

M. Tsvetaeva

One of the four or five greatest poets in the Russian language, applying in writing to wash dishes in a canteen that did not exist yet, and being turned down. Five days later she left three notes. This is the one to her son.

Murlyga! Forgive me, but to go on would be worse. I am gravely ill, this is not me anymore. I love you passionately. Do understand that I could not live anymore. Tell Papa and Alya — if you see them — that I loved them to the last minute, and explain to them that I had reached a dead end.

Twenty-eight years earlier she had written, from a beach in the Crimea, that a time would come for her poems, and had told a passer-by not to be upset by her voice from underground. Both statements are now true. The rest of us just have to live with the interval.

Marie Curie

Warsaw & Paris, 1867–1934 · two Nobel Prizes, one dead husband she wrote letters to, and a nation that adored her right up until it found out she was a woman

aug 8, 2026▼

childhood & early life

She was born Maria Salomea Skłodowska in Warsaw on 7 November 1867, in a country that did not legally exist. Poland had been carved up by Russia, Prussia and Austria; her half of it was run by the Tsar. Both her parents were teachers, which in occupied Warsaw was a job with a police file attached. Her father taught physics and mathematics at a lyceum. Her mother ran one of the best girls' schools in the city.

She was the youngest of five. Her eldest sister Zofia died of typhus at fourteen. Her mother, worn out by tuberculosis and by burying a daughter, died at forty-two. Maria was nine. She wrote about it, sixty years later, in the flat voice of somebody who has decided never to make a scene about it again: "This catastrophe was the first great sorrow of my life and threw me into a profound depression." Then, in the next breath, the reason it mattered so much: "in me the natural love of the little girl for her mother was united with a passionate admiration." Her father responded by disappearing into his work. That is the household she was assembled in. Grief handled by working harder.

School was a surveillance operation. Russian teachers, Russian lessons, informers, and the knowledge that one Polish sentence in the wrong ear could destroy your whole family. She finished top of her class at fifteen, then collapsed and had to spend a year in the countryside recovering, which is the first entry in a lifelong pattern of working past the point of damage and then being physically removed from the field.

There was no university in Poland that would take a woman. So she made a deal with her sister Bronisława: Maria would work as a governess and pay for Bronya's medical degree in Paris, and Bronya would then pay for hers. She was seventeen when she left home for a stranger's estate in the country. She kept that bargain for five years. She taught the landowner's daughters by day and ran an illegal Polish reading class for the village children in the evening, which could have got her deported to Siberia, and studied physics on her own at night with whatever books she could find. She fell in love with the family's eldest son. His parents refused to let him marry a governess. He obeyed them.

She arrived in Paris in November 1891, aged twenty-four, and registered at the Sorbonne under the French version of her name. She lived in a garret with almost no coal. The water in her washbasin froze at night and she piled every item of clothing she owned onto the bed. She once fainted from hunger in a lecture. She graduated first in her class in physics, and second in mathematics. She never once described this period as anything but the happiest of her life.

what she did & why she's remembered

Henri Becquerel had noticed that uranium salts fogged a photographic plate through black paper. Everyone thought it was a curiosity. She picked it as a thesis topic because nobody else wanted it, which meant there was almost no literature to read, which she considered an advantage.

Her first real move was not a discovery, it was a decision about method. Instead of photographic plates she measured the rays electrically, using an instrument built around Pierre's piezoelectric quartz, and got numbers instead of smudges. The numbers immediately told her something nobody had said out loud: the effect was proportional to how much uranium was present, and nothing else. Not the compound, not the temperature, not the light. It was a property of the atom itself. In 1898 that was close to heresy; the atom was supposed to be indivisible and inert.

Then she measured pitchblende ore and it came out three or four times more active than its uranium content allowed. Most people would have hunted for the error. She checked it carefully, believed the number, and drew the only conclusion available: there was something else in there. Two something-elses, in fact. In July 1898 they announced polonium, which she named after the country that did not exist. In December 1898, radium.

Announcing it was the easy part. Chemistry demanded a pure sample and an atomic weight, and radium turned out to be present at something like one part in ten million. So she spent nearly four years in a leaking, unheated shed with a glass roof, a former dissecting room the school had no other use for, processing tons of Bohemian pitchblende residue by hand. Boiling. Stirring vats with an iron rod nearly as tall as she was. Fractional crystallisation, over and over. In 1902 she had one decigramme of pure radium chloride and an atomic weight. She had invented a new science and proved that atoms come apart.

She and Pierre took no patent. They published the extraction method in full, free, immediately, and watched an industry worth $70,000 a gramme grow out of it while they could not afford a laboratory. She never wavered on that. Nobel Prize in Physics 1903, shared, and only after Pierre told the committee he would not accept it without her. Nobel Prize in Chemistry 1911, alone. First woman to win one, first person to win two, still the only person to win in two different sciences.

And then in 1914, at forty-six, she stopped doing physics and became a logistics operation. She personally designed and equipped twenty mobile X-ray units, the petites Curies, learned to drive so she could take them to the front herself, and set up radiology in hundreds of field hospitals so surgeons could see where the shrapnel actually was. When the army kept reassigning her trained male operators, she solved it by opening a school and training a hundred and fifty women instead. Her seventeen-year-old daughter Irène taught on the course.

family life

Father: Władysław Skłodowski, physics teacher, wrote little poems for family occasions, read the family Polish literature aloud on Saturday nights, lost most of the family money in a bad investment and never forgave himself. He died in 1902, weeks before she finished the radium work. Mother: Bronisława, dead when Marie was nine. One brother, Józef, who became a Warsaw physician. Three sisters, one of whom died before Marie was ten, and Bronya, the one she bought a medical degree for and who came to Stockholm in 1911 to hold her up.

Her own two daughters were raised in a house that had zero interest in normal schooling. She thought French lycées kept children indoors too long, so she and a group of Sorbonne friends simply built their own co-op school and each taught their own subject to all the children. She insisted on gymnastics, swimming, canoeing, long walks. She wrote, plainly, of combining a scientific career with children: "Well, it has not been easy; it required a great deal of decision and of self-sacrifice."

Irène, born 1897, slow and stubborn and exactly like her father, won the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 1935 with her husband Frédéric Joliot for producing artificial radioactivity. Her mother died the year before the announcement, which is the cruellest possible timing. Ève, born 1904, was the musical one who refused to be a scientist, wrote the 1937 biography Madame Curie that made her mother a myth, became a war correspondent, and lived to a hundred and two. It is a family with five Nobel Prizes in it and a granddaughter still working on radiation biology.

romantic life

She met Pierre Curie in the spring of 1894, in a friend's flat. He was thirty-five, already a serious physicist, and standing in a window. She noticed his hands and his slowness. He was, by his own account, a man who had assumed he would never marry, because at twenty-two he had written in his diary that women drag men back from the work: "in the name of life and of nature they seek to lead us back." He had been waiting twelve years for the exception.

He proposed almost immediately. She said no, or rather she said she could not abandon Poland, and left for the summer. He wrote to her all through it. They married in July 1895 with no white dress, no church, and no ring; she wore a navy suit she could work in afterwards. A relative gave them money and they bought two bicycles with it, and went on honeymoon in the general direction of wherever the road went.

Eleven years. She said almost nothing about them for the rest of her life except that the shed was where they were happiest.

Then, in 1910, four years widowed, she fell in love with Paul Langevin. He was five years younger, a brilliant physicist, Pierre's former student, and unhappily married with four children. He rented a flat near her lab. His wife hired someone to break in and steal the letters, and in November 1911 a Paris paper printed them under the headline A Story of Love: Madame Curie and Professor Langevin. Overnight the national heroine became "the foreign Jewish home-wrecker", which was doubly stupid because she was neither. A mob gathered outside the house in Sceaux while fourteen-year-old Irène and seven-year-old Ève were inside. Five duels were fought over it. She had to move her children out.

In the middle of that, the Nobel committee awarded her the chemistry prize, then got nervous and asked her to stay home. She went. Her reply to them is in the works section below and it is the most controlled piece of anger in the file. Langevin, for his part, went back to his wife. Decades later his grandson married her granddaughter, which is a strange kind of ending.

Note what is missing from her own autobiography, written twelve years later: all of it. Not a syllable. She would write in agonising detail about a shed, and would not give the press one sentence.

life until death

On 19 April 1906, Pierre spent the day arguing pleasantly with colleagues about teaching methods. Walking out into the rue Dauphine in the rain, he stepped behind a cab, slipped, and went under the wheels of a horse-drawn wagon loaded with military uniforms. A rear wheel crushed his skull. He was forty-six. She wrote one sentence about the room he did not come back to, and it is the whole of her grief compressed into an object: "In the study room to which he was never to return, the water buttercups he had brought from the country were still fresh."

She refused the state funeral, refused the black cloth over the coffin, and put flowers and a small photograph of herself inside it, the one he used to call "the good little student". Then she started a private journal addressed directly to him, kept it for about a year, and went back to work. The Sorbonne gave her his chair, the first woman ever to hold one there. Her first lecture began at the exact sentence in the syllabus where Pierre's last one had stopped. She did not mention him. The hall was packed with people who had come to watch a widow cry, and she gave them a physics lecture.

The rest is a long, slow poisoning that she saw coming and kept working through anyway. She carried radium tubes in her coat pockets and kept them in a desk drawer because she liked the glow. She writes, in a single dry parenthesis about handling the material, "several times I have felt a discomfort which I consider a result of this cause." Cataracts blinded her by degrees; she had four operations and hid it, writing her lecture notes in enormous letters and having Ève read to her. Her fingertips were scarred and numb. Her blood count fell for years.

She died on 4 July 1934, aged sixty-six, at a sanatorium at Sancellemoz in the Alps, of aplastic anaemia. Her bone marrow had simply stopped. The doctor's phrasing was that the illness was caused by an accumulation of radiation. She had spent thirty-five years in the beam of the thing she found. She was buried at Sceaux, on top of Pierre. In 1995 they were both moved to the Panthéon, and she became the first woman put there on her own merits.

Her laboratory notebooks from the 1890s are still so radioactive that they are stored in lead-lined boxes at the Bibliothèque nationale, and you sign a liability waiver to read them. Radium has a half-life of about 1,600 years. Her handwriting will still be dangerous in the year 3500. There is no better monument, and she would have hated it being called one.

her works

Most of what follows is from Pierre Curie (Macmillan, 1923), her biography of her husband with her own Autobiographical Notes attached, translated by Charlotte and Vernon Kellogg. It is out of copyright and free on Project Gutenberg. The mourning journal is the exception: it has never been published in full, and the passages below are the ones Ève Curie released in 1937. Read the shed first and the journal last.

She opens her autobiography by refusing to write an autobiography. This is the first paragraph, and it is a woman setting the terms of her own exposure before anyone else can.

I have been asked by my American friends to write the story of my life. At first, the idea seemed alien to me, but I yielded to persuasion. However, I could not conceive my biography as a complete expression of personal feelings or a detailed description of all incidents I would remember. Many of our feelings change with the years, and, when faded away, may seem altogether strange; incidents lose their momentary interest and may be remembered as if they have occurred to some other person. But there may be in a life some general direction, some continuous thread, due to a few dominant ideas and a few strong feelings, that explain the life and are characteristic of a human personality. Of my life, which has not been easy on the whole, I have described the general course and the essential features.

"Of my life, which has not been easy on the whole." One clause. Dead mother, dead sister, occupied country, dead husband, public destruction, radiation poisoning. Not easy on the whole.

The garret years in Paris, where she was cold and hungry and has to be talked into admitting it was hard, because she is too busy telling you it was the best time of her life.

The room I lived in was in a garret, very cold in winter, for it was insufficiently heated by a small stove which often lacked coal. During a particularly rigorous winter, it was not unusual for the water to freeze in the basin in the night; to be able to sleep I was obliged to pile all my clothes on the bedcovers. In the same room I prepared my meals with the aid of an alcohol lamp and a few kitchen utensils. These meals were often reduced to bread with a cup of chocolate, eggs or fruit. I had no help in housekeeping and I myself carried the little coal I used up the six flights.

This life, painful from certain points of view, had, for all that, a real charm for me. It gave me a very precious sense of liberty and independence. Unknown in Paris, I was lost in the great city, but the feeling of living there alone, taking care of myself without any aid, did not at all depress me. If sometimes I felt lonesome, my usual state of mind was one of calm and great moral satisfaction.

Pierre's proposal letter, 1894. It is not a romantic letter. It is a letter that argues its own case, refuses to promise anything it cannot deliver, and then says the most romantic thing in the file anyway.

We have promised each other (is it not true?) to have, the one for the other, at least a great affection. Provided that you do not change your mind! For there are no promises which hold; these are things that do not admit of compulsion.

It would, nevertheless, be a beautiful thing in which I hardly dare believe, to pass through life together hypnotized in our dreams: your dream for your country; our dream for humanity; our dream for science. Of all these dreams, I believe the last, alone, is legitimate. …

I strongly advise you to return to Paris in October. I shall be very unhappy if you do not come this year, but it is not my friend's selfishness that makes me ask you to return. I ask it because I believe you will work better here and that you can accomplish here something more substantial and more useful.

"There are no promises which hold." He proposes by telling her he cannot bind her, and then closes by arguing she should come back for her own career, not for him. She kept it, quoted it in a book, and married the man.

Pierre on compromise, from another letter she chose to print. He is describing the arithmetic of survival.

We are all the slaves of our affections, slaves of the prejudices of those we love. Besides, we must make a living, and this forces us to become a wheel in the machine. The most painful are the concessions we are forced to make to the prejudices of the society in which we live. We must make more or fewer concessions according as we feel ourselves feebler or stronger. If one does not make enough concessions he is crushed; if he makes too many he is ignoble and despises himself.

Now the shed. Four years, seven tons of ore, no ventilation, no funding, no help. She calls it the happiest time of her life and means it, and then she gives you the one image everyone remembers.

The School of Physics could give us no suitable premises, but for lack of anything better, the Director permitted us to use an abandoned shed which had been in service as a dissecting room of the School of Medicine. Its glass roof did not afford complete shelter against rain; the heat was suffocating in summer, and the bitter cold of winter was only a little lessened by the iron stove, except in its immediate vicinity. …

Yet it was in this miserable old shed that we passed the best and happiest years of our life, devoting our entire days to our work. Often I had to prepare our lunch in the shed, so as not to interrupt some particularly important operation. Sometimes I had to spend a whole day mixing a boiling mass with a heavy iron rod nearly as large as myself. I would be broken with fatigue at the day's end. … But I shall never be able to express the joy of the untroubled quietness of this atmosphere of research and the excitement of actual progress with the confident hope of still better results.

One of our joys was to go into our workroom at night; we then perceived on all sides the feebly luminous silhouettes of the bottles or capsules containing our products. It was really a lovely sight and one always new to us. The glowing tubes looked like faint, fairy lights.

They went in at night to look at the glow. The glow was killing them. She wrote that sentence in 1923, knowing exactly what it had cost her, and did not take it out.

On fame arriving, which she experienced not as a reward but as an intrusion with the force of a natural disaster.

The fatigue resulting from the effort exceeding our forces, imposed by the unsatisfactory conditions of our labor, was augmented by the invasion of publicity. The overturn of our voluntary isolation was a cause of real suffering for us and had all the effect of disaster. … Of course, people who contribute to that kind of trouble generally mean it kindly. It is only that they do not realize the conditions of the problem.

On losing Pierre, in the register she used in public. Nineteen words at the end of the first paragraph do all the work.

In 1906 just as we were definitely giving up the old shed laboratory where we had been so happy, there came the dreadful catastrophe which took my husband away from me and left me alone to bring up our children and, at the same time, to continue our work of research.

It is impossible for me to express the profoundness and importance of the crisis brought into my life by the loss of the one who had been my closest companion and best friend. Crushed by the blow, I did not feel able to face the future. I could not forget, however, what my husband used sometimes to say, that, even deprived of him, I ought to continue my work.

And now the register she used in private. This is from the mourning journal, written to a dead man in the second person, months after the funeral. She had just been given his chair at the Sorbonne.

14 May 1906

My little Pierre, I want to tell you that the laburnum is in flower, the wisteria, the hawthorn and the iris are beginning — you would have loved all that.

I want to tell you, too, that I have been named to your chair, and that there have been some imbeciles to congratulate me on it.

And still, I live in constant desolation, and I don't know what I will become and how I will bear the task that remains. At times, it seems to me that my pain is wearing off, but immediately it is reborn, tenacious and powerful.

I want to tell you that I no longer love the sun or the flowers. The sight of them makes me suffer. I feel better on dark days like the day of your death, and if I have not learned to hate fine weather it is because my children have need of it.

On Sunday morning, I went to my Pierre's grave. I will have a vault made, and the coffin will have to be moved.

I work in the laboratory all day, it's all I can do; I feel better there than anywhere else. I cannot conceive of anything which would give me real personal happiness, except perhaps scientific work — and not even that, because, if successful, I would be distressed that you didn't know about it.

I found a little photograph of you near the scales. You were smiling so beautifully. I can't look at it without sobbing. I'll never see that smile again.

"There have been some imbeciles to congratulate me on it." She is being handed the most prestigious appointment ever given to a woman in France and she experiences it as an insult, because it is his. Elsewhere in the journal: "You would have been happy to see me as a professor at the Sorbonne, and I myself would have so willingly done it for you. But to do it in your place, my Pierre, could one dream of a thing more cruel."

And the burial, from the same journal.

We put you into the coffin Saturday morning, and I held your head up for this move. We kissed your cold face for the last time. Then a few periwinkles from the garden on the coffin and the little picture of me that you called "the good little student" and that you loved. … Your coffin was closed and I could see you no more. I didn't allow them to cover it with the horrible black cloth. I covered it with flowers and I sat beside it. …

They filled the grave and put sheaves of flowers on it. Everything is over, Pierre is sleeping his last sleep beneath the earth; it is the end of everything, everything, everything.

Five years later, the press had her letters and the Nobel committee wanted her to stay away from Stockholm until her name was cleared. Svante Arrhenius asked her to cable a refusal. She sent this instead, and then got on the train.

The prize has been awarded for the discovery of radium and polonium. I believe that there is no connection between my scientific work and the facts of private life. … I cannot accept the idea in principle that the appreciation of the value of scientific work should be influenced by libel and slander concerning private life.

Einstein, who had met her at Solvay three weeks earlier, wrote to her from Prague on 23 November 1911, in the middle of the worst of it. He apologises for having nothing useful to say and then says the useful thing.

Highly esteemed Mrs. Curie,

Do not laugh at me for writing you without having anything sensible to say. But I am so enraged by the base manner in which the public is presently daring to concern itself with you that I absolutely must give vent to this feeling. However, I am convinced that you consistently despise this rabble, whether it obsequiously lavishes respect on you or whether it attempts to satiate its lust for sensationalism!

I am impelled to tell you how much I have come to admire your intellect, your drive, and your honesty, and that I consider myself lucky to have made your personal acquaintance in Brussels. Anyone who does not number among these reptiles is certainly happy, now as before, that we have such personages among us as you, and Langevin too, real people with whom one feels privileged to be in contact. If the rabble continues to occupy itself with you, then simply don't read that hogwash, but rather leave it to the reptile for whom it has been fabricated.

With most amicable regards to you, Langevin, and Perrin, yours very truly,
A. Einstein

On the war, in which she drove X-ray vans to the front and looked at what was in them. She almost never expressed a political opinion in print. Here is the exception.

I can never forget the terrible impression of all that destruction of human life and health. To hate the very idea of war, it ought to be sufficient to see once what I have seen so many times, all through those years: men and boys brought to the advanced ambulance in a mixture of mud and blood, many of them dying of their injuries, many others recovering but slowly through months of pain and suffering.

And, in a passage about the same period, the single most understated sentence she ever wrote about her own body: "Since the handling of radium is far from being free of danger (several times I have felt a discomfort which I consider a result of this cause), measures were taken to prevent harmful effects of the rays on the persons preparing emanation." Measures for everyone else. Not for her.

On refusing to patent radium, which made other people rich and left her begging for lab funding for the rest of her life. She is fully aware of what it cost her children and she does not budge.

My husband, as well as myself, always refused to draw from our discovery any material profit. We have published, since the beginning, without any reserve, the process that we used to prepare the radium. We took out no patent and we did not reserve any advantage in any industrial exploitation. …

So it is a fortune which we have sacrificed in renouncing the exploitation of our discovery, a fortune that could, after us, have gone to our children. … Yet, I still believe that we have done right.

Humanity, surely, needs practical men who make the best of their work for the sake of their own interests, without forgetting the general interest. But it also needs dreamers, for whom the unselfish following of a purpose is so imperative that it becomes impossible for them to devote much attention to their own material benefit. No doubt it could be said that these idealists do not deserve riches since they do not have the desire for them. It seems, however, that a society well organized ought to assure to these workers the means for efficient labor.

On what research actually feels like from the inside, which is the passage to reread on a bad week.

The life of a great scientist in his laboratory is not, as many may think, a peaceful idyll. More often it is a bitter battle with things, with one's surroundings, and above all with oneself. A great discovery does not leap completely achieved from the brain of the scientist, as Minerva sprang, all panoplied, from the head of Jupiter; it is the fruit of accumulated preliminary work. Between the days of fecund productivity are inserted days of uncertainty when nothing seems to succeed, and when even matter itself seems hostile; and it is then that one must hold out against discouragement.

Thus without ever forsaking his inexhaustible patience, Pierre Curie used sometimes to say to me: "It is nevertheless hard, this life that we have chosen."

Two Nobel Prizes and she is telling you that most days nothing works and matter itself appears to be against you. Also: "I was taught that the way of progress is neither swift nor easy." Taught. Not realised, not discovered. Somebody sat her down.

Last, the small ugly coincidence at the back of the book. Among the tributes she collected for Pierre's memorial pages, printed under his name, is one from a young physicist who had been his student. Five years after she typeset it, she was in love with him and the whole country knew.

… The hours when one could meet him and in which one loved to talk about his science and in which one thought with him, return each day to recall his memory, to bring back his kindly and thoughtful face, his luminous eyes and his beautiful, expressive head modeled by twenty-five years passed in the laboratory, and by a life of unremittent work and complete simplicity. … Probably my finest memories of my school years are those of moments passed there standing before the blackboard where he took pleasure in talking with us, in awakening in us fruitful ideas. … His live and contagious curiosity, the fullness and surety of his information made him an admirable awakener of spirits.
— Paul Langevin, in Pierre Curie, collected and published by Marie Curie

She left it in. 1923, twelve years after the scandal, she is still assembling her husband's memorial and she prints her former lover's eulogy for him in full, without comment, and says nothing about any of it anywhere in the volume. That is the whole woman in one editorial decision.

Murasaki Shikibu

Heian Japan, c. 973–c. 1014 · lady-in-waiting, widow, and the woman who wrote the world's first novel in a language men thought was beneath them

aug 7, 2026▼

childhood & early life

Nobody knows her name. That is the first thing to say about the most important writer in Japanese literature. "Murasaki" is the violet-purple of her own heroine, or of a poem about purple grass; "Shikibu" is her father's office, the Bureau of Rites. Heian women of good family did not let their personal names be written down. So the woman who invented the psychological novel is filed under a nickname and a job title belonging to her dad.

She was born in Kyoto around 973, into a branch of the Fujiwara clan that had the name and none of the power. Her father, Fujiwara no Tametoki, was a genuine scholar of Chinese who spent his life scrambling for mid-level provincial posts. Her mother died when she was small. She grew up in a house of books, and the story she tells about that house is one of the most quietly furious things she ever wrote down. Her older brother was being drilled in the Chinese histories, the serious male curriculum. She was sitting beside him, not being taught, and learning it faster than he did. Her father's reaction was not to educate her. It was to sigh: "Father, who was devoted to study, regretted that I had not been a son."

In 996 Tametoki was made governor of Echizen, a cold province on the Sea of Japan, and she went with him, which noblewomen almost never did; the journey took days. She came back to the capital around 998 to be married off. She was in her mid-twenties, which by Heian standards was late enough for everyone to have opinions about it.

what she did & why she's remembered

She wrote The Tale of Genji. Fifty-four chapters, four hundred-odd characters, a genealogy that runs eighty pages on its own, following one prince from birth to death and then continuing past him into the lives of the people he damaged. It was written around 1002 to 1010, roughly six hundred years before Shakespeare and about seven hundred before anything in Europe that behaves like a novel. And the thing that makes it a novel rather than a tale is that nothing supernatural is doing the work. People are ruined by their own temperaments. They want the wrong person, they misread a letter, they age, they get bored, they die offstage, and the book keeps going.

Here is the part that should be underlined. Serious writing in her world was done in Chinese, by men. Japanese script, kana, was the women's hand, considered light, private, unimportant. She wrote the greatest work in her language in the medium nobody was guarding. The prestige form of her era produced almost nothing anyone reads for pleasure now. The despised form produced Genji. That is not an accident; it happened because nobody was supervising it.

The book made her famous enough to be hired. Around 1005 the regent Fujiwara no Michinaga, the most powerful man in Japan, brought her into the household of his teenage daughter Shoshi, the young empress. Her actual job was to be an ornament of the empress's court, and, in secret, her tutor. Shoshi wanted to read the Chinese poet Bai Juyi. Women were not supposed to. So they did it before dawn, with the doors shut, and when Michinaga found out he did not stop it, he sent more books.

The other thing she left is the diary she kept from 1007 to 1010, and it is a strange document: half court reportage in obsessive fabric detail, half a cold-eyed appraisal of everyone around her, half a private confession of loneliness that she seems to have half wanted someone to read. It contains the single best eyewitness account of an eleventh-century childbirth, and the most famous piece of literary bitchiness in Japanese, and a woman sitting alone at night with a koto she cannot bring herself to play properly.

family life

Father: Tametoki, the scholar who wished she were a boy and then, decades later, resigned his post and rushed back to the capital in 1014, which is one of the main reasons historians think that is the year she died. Mother: a daughter of Fujiwara no Tamenobu, dead early, name unrecorded. An older brother, Nobunori, the slow one at the Chinese lessons. A sister who died young.

Her own daughter was born in 999, called Kenshi, later known at court as Daini no Sanmi. She grew up to be a good poet in her own right, served as wet nurse to an emperor, and lived to about eighty, which after a mother who died around forty is its own kind of quiet victory. When Fujiwara no Teika assembled the Hundred Poets centuries later, he put Murasaki at number 57 and her daughter at number 58. Mother and daughter, back to back, in the anthology every literate Japanese person still learns as a card game. Both poems are in the works section below. Read them in order.

romantic life

She married Fujiwara no Nobutaka, a distant relative and a friend of her father's, around 998. He was roughly her father's age, mid-forties to her mid-twenties, had already outlived several wives, had children by at least three other women, and had a reputation as a bit of a peacock. On the face of it, a resigned arrangement.

It seems to have been happy. That is the surprise. Their surviving poem exchanges are teasing and warm, and the grief in the diary never entirely closes over. In 1001, two or three years in, an epidemic went through the capital and he died in it. She was widowed at about twenty-eight with a two-year-old.

And then she went home and wrote Genji. The most persuasive account of how the book began is that it was written by a widow in the provinces with nothing else to do with her nights. In the diary she keeps calling this her "extraordinary sorrow" and refuses to elaborate. She never remarried. Michinaga, her patron, seems to have tried something; there is a night in the diary where someone stands outside her door knocking, and the poem he sends the next morning is unambiguous, and the poem she sends back is a door closing. Later ages could not stand it and invented affairs for her. There is no evidence for any of them. As one early note puts it, no shade of scandal ever touched her name, which in that court is nearly a supernatural achievement.

life until death

The last dated entry in the diary is early 1010. She was still at court in 1012 or so, then she and the empress withdrew toward the Lake Biwa region, and then the record simply stops. Most scholars put her death at 1014, aged around forty-one, on the strength of her father abandoning his governorship and coming home. A minority argue she was still alive in 1025. Nobody knows where she is buried, though Kyoto will happily show you a grave. Nobody knows what she looked like. Nobody knows her name.

What happened afterwards is the good part. Eleven years after she probably died, a thirteen-year-old girl in the provinces, the daughter of a minor official, was so obsessed with the few chapters of Genji she had managed to get hold of that she prayed for the rest of it. Her aunt gave her fifty volumes in a case. The girl grew up, got old, and wrote it down in what we now call the Sarashina Diary, and it is the earliest fan letter in world literature:

How happy I was when I came home carrying these books in a bag! Until then I had only read a volume here and there, and was dissatisfied because I could not understand the story. Now I could be absorbed in these stories, taking them out one by one, shutting myself in behind the kichō. To be a Queen were nothing compared to this!

All day and all night, as late as I could keep my eyes open, I did nothing but look at the books, setting a lamp close beside me. Soon I learnt by heart all the names in the books, and I thought that a great thing. … I did not tell any one about this, nor had I any mind to learn [the sutra], but continued to bathe in the romances. Although I was still ugly and undeveloped, the time would come when I should be beautiful beyond compare, with long, long hair. I should be like the Lady Yugao loved by the Shining Prince Genji … Oh! I was like a crazy girl.

A thousand years later the book has never been out of print, has been continuously illustrated, staged, filmed, turned into manga, and retranslated roughly once a generation, and the woman on the 2024 Japanese drama about her court is played by a movie star. Her father wanted a son. He got the founder of the novel.

her works

Everything below is out of copyright. The diary is Annie Shepley Omori and Kochi Doi's 1920 translation; the Genji excerpts are Suematsu Kenchō's 1882 version, the first ever into English; the two Hundred Poets verses are Clay MacCauley, 1917. Start with the diary, because that is where she stops being a monument and starts being a person in a room.

First, how she writes an ordinary morning. Michinaga, the most powerful man in the country, ambushes her before she has done her face, and demands a poem on a flower. She writes one about being passed over.

I can see the garden from my room beside the entrance to the gallery. The air is misty, the dew is still on the leaves. The Lord Prime Minister is walking there; he orders his men to cleanse the brook. He breaks off a stalk of omenaishi [flower maiden] which is in full bloom by the south end of the bridge. He peeps in over my screen! His noble appearance embarrasses us, and I am ashamed of my morning [not yet painted and powdered] face. He says, "Your poem on this! If you delay so much the fun is gone!" and I seize the chance to run away to the writing-box, hiding my face—

Flower-maiden in bloom—
Even more beautiful for the bright dew,
Which is partial, and never favors me.

"So prompt!" said he, smiling, and ordered a writing-box to be brought [for himself]. His answer:

The silver dew is never partial.
From her heart
The flower-maiden's beauty.

He tells her the dew is fair and beauty comes from within, which is the smoothest possible way of saying you did that to yourself. She writes down both poems, including his better one. She always does.

Now the most famous small thing in the diary. She is watching birds on the pond and does the thing she does in every mode: refuses the pretty reading.

I wish I could be more adaptable and live more gaily in the present world—had I not an extraordinary sorrow—but whenever I hear delightful or interesting things my yearning for a religious life grows stronger. I become melancholy and lament. I try to forget, for sorrow is vain. Am I too sinful? So I was musing one morning when I saw waterfowl playing heedlessly in the pond.

Waterfowl floating on the water—
They seem so gay,
But in truth
It is not gay to live anxiously seeking means of existence.

I sympathized with them who outwardly have no other thought but amusement, yet in reality are seeking a livelihood in great anxiety.

She is describing herself and every other woman at that court. Serene on the surface, paddling for her life underneath, employed entirely at the pleasure of a man who could reassign her tomorrow.

The Chinese passage. A rival lady-in-waiting nicknamed her "The Japanese Chronicle Lady" to spread the idea that she was showing off her learning, and this is her account of it. Watch how much of her life gets organised around not being caught knowing things.

Once the King was listening to a reading of my Genji-monogatari, and said, "She is gifted, she must have read the Chronicle of Japan." This lady heard of it, and unreflectingly spread abroad among the courtiers the idea that I am very proud of my learning, giving me the name of "The Japanese Chronicle lady"—it is laughable, indeed! I am reserved even before the maids of my own house; how then should I show my learning in Court?

When my elder brother Shikibu-no-Jo was a boy he was taught to read "Chinese Historical Records." I listened, sitting beside him, and learned wonderfully fast, though he was sometimes slow and forgot. Father, who was devoted to study, regretted that I had not been a son, but I heard people saying that it is not beautiful even for a man to be proud of his learning, and after that I did not write so much as the figure one in Chinese. I grew clumsy with my [writing] brush. For a long time I did not care for the books I had already read. Thus I was ashamed to think how others would hate me on hearing what Lady Saémon said, and I assumed an air of not being able to read the characters written on the Royal screen.

But the Queen made me read [to her] the poetical works of Li T'ai Po, and as she wished to learn them I have been teaching her since the Summer of two years ago the second and third volumes of that collection very secretly when none were present. Her Majesty and I tried to conceal it, but His Majesty the King and the Lord Prime Minister finding it out, the latter presented to the Queen many poetical books which he had had copied. I think that bitter Saémon does not know it yet. If she did, how she would criticize me!

She trained herself to be unable to write the character for "one." She stood in front of a screen with Chinese on it and performed illiteracy. And she was teaching the empress of Japan Tang poetry in the dark. That is not modesty, it is tradecraft.

Her review of the competition. Izumi Shikibu was the celebrated poet of the age; Sei Shōnagon, author of the Pillow Book, had served the rival empress. This is a thousand years old and still the most quoted piece of shade in the language.

LADY IZUMI SHIKIBU corresponds charmingly, but her behaviour is improper indeed. She writes with grace and ease and with a flashing wit. There is fragrance even in her smallest words. Her poems are attractive, but they are only improvisations which drop from her mouth spontaneously. Every one of them has some interesting point, and she is acquainted with ancient literature also, but she is not like a true artist who is filled with the genuine spirit of poetry. Yet I think even she cannot presume to pass judgment on the poems of others.

… Those who compose poems whose loins are all but broken, yet who are infinitely self-exalted and vain, deserve our contempt and pity.

LADY SEISHONAGON. A very proud person. She values herself highly, and scatters her Chinese writings all about. Yet should we study her closely, we should find that she is still imperfect. She tries to be exceptional, but naturally persons of that sort give offence. She is piling up trouble for her future. One who is too richly gifted, who indulges too much in emotion, even when she ought to be reserved, and cannot turn aside from anything she is interested in, in spite of herself will lose self-control. How can such a vain and reckless person end her days happily!

"She scatters her Chinese writings all about." From the woman secretly teaching Chinese poetry at dawn. The charge is not that Sei knows Chinese. It is that she lets people see her know it.

Then, without warning, the diary turns inward and she writes the loneliest paragraphs in Heian literature.

Having no excellence within myself, I have passed my days without making any special impression on any one. Especially the fact that I have no man who will look out for my future makes me comfortless. I do not wish to bury myself in dreariness. Is it because of my worldly mind that I feel lonely? On moonlight nights in autumn, when I am hopelessly sad, I often go out on the balcony and gaze dreamily at the moon. It makes me think of days gone by. People say that it is dangerous to look at the moon in solitude, but something impels me, and sitting a little withdrawn I muse there. In the wind-cooled evening I play on the koto, though others may not care to hear it. I fear that my playing betrays the sorrow which becomes more intense, and I become disgusted with myself—so foolish and miserable am I.

My room is ugly, blackened by smoke. I play on a thirteen or six-stringed koto, but I neglect to take away the bridges even in rainy weather, and I lean it up against the wall between the cabinet and the door jamb. On either side of the koto stands a lute. A pair of big bookcases have in them all the books they can hold. In one of them are placed old poems and romances. They are the homes of worms which come frightening us when we turn the pages, so none ever wish to read them. As to the other cabinet, since the person who placed his own books [there] no hand has touched it.

"Since the person who placed his own books there, no hand has touched it." Her husband, dead eight years, is in that sentence and is never named. She wrote a book about a man's whole erotic career and could not write the words my husband's shelf.

Two passages on being read by other people. The first is the gap between her reputation and her face; the second is her rule for conversation, which is the single most INFJ sentence written before the twentieth century.

Pretty and coy, shrinking from sight, unsociable, proud, fond of romance, vain and poetic, looking down upon others with a jealous eye—such is the opinion of those who do not know me, but after seeing me they say, "You are wonderfully gentle to meet with; I cannot identify you with that imagined one."

I see that I have been slighted, hated, and looked down upon as an old gossip, and I must bear it, for it is my destiny to be solitary. The Queen said once, "You were ever mindful not to show your soul, but I have become more intimate with you than others."

… after all, when I was among the ladies of the Court I did not say what I wanted to say either, for it is useless to talk with those who do not understand one and troublesome to talk with those who criticize from a feeling of superiority. Especially one-sided persons are troublesome. Few are accomplished in many arts and most cling narrowly to their own opinion.

… Moreover, in this muddy world it is best to let alone the persons who hate us. If we compare one who tries to excel in hatred saying extraordinary words and watching [their effect] ill-humouredly face to face, with one who coldly hides her heart with a tranquil manner, we can see which is superior.

The night at the door. Michinaga knocks; she lies still in the dark and does not answer; in the morning the poem arrives. A kuina, water-rail, is a bird famous for a knocking call, and it is also the standard poetic euphemism for a man at a woman's door at night. Her reply uses his own bird against him.

One night I slept in a room near the corridor. Some one came knocking at the door. I was afraid and passed the night without making a sound. The next morning the following poem was sent me [from the Prime Minister]:

All the night through, knocking louder than a water-rail,
I stood in vain at the door of hinoki wood
weary and lamenting.

I wrote back:

A cause of deep regret, indeed,
Had the door opened at the knocking of the water-rail!

No. Politely, in metre, to the most powerful man in Japan, with the deniability of a joke. And then she copied the exchange into her own diary so that it would survive.

Court life in one scene. A drunken banquet, ministers making holes in the screens, and a novelist trying to physically hide from her own reputation.

The Minister of the Right came dancing wildly and made a hole in the kichō behind which ladies were sitting. They laughed, saying, "He has long passed the age for that." He did not notice, but made a great many unbecoming jokes, taking away ladies' fans. … The First Officer of the Light Bodyguard said, "I think Lady Murasaki must be somewhere here!" I listened, thinking, "How can she be here in a place where there is no such graceful person as Prince Genji?" … It was an awful night of carousal, so after the ceremony I signalled to Lady Saisho and we hid ourselves, but there came noisily the Prime Minister's sons and Lieutenant-General Saisho, so, although we two had remained hidden behind the screen, even this was taken away and we were captives. "Compose a poem each, and you shall be excused," said the Lord Prime Minister.

A man shouts her character's name across a party as a pickup line. She thinks a witheringly good comeback and says nothing. Then she is dragged out from behind a screen and made to perform a poem on demand at midnight. Freelancing has not changed.

Two on friendship, which she took more seriously than romance. She is at her parents' house, missing Lady Dainagon, with whom she shared a room at court; the reply came back the same day.

I long for Lady Dainagon with whom I spent every night before the Queen, when we told each other all our heart's secrets—is it also my worldly heart that longs for a companion other than Buddha?

Like two wild ducks
Floating with unrestful slumber,
Yet even those nights I would recall—
Feathers wet and cold—
But colder tears!

Lady Dainagon returned this answer:

Midnight sleep was broken
But no friend to brush away the cold tears!
I envy the Oshidori which has ever its mate by its side.

And then the fragment that is not a diary entry at all. It looks like the covering letter she sent with the manuscript to a friend, and it is the closest she ever comes to asking for something.

I wish I could make known everything to you, good and bad, things of the world, and those relating to my life—all that I could not write in my letters. You could not expect such writing as this from your friend? You feel weary of life; please look into my heart, also weary. Please write to me—even a little—whatever comes into your mind. It would be very unfortunate if my writings were scattered about and made known to others. I have written many things of this sort, but recently I have torn up all my old writings, burying some, and making dolls' houses of the rest. Since that time I have received no letters and am determined to write no more on fresh paper, so thrifty have I become! I think I am not in the wrong. After reading, please return quickly. As I could not revise all there may be some defects; read—overlooking them.

She burned and buried her old papers and pasted the rest into dolls' houses for her daughter. Then she asked one person to write back, said please don't show anyone, said please send it back, and apologised for not proofreading. A thousand years later it is on a private dashboard on the other side of the planet. She would be appalled.

Now Genji. Suematsu's 1882 English is stiff and Victorian, but the architecture survives. This is the first paragraph of the first novel, and notice that it opens not with a hero but with office politics and a woman being bullied to death.

In the reign of a certain Emperor, whose name is unknown to us, there was, among the Niogo and Koyi of the Imperial Court, one who, though she was not of high birth, enjoyed the full tide of Royal favor. Hence her superiors, each one of whom had always been thinking—"I shall be the one," gazed upon her disdainfully with malignant eyes, and her equals and inferiors were more indignant still.

Such being the state of affairs, the anxiety which she had to endure was great and constant, and this was probably the reason why her health was at last so much affected…

Genji's mother dies a few pages in. The Emperor has just extracted a promise that neither of them will go first.

Distracted by his vain endeavors to devise means to aid her, the Emperor … went to her apartment and cried despairingly: "Did not we vow that we would neither of us be either before or after the other even in travelling the last long journey of life? And can you find it in your heart to leave me now?" Sadly and tenderly looking up, she thus replied, with almost failing breath:—

"Since my departure for this dark journey,
Makes you so sad and lonely,
Fain would I stay though weak and weary,
And live for your sake only!"

"Had I but known this before—"

She appeared to have much more to say, but was too weak to continue.

"Had I but known this before" and then nothing. She cuts the sentence off mid-clause because the woman ran out of air. That is a novelist's instinct, and it is the year 1002.

Later, the grieving Emperor sends a messenger to the dead woman's mother with a poem about their orphaned son. The imagery is a fawn asleep under bush clover on a windy autumn plain, which is also the palace, which is also him.

"I thought that time only would assuage my grief; but time only brings before me more vividly my recollection of the lost one. Yet, it is inevitable. How is my boy? Of him, too, I am always thinking. Time once was when we both hoped to bring him up together. May he still be to you a memento of his mother!"

"The sound of the wind is dull and drear
Across Miyagi's dewy lea,
And makes me mourn for the motherless deer
That sleeps beneath the Hagi tree."

And the scene that founded a genre: the rainy night, chapter two, where four young men shut in by the weather sort women into categories and rate them. She wrote the whole thing as a set piece of male self-satisfaction, and then spends the next fifty chapters demonstrating that every rule they invent is wrong.

"I have only just now discovered," continued he, "how difficult it is to meet with a fair creature, of whom one can say, 'This is, indeed, the one; here is, at last, perfection.' There are, indeed, many who fascinate; many who are ready with their pens, and who, when occasion may require, are quick at repartee. But how often such girls as these are conceited about their own accomplishments, and endeavor unduly to disparage those of others! There are again some who are special pets of their parents, and most jealously watched over at home. Often, no doubt, they are pretty, often graceful; and frequently they will apply themselves with effect to music and to poetry, in which they may even attain to special excellence. But then, their friends will keep their drawbacks in the dark, and eulogize their merits to the utmost. If we were to give full credence to this exaggerated praise, we could not but fail in every single instance to be more or less disappointed."

… "In my opinion, the most and the least favored are in the same proportion. I mean, they are both not many. Their birth, also, divides them into three classes. Those, however, who are especially well born, are often too jealously guarded, and are, for the most part, kept secluded from the outside gaze, which frequently tends to make their deportment shy and timid. It is those of the middle class, who are much more frequently seen by us, who afford us most chance of studying their character."

A woman of exactly that middle class, sitting in a room being studied, wrote this transcript of men deciding what she is. Then she gave every one of those categorised women an interior life and let them ruin the men who catalogued them.

Last, the two poems in the Hundred Poets, in MacCauley's 1917 version. Hers, number 57, is about running into someone she knew long ago and losing them again before she could be sure it was them. The headnote says a childhood friend, glimpsed and gone in the same instant.

Meeting in the way—,
While I can not clearly know
If 'tis friend or not;—
Lo! the midnight moon, ah me!
In a cloud has disappeared.
— Murasaki Shikibu, no. 57

And her daughter's, number 58, which is an answer to a man who accused her of being unfaithful. Mount Arima's winds through the bamboo of Ina is a proverb for gossip and inconstancy.

If Mount Arima
Sends his rustling winds across
Ina's bamboo-plains;—
Well! in truth, 'tis as you say;
Yet how can I e'er forget?
— Daini no Sanmi, no. 58, her daughter

The mother's poem is about someone vanishing before she could hold them. The daughter's is about refusing to be talked out of what she feels. Two women, one household, filed next to each other for a thousand years, and neither of their real names survives.

Marcus Aurelius

Rome, 121–180 AD · emperor, Stoic, the most powerful man alive writing a private notebook telling himself to get out of bed

aug 6, 2026▼

childhood & early life

He was born in Rome on 26 April 121, into a family with money and a name, and his father died when he was about three. That is the first fact and it quietly explains a lot of the rest. He was raised by his grandfather and by his mother, Domitia Lucilla, who had inherited an enormous brick and tile works and was, in practical terms, an industrialist. The boy grew up in a house full of tutors and around a woman who ran a business.

He was a serious child in an obvious way. Somewhere around twelve he took up philosophy properly, started sleeping on the floor on a plank with a skin over it, in the Greek manner, and his mother had to beg him to use a bed. Hadrian noticed him early and called him Verissimus, a pun on his family name Verus, meaning "most truthful", which is either affectionate or slightly mocking depending on how you read emperors. In 138 Hadrian arranged the succession so that Antoninus Pius adopted Marcus. He was sixteen or seventeen and had just been handed the largest empire on earth as a homework assignment.

There is a story, from the Historia Augusta, about the night he was told. He was distressed rather than delighted, and when his household asked him why he was sad about being adopted into the imperial family, he listed for them all the evils that sovereignty carries with it. He was a teenager. He had already done the maths.

what he did & why he's remembered

He ruled from 161 to 180 and it was, by any external measure, a bad nineteen years to be in charge. He insisted from day one on sharing power with his adoptive brother Lucius Verus, which had never been done, apparently because he thought it was fair. Then the Parthian war. Then the returning armies brought back what we now call the Antonine Plague, almost certainly smallpox, which burned through the empire for fifteen years and may have killed millions. Then the Danube frontier collapsed and Germanic and Sarmatian peoples poured across into Italy for the first time in centuries. Then, in 175, one of his own generals, Avidius Cassius, declared himself emperor in the East.

Look at what he actually did with each of those. When the treasury was empty and he refused to raise emergency taxes on the provinces, he held a two-month public auction in the Forum of Trajan and sold the palace: gold and crystal goblets, jewels out of Hadrian's cabinet, paintings by great artists, and his wife's silk gold-embroidered robes. Afterwards he told the buyers that anyone who wanted to return what they had bought and get their money back could, and he made it awkward for nobody.

When Cassius was killed by his own soldiers and the head was brought to Marcus, he refused to look at it and ordered it buried. He then asked the Senate, in writing, to execute nobody: not Cassius's family, not the senators involved. Cassius's children kept over half their father's estate and were allowed to travel where they liked. Marcus said he grieved that he had not managed to finish his reign without a single senator's blood.

And he is remembered for none of that. He is remembered because in a tent on the Danube, in the middle of the plague years, he kept a private notebook in Greek arguing with himself. It has no title in the manuscript tradition beyond Ta eis heauton, "to himself". It was never meant to be read by you. That is precisely why it works: there is no audience to perform for, so he does not perform. The most powerful human being alive is writing notes to remind himself that fame is noise, that his position is a costume, and that he has to stop being irritated by his colleagues.

family life

He married his cousin Faustina the Younger, daughter of Antoninus Pius, in 145. Over the next twenty-three years she bore at least thirteen children, including two sets of twins. Roughly six lived to be adults. He buried something like eight of his own children.

The one that gets to me is Verus Caesar, aged seven, dead from an operation on a tumour behind his ear in 169. Marcus was at Praeneste, about to leave for the German war. The record says he mourned for five days, and that even during those five days he answered questions on state business when they were put to him. The games of Jupiter were running and he would not have them stopped for public mourning, so all he did was order statues for his dead son and have the boy's name written into the hymn the Salian priests sang. That is a father managing a schedule around his own child's death because thousands of people are waiting on him. Whether you read that as monstrous discipline or as the only way he could stay upright, it is the same five days.

The twins Commodus and Titus Aurelius Fulvus Antoninus were born in 161. The second one died at four. The first one survived, and Rome would have been better off the other way round. Marcus knew. Two days before his death he told his friends he thought about his son roughly what Philip had thought about Alexander, and said it grieved him to leave a son behind at all.

The other family, the one he chose, was his teachers. Book One of the Meditations is nothing but a list of people and exactly what he took from each of them, and it reads like a man doing an inventory of everything in him that came from somebody else. His letters to his Latin tutor Fronto, which survive because a monk scraped a manuscript to reuse the parchment and somebody read the underwriting in 1815, are absurdly warm. He signs off to a middle-aged rhetoric teacher with "my love, my delight".

romantic life

Thirty years with one woman, and Rome spent most of them gossiping about her. Faustina was accused of sleeping with gladiators, sailors, pantomime actors, and half the Senate. The story went around that Commodus came out the way he did because he was really a gladiator's son. It was also said she backed Avidius Cassius's coup because she thought her husband was dying and wanted a strong man in place.

Marcus's response to all of it was to promote the men she was rumoured to be sleeping with, defend her in letters, and, when someone told him he should divorce her if not kill her, reply: "If we send our wife away, we must also return her dowry." Her dowry was the empire. It is the driest possible way to end a conversation.

She died suddenly in 175, in a village in the foothills of the Taurus mountains, having followed him on campaign for years; the soldiers called her Mother of the Camp. He asked the Senate to make her a goddess, delivered the eulogy himself, founded a charity for orphan girls in her name, turned the village where she died into a city, and built her a temple there. And in the private notebook that nobody was supposed to read, where he had no reason at all to lie, he thanked the gods for a wife who was "so obedient, and so affectionate, and so simple." Simple, in his vocabulary, is high praise. It means unpretending, without an angle.

After she died a noblewoman named Fabia manoeuvred to marry him. He took a concubine instead, the daughter of one of his wife's stewards, on the stated grounds that he did not want to put a stepmother over that many children.

life until death

He spent the last decade of his life in military camps on the Danube, in a climate he hated, with a stomach that barely worked, dosed on theriac, sleeping badly, running an empire by dictation. He was close to annexing Marcomannia and Sarmatia as provinces. One more year would probably have done it.

He died on 17 March 180, at 58, either at Vindobona (Vienna) or Sirmium; the sources disagree. The account in the Historia Augusta is unusually plain. He got sick, called Commodus in and begged him not to abandon what was left of the war. Commodus said what he wanted was to not get sick. Marcus let him go, asking only that he wait a few days. Then, being ready to die, Marcus stopped eating and drinking, which sped things up. On the sixth day he called his friends in and, with what the text calls derision for all human affairs, said: "Why do you weep for me, instead of thinking about the pestilence and about death which is the common lot of us all?" As they were leaving he groaned and said, "If you now grant me leave to go, I bid you farewell and pass on before." Asked to whom he entrusted his son, he said: "To you, if he prove worthy, and to the immortal gods."

On the seventh day he was too tired for anyone. He allowed Commodus in and then sent him straight out again, afraid the boy would catch it. Cassius Dio adds one detail: a tribune came for the night's watchword, and the dying emperor said "Go to the rising sun; I am setting." Then he covered his head as if to sleep and died in the night.

The Senate and the people, sitting together, which was not done, hailed him as a gracious god. For generations afterwards Romans kept small statues of him among their household gods, next to the family spirits, which is a strange and touching thing to do with an emperor. And then Commodus took over, fought a thousand gladiatorial bouts in the arena, was strangled in his bath by his wrestling partner in 192, and the empire fell into a civil war it took a century to finish having. Marcus was the last of the so-called Five Good Emperors and the one who broke the pattern by having a biological son to hand it to. He saw it coming and did it anyway, which is the one place where his judgement, or his nerve, plainly failed.

his works

Everything below is George Long's 1862 translation of the Meditations, out of copyright, plus two letters in C. R. Haines's 1919 Loeb translation. Long's "thou" is old-fashioned but he keeps the shape of the Greek, which matters, because the whole book is written in the second person. Marcus is talking to himself the entire time. Read it as somebody's private argument with their own morning.

Start with the most famous thing in it. Book Two, section one. This is the first substantive line of the book, written by the emperor of Rome, about having to go to work.

Begin the morning by saying to thyself, I shall meet with the busybody, the ungrateful, arrogant, deceitful, envious, unsocial. All these things happen to them by reason of their ignorance of what is good and evil. But I who have seen the nature of the good that it is beautiful, and of the bad that it is ugly, and the nature of him who does wrong, that it is akin to me; not only of the same blood or seed, but that it participates in the same intelligence and the same portion of the divinity, I can neither be injured by any of them, for no one can fix on me what is ugly, nor can I be angry with my kinsman, nor hate him. For we are made for co-operation, like feet, like hands, like eyelids, like the rows of the upper and lower teeth. To act against one another, then, is contrary to nature; and it is acting against one another to be vexed and to turn away.

He is not saying be nice. He is saying: pre-load the difficulty, so that when it arrives it costs you nothing. And notice the tell in "I can neither be injured by any of them" - that is a man who is, in fact, injured by them, writing the sentence down again in the hope it takes.

Then Book Five, section one, which is the single least imperial thing ever written by an emperor.

In the morning when thou risest unwillingly, let this thought be present, — I am rising to the work of a human being. Why then am I dissatisfied if I am going to do the things for which I exist and for which I was brought into the world? Or have I been made for this, to lie in the bed-clothes and keep myself warm? — But this is more pleasant. — Dost thou exist then to take thy pleasure, and not at all for action or exertion? Dost thou not see the little plants, the little birds, the ants, the spiders, the bees working together to put in order their several parts of the universe? And art thou unwilling to do the work of a human being, and dost thou not make haste to do that which is according to thy nature? But it is necessary to take rest also. — It is necessary. However, Nature has fixed bounds to this too.

He argues with himself in dialogue. He puts the lazy voice in the text, gives it the good line ("but this is more pleasant"), and then answers it. Eighteen centuries later this is still the best written account of not wanting to get up.

Book Four, section three. This one is for people who keep thinking that the answer is a different location.

Men seek retreats for themselves, houses in the country, sea-shores, and mountains; and thou too art wont to desire such things very much. But this is altogether a mark of the most common sort of men, for it is in thy power whenever thou shalt choose to retire into thyself. For nowhere either with more quiet or more freedom from trouble does a man retire than into his own soul, particularly when he has within him such thoughts that by looking into them he is immediately in perfect tranquillity; and I affirm that tranquillity is nothing else than the good ordering of the mind. Constantly then give to thyself this retreat, and renew thyself; and let thy principles be brief and fundamental.

Book Two, section seventeen, written at Carnuntum, in camp, during the plague. The note "This in Carnuntum" sits at the end of the book in the manuscript like a location stamp on a photograph.

Of human life the time is a point, and the substance is in a flux, and the perception dull, and the composition of the whole body subject to putrefaction, and the soul a whirl, and fortune hard to divine, and fame a thing devoid of judgment. And, to say all in a word, everything which belongs to the body is a stream, and what belongs to the soul is a dream and vapour, and life is a warfare and a stranger's sojourn, and after fame is oblivion. What then is that which is able to conduct a man? One thing, and only one, philosophy. But this consists in keeping the daemon within a man free from violence and unharmed, superior to pains and pleasures, doing nothing without a purpose, nor yet falsely and with hypocrisy, not feeling the need of another man's doing or not doing anything; and besides, accepting all that happens, and all that is allotted, as coming from thence, wherever it is, from whence he himself came.

Book Six, section thirty. He is warning himself, by name, about the specific occupational hazard of his own job.

Take care that thou art not made into a Caesar, that thou art not dyed with this dye; for such things happen. Keep thyself then simple, good, pure, serious, free from affectation, a friend of justice, a worshipper of the gods, kind, affectionate, strenuous in all proper acts. Strive to continue to be such as philosophy wished to make thee. Reverence the gods, and help men. Short is life. There is only one fruit of this terrene life — a pious disposition and social acts.

"Take care that thou art not made into a Caesar." He is Caesar. He means the personality, not the office. He is afraid of turning into the thing he already is.

A run of short ones, because the short ones are where he stops being tactful with himself:

No longer talk at all about the kind of man that a good man ought to be, but be such. (x. 16)

Do not act as if thou wert going to live ten thousand years. Death hangs over thee. While thou livest, while it is in thy power, be good. (iv. 17)

Take away thy opinion, and then there is taken away the complaint, "I have been harmed." Take away the complaint, "I have been harmed," and the harm is taken away. (iv. 7)

The best way of avenging thyself is not to become like the wrong-doer. (vi. 6)

When a man has done thee any wrong, immediately consider with what opinion about good or evil he has done wrong. For when thou hast seen this, thou wilt pity him, and wilt neither wonder nor be angry. (vii. 26)

Think not so much of what thou hast not as of what thou hast: but of the things which thou hast select the best, and then reflect how eagerly they would have been sought, if thou hadst them not. (vii. 27)

Consider thyself to be dead, and to have completed thy life up to the present time; and live according to nature the remainder which is allowed thee. (vii. 56)

Loss is nothing else than change. (ix. 35)

Be like the promontory against which the waves continually break, but it stands firm and tames the fury of the water around it. (iv. 49)

Soon, very soon, thou wilt be ashes, or a skeleton, and either a name or not even a name; but name is sound and echo. (v. 33)

A spider is proud when it has caught a fly, and another when he has caught a poor hare, and another when he has taken a little fish in a net, and another when he has taken wild boars, and another when he has taken bears, and another when he has taken Sarmatians. Are not these robbers, if thou examinest their opinions? (x. 10)

That last one is him. He had just taken Sarmatians. He put himself in the list of spiders.

Book Six, section forty-seven, on the levelling effect of the plague years, and Book Four's answer to it:

Hippocrates, after curing many diseases, himself fell sick and died. The Chaldaei foretold the deaths of many, and then fate caught them too. Alexander and Pompeius, and Caius Caesar, after so often completely destroying whole cities, and in battle cutting to pieces many ten thousands of cavalry and infantry, themselves too at last departed from life. Heraclitus, after so many speculations on the conflagration of the universe, was filled with water internally and died smeared all over with mud. And lice destroyed Democritus; and other lice killed Socrates. What means all this? Thou hast embarked, thou hast made the voyage, thou art come to shore; get out.

… always observe how ephemeral and worthless human things are, and what was yesterday a little mucus, to-morrow will be a mummy or ashes. Pass then through this little space of time conformably to nature, and end thy journey in content, as an olive falls off when it is ripe, blessing nature who produced it, and thanking the tree on which it grew.

And this, from Book Four, is the closest the book comes to a prayer:

Everything harmonizes with me, which is harmonious to thee, O Universe. Nothing for me is too early nor too late, which is in due time for thee. Everything is fruit to me which thy seasons bring, O Nature: from thee are all things, in thee are all things, to thee all things return.
