Charlotte's romantic life was not blank, but it was mostly made of refusal, repression, and inconvenient longing. In Brussels, she became emotionally attached to Constantin Héger, who was married. She wrote him intense letters after returning to England. He tore some up, and his wife later saved pieces. Rude, but also historically useful. The attachment was not a conventional affair; it was more humiliating and more literary than that: hunger pointed at a closed door.

She rejected several marriage proposals, including one from Henry Nussey and another from James Taylor. Eventually she married Arthur Bell Nicholls, her father's curate, in 1854. Her father resisted at first, because apparently even Victorian dads liked making things worse. The marriage seems to have brought her some happiness, but it was painfully brief.

life until death

After Jane Eyre, Charlotte became known in London literary circles, though she remained socially awkward, shy, and visibly shaped by Haworth. She met writers like William Makepeace Thackeray and Elizabeth Gaskell, who later wrote her biography. But Charlotte never became sleek or easy. Her genius had mud on its hem and nerves under the skin.

She became pregnant after marrying Nicholls, then fell severely ill. She died on March 31, 1855, at thirty-eight. Her death certificate listed phthisis, but modern scholars often suggest extreme pregnancy sickness may have contributed. Whatever the medical label, the ending is savage: after years of writing women who fight for life, love, dignity, and a room to stand in, Charlotte barely got a few months of ordinary married peace before death took her too.

their works

Charlotte's voice is volcanic under a plain dress. She writes morality, but not meekness. She writes love, but makes it answer to self-respect first.

I am no bird; and no net ensnares me; I am a free human being with an independent will, which I now exert to leave you.

From Jane Eyre. This is the line people put on mugs, but it deserves better than mug life. It is Jane refusing to be reduced to someone's pretty captive.

Do you think I am an automaton? - a machine without feelings? and can bear to have my morsel of bread snatched from my lips, and my drop of living water dashed from my cup? Do you think, because I am poor, obscure, plain, and little, I am soulless and heartless? You think wrong! - I have as much soul as you - and full as much heart!

This is why Jane survives centuries. She does not ask to be beautiful before demanding to be treated as fully human.

Reader, I married him.

Four words. Maximum control. She does not say he married me. She says I married him. Jane narrates her own ending like she owns the deed.

No mockery in this world ever sounds to me so hollow as that of being told to cultivate happiness. What does such advice mean? Happiness is not a potato, to be planted in mould, and tilled with manure.

From Villette. Charlotte saw toxic positivity coming from 1853 and already hated it. Correct woman.

Life appears to me too short to be spent in nursing animosity, or registering wrongs.

Also Jane Eyre. Not forgiveness as weakness, more like emotional bookkeeping is expensive and Jane has other things to do.

There was no possibility of taking a walk that day. We had been wandering, indeed, in the leafless shrubbery an hour in the morning; but since dinner the cold winter wind had brought with it clouds so sombre, and a rain so penetrating, that further out-door exercise was now out of the question.

The opening of Jane Eyre. A child trapped indoors by weather and dependence. Charlotte understood that sometimes the whole plot begins with a girl having nowhere acceptable to go.

Emily Dickinson

American poet, recluse-myth, surgeon of death and desire · 1830-1886

jul 31, 2026▼

childhood & early life

Emily Elizabeth Dickinson was born on December 10, 1830, in Amherst, Massachusetts, in the family Homestead on Main Street. Amherst was small, religious, educated, and watchful: exactly the kind of place that makes a private mind either shrink or become lethal. Emily chose lethal, but quietly.

Her father, Edward Dickinson, was a lawyer, treasurer of Amherst College, and later a congressman. He was stern, civic-minded, and emotionally about as soft as furniture. Her mother, Emily Norcross Dickinson, was more withdrawn and often ill. Emily grew up with her older brother Austin and younger sister Lavinia, called Vinnie, who would later become the accidental guardian of her immortality.

She was educated seriously for a girl of her time. She attended Amherst Academy, studied literature, botany, Latin, science, and religion, then spent one year at Mount Holyoke Female Seminary. The school sorted girls by religious certainty, and Emily landed among the spiritually unresolved. Very on brand. She returned home, and over time the world got smaller around her while the poems got stranger and larger.

what she did & why she's remembered

Dickinson wrote nearly 1,800 poems, most of them unseen by the public while she was alive. Only about ten poems were published in her lifetime, usually altered by editors who thought her dashes, slant rhymes, and weird compression needed manners. They did not. Her poems are tiny locked rooms where death, God, desire, grief, nature, madness, and power all breathe too close to the wall.

Between the late 1850s and mid-1860s, she entered her great writing period and copied hundreds of poems into hand-sewn booklets now called fascicles. She was not merely scribbling private feelings. She was building an architecture of thought. Her poems cut away explanation and leave the nerve exposed. She could make a fly ruin a deathbed, make hope into a bird, make loneliness feel like weather inside the skull.

The myth says she was a sad shut-in in white. The truth is sharper. She was private, selective, intense, funny, stubborn, and probably uninterested in performing normal womanhood for people who would not understand the work anyway. Respectfully: she opted out and became unavoidable.

family life

Emily lived much of her life in the Dickinson Homestead, close to family and yet inwardly unreachable. Austin married Susan Gilbert and moved next door into The Evergreens. Susan became Emily's sister-in-law, friend, reader, emotional force field, and one of the most important recipients of her letters and poems. Their relationship was intense enough that scholars still argue about how to name it. Friendship feels too small. Romance may or may not fit cleanly. The poems do not care about our categories.

Vinnie never married and remained with Emily at home. After Emily died, Vinnie found the fascicles and refused to burn them, despite Emily's request that some papers be destroyed. That disobedience gave the world Dickinson. Sometimes sibling betrayal is a public service. Austin, meanwhile, later had an affair with Mabel Loomis Todd, who would become one of the first editors of Emily's poems. The family afterlife was messy, dramatic, and extremely Amherst.

romantic life

Dickinson never married, but the idea that she lacked passion is lazy reading with shoes on the bed. Her poems and letters are full of voltage. Susan Gilbert is central: Emily wrote to her with devotion, jealousy, playfulness, injury, and longing. Whether we call it romantic love, queer attachment, soul-friendship, or something more nineteenth-century and less tidy, Susan mattered deeply.

There were also the mysterious “Master” letters, addressed to an unknown beloved with language so intense it practically claws at the page. Possible candidates have included Reverend Charles Wadsworth, Samuel Bowles, and others, but no answer is secure. Later in life, she formed a warm attachment with Judge Otis Phillips Lord, a widower and family friend; marriage may even have been considered, but he died in 1884. Emily's romantic life is not blank. It is encrypted.

life until death

As she aged, Dickinson became more physically secluded. She dressed often in white, spoke to some visitors from behind doors, gardened, baked, wrote letters, and made the house into both monastery and laboratory. But seclusion was not emptiness. She maintained intense correspondence with friends, editors, relatives, and literary figures, including Thomas Wentworth Higginson, whom she asked, “Are you too deeply occupied to say if my Verse is alive?” That is such a clean little knife of a question.

The deaths kept coming: friends, mentors, parents, nephews, Lord. Her mother died in 1882, Lord in 1884, and Emily's own health declined afterward. She died in Amherst on May 15, 1886, at fifty-five, likely from complications then described as Bright's disease. Afterward, Vinnie discovered the poems. The first volume appeared in 1890, edited and regularized. Only decades later did readers begin to meet Dickinson closer to her own punctuation, her own fractures, her own dangerous little dashes.

their works

Dickinson is best read directly. Her poems are short, but they do not behave like small things. They sit there looking harmless, then bite through the floor.

“Hope” is the thing with feathers -
That perches in the soul -
And sings the tune without the words -
And never stops - at all -

And sweetest - in the Gale - is heard -
And sore must be the storm -
That could abash the little Bird
That kept so many warm -

I've heard it in the chillest land -
And on the strangest Sea -
Yet - never - in Extremity,
It asked a crumb - of me.

This is the famous one, but fame has made people forget how severe it is. Hope is not a slogan here. It is a creature surviving bad weather without asking permission.

Because I could not stop for Death -
He kindly stopped for me -
The Carriage held but just Ourselves -
And Immortality.

We slowly drove - He knew no haste
And I had put away
My labor and my leisure too,
For His Civility -

We passed the School, where Children strove
At Recess - in the Ring -
We passed the Fields of Gazing Grain -
We passed the Setting Sun -

Or rather - He passed Us -
The Dews drew quivering and Chill -
For only Gossamer, my Gown -
My Tippet - only Tulle -

We paused before a House that seemed
A Swelling of the Ground -
The Roof was scarcely visible -
The Cornice - in the Ground -

Since then - 'tis Centuries - and yet
Feels shorter than the Day
I first surmised the Horses' Heads
Were toward Eternity -

Death arrives like a polite date with a carriage. Horror wearing manners. Very efficient, very cursed.

I felt a Funeral, in my Brain,
And Mourners to and fro
Kept treading - treading - till it seemed
That Sense was breaking through -

And when they all were seated,
A Service, like a Drum -
Kept beating - beating - till I thought
My Mind was going numb -

And then I heard them lift a Box
And creak across my Soul
With those same Boots of Lead, again,
Then Space - began to toll,

As all the Heavens were a Bell,
And Being, but an Ear,
And I, and Silence, some strange Race
Wrecked, solitary, here -

And then a Plank in Reason, broke,
And I dropped down, and down -
And hit a World, at every plunge,
And Finished knowing - then -

This is not “sad girl poetry.” This is a neurological event with church architecture.

My Life had stood - a Loaded Gun -
In Corners - till a Day
The Owner passed - identified -
And carried Me away -

And now We roam in Sovereign Woods -
And now We hunt the Doe -
And every time I speak for Him
The Mountains straight reply -

And do I smile, such cordial light
Upon the Valley glow -
It is as a Vesuvian face
Had let its pleasure through -

And when at Night - Our good Day done -
I guard My Master's Head -
'Tis better than the Eider-Duck's
Deep Pillow - to have shared -

To foe of His - I'm deadly foe -
None stir the second time -
On whom I lay a Yellow Eye -
Or an emphatic Thumb -

Though I than He - may longer live
He longer must - than I -
For I have but the power to kill,
Without - the power to die -

Everyone who tries to make Dickinson harmless should be forced to read this twice. It is possession, rage, agency, erotic charge, violence as metaphor, and the terror of being powerful but not free.

Tell all the truth but tell it slant -
Success in Circuit lies
Too bright for our infirm Delight
The Truth's superb surprise

As Lightning to the Children eased
With explanation kind
The Truth must dazzle gradually
Or every man be blind -

This is basically Dickinson's operating manual. Direct truth is not always the most accurate delivery system. Sometimes you tilt the blade.

Mary Shelley

English novelist, gothic architect, mother of science fiction · 1797–1851

jul 30, 2026▼

childhood & early life

Mary Wollstonecraft Godwin was born in London on August 30, 1797, into a household already crowded with dangerous ideas. Her mother was Mary Wollstonecraft, author of A Vindication of the Rights of Woman, one of the fiercest early arguments for women's education and independence. Her father was William Godwin, radical philosopher, novelist, and professional enemy of lazy authority. Mary inherited an intellectual throne and a wound at the same time: Wollstonecraft died eleven days after giving birth to her.

So Mary's first relationship with her mother was absence. She learned her through books, reputation, and a grave. As a girl, she would visit Wollstonecraft's grave at St Pancras, read there, think there, and later meet Percy Bysshe Shelley there. Romanticism was not subtle. Her father educated her unusually well for a girl: history, literature, languages, philosophy, politics, and the habit of treating ideas like living creatures that might escape their cages.

what she did & why she's remembered

Mary Shelley wrote Frankenstein; or, The Modern Prometheus before she was twenty. That alone is annoying in the best way. In 1816, during the cold, stormy “year without a summer,” she was in Switzerland with Percy Shelley, Lord Byron, John Polidori, and Claire Clairmont. They read ghost stories, talked about galvanism and experiments on life, and Byron proposed that each of them write a supernatural tale. Mary struggled at first, then had the waking nightmare that became Victor Frankenstein and his abandoned Creature.

Frankenstein is not just a monster story. It is about creation without responsibility, genius without care, parental abandonment, loneliness turning into violence, and men wanting godlike power while having the emotional regulation of wet paper. Science fiction basically crawls out of this book with grave dirt under its nails. She later wrote Valperga, The Last Man, Lodore, Falkner, travel writing, biographies, short stories, and edited Percy's poetry after his death, preserving much of his reputation while her own kept getting treated like an accessory. Rude. Predictable.

family life

Mary's family was brilliant and messy. Her father remarried Mary Jane Clairmont in 1801, bringing stepsiblings into the household, including Claire Clairmont, who would become both companion and chaos device. Mary had a half-sister, Fanny Imlay, Wollstonecraft's older daughter, whose life ended in suicide in 1816. That same year, Percy's first wife Harriet also died by suicide. The Shelley orbit was not romantic in the soft candlelight sense. It was debt, scandal, ideology, grief, bodies in rivers, and young people insisting freedom would save them while reality sharpened its teeth.

Mary and Percy had four children, but only one, Percy Florence Shelley, survived into adulthood. Their first daughter was born prematurely and died within weeks. Clara died in Venice in 1818. William died of malaria in Rome in 1819. Mary carried motherhood as repeated loss, which makes Frankenstein even more brutal: a creator makes life, looks at it, recoils, and abandons it. The book knows that birth is not the same thing as love.

romantic life

Mary met Percy Shelley as a teenager. He was already married to Harriet Westbrook, already dramatic, already broke in the morally fashionable way, and already fascinated by Mary's father. Mary was sixteen when she and Percy ran away to Europe in 1814 with Claire Clairmont. Godwin, radical in theory and very father in practice, was furious and refused to see her for years.

Mary and Percy married on December 30, 1816, after Harriet's death. Their love was intense, intellectually alive, and often painful. Percy believed in free love; Mary, unsurprisingly, did not always enjoy being the woman asked to endure the theory. There were emotional entanglements, money problems, pregnancies, deaths, and the constant pressure of being young and scandalous in a world that punished women harder. Then Percy drowned in 1822 at twenty-nine. Mary was twenty-four, widowed, broke, famous without security, and left to raise their surviving son.

life until death

After Percy's death, Mary returned to England and chose survival over melodrama, which is less glamorous and much harder. She wrote for money, edited Percy's works, negotiated with his difficult father, protected her son, and built a literary life in a society that preferred widows quiet, grateful, and economically helpless. She did not remarry, though she had attachments and friendships. Her public identity became half “author of Frankenstein,” half guardian of Percy Shelley, which is a strange fate for the woman who gave the modern world one of its most durable myths.

Her later years were marked by illness, likely involving brain tumors or neurological disease, with headaches and weakness. She died in London on February 1, 1851, at fifty-three. After her death, her family found locks of her dead children's hair, Percy's ashes, and remnants of his heart among her things. Very gothic. Very Mary. She had spent a life among graves, texts, children lost, men mythologized, and monsters misunderstood, and somehow she still kept writing.

their works

Mary's sentences are colder and more wounded than the pop-culture monster suggests. The Creature is not born evil. He is born needy, abandoned, observant, and then taught hatred by rejection. Horrible little mirror for humanity, thanks Mary.

It was on a dreary night of November that I beheld the accomplishment of my toils. With an anxiety that almost amounted to agony, I collected the instruments of life around me, that I might infuse a spark of being into the lifeless thing that lay at my feet.

That is the birth scene from Frankenstein, and it is basically anti-maternity: creation without welcome. Victor wants the miracle until the miracle looks back.

I ought to be thy Adam; but I am rather the fallen angel, whom thou drivest from joy for no misdeed.

The Creature understands literature faster than most men understand accountability. He knows the story he was promised and the one he was given.

I was benevolent and good; misery made me a fiend. Make me happy, and I shall again be virtuous.

This is why the book still works. The monster is not asking to be excused. He is explaining the terrifying social math of abandonment.

Beware; for I am fearless, and therefore powerful.

Tiny line, enormous threat. Fearlessness here is not inspirational poster nonsense. It is what happens when someone has nothing left to protect.

Nothing is so painful to the human mind as a great and sudden change.

Mary earned that sentence the ugly way.

The last man! Yes, I may well describe that solitary being's feelings, feeling myself as the last relic of a beloved race, my companions extinct before me.

From The Last Man, her plague apocalypse novel. It was dismissed in its time, because people are often useless when a woman writes ahead of them. It reads differently after you know how many dead beloveds she carried.

Jane Austen

English novelist, social surgeon, professional observer of polite nonsense · 1775–1817

jul 29, 2026▼

childhood & early life

Jane Austen was born on December 16, 1775, in Steventon, Hampshire, into a parsonage full of books, brothers, noise, jokes, and extremely useful human material. Her father, George Austen, was the Anglican rector of the village and ran a small school for boys from the home. Her mother, Cassandra Leigh Austen, had wit of her own and came from a more socially connected family. Jane was the seventh of eight children, with six brothers and one beloved sister, Cassandra, who would become the fixed star of her life.

The Austen children read, wrote, acted in family theatricals, made jokes, and watched adults perform status anxiety in real time. Jane learned early that drawing rooms were battlefields, marriage was an economic contract pretending to be romance, and foolish people rarely know they are foolish. By twelve, she was already writing comic pieces for family entertainment. She was not a sheltered little rose. She was a child with a scalpel.

what she did & why she's remembered

Austen helped give the English novel its modern nerve: precise character psychology, social comedy, moral intelligence, and romance that only works when both people become less stupid. Her novels look small if you only count events. A few families visit, dance, gossip, misunderstand, propose, refuse, inherit, and marry. But inside that small world she maps vanity, self-deception, class pressure, money panic, female dependence, bad parenting, charm without character, and intelligence learning humility.

Sense and Sensibility appeared in 1811 as “By a Lady.” Pride and Prejudice followed in 1813, Mansfield Park in 1814, and Emma in 1815. Northanger Abbey and Persuasion were published after her death. She did not publish under her own name while alive, but she knew exactly what she was doing. Her irony is so clean people mistake it for gentleness. That is their problem.

family life

Jane's family gave her affection and material, but not financial security. When George Austen retired, the family moved to Bath, a city Jane did not love, and after his death in 1805, Jane, Cassandra, and their mother were left dependent on the support of the Austen brothers. They moved around until her brother Edward provided them a cottage at Chawton in 1809. That cottage gave Jane stability, and stability gave the novels their final form.

Cassandra was Jane's closest companion. Neither sister married. After Jane's death, Cassandra destroyed many personal letters, probably to protect her sister's privacy and family reputation. Annoying for historians, understandable for a sister. The result is that Austen remains partly hidden, which feels appropriate. She spent her life observing everyone else with terrifying accuracy while leaving fewer fingerprints than we want.

romantic life

Austen wrote some of the most enduring love stories in English and still did not marry, which is exactly the sort of irony she would have enjoyed if it had happened to someone else. At twenty, she met Tom Lefroy, a charming young Irishman, and their flirtation seems to have been real enough for family notice. It went nowhere; neither of them had money, and romance without money was not a plot twist in her world. It was a problem.

In 1802, she accepted a proposal from Harris Bigg-Wither, a wealthy family friend, then withdrew it the next morning. That reversal matters. She understood the practical security marriage offered, especially to women without fortunes. She also understood the horror of tying herself for life to a man she could not love or respect. Austen's novels reward good marriages, not marriage as surrender. Very sensible. Very inconvenient.

life until death

At Chawton, Austen revised earlier drafts and wrote with new force. She worked in the family sitting room, famously on small sheets of paper, with a creaking door that warned her when someone approached. There is something delicious about one of the sharpest minds in literature needing a squeaky hinge for privacy. Operations solution, Regency edition.

By 1816, illness was closing in, but she kept working. She revised Persuasion, wrote the comic Plan of a Novel, and began Sanditon, an unfinished novel that already shows her turning toward hypochondria, speculation, seaside development, and modern nonsense. She died in Winchester on July 18, 1817, at forty-one, with Cassandra beside her, and was buried in Winchester Cathedral. Her brother Henry later identified her publicly as the author. The world finally got her name after losing the woman.

their works

Austen's actual language is the point. The plots are famous, but the sentences are where the knives live.

It is a truth universally acknowledged, that a single man in possession of a good fortune, must be in want of a wife.

The opening of Pride and Prejudice is a trap disguised as a proverb. It pretends to state what everyone knows, while actually exposing what everyone wants: rich men, marriage markets, social climbing, and mothers doing math in their heads.

Vanity and pride are different things, though the words are often used synonymously. A person may be proud without being vain. Pride relates more to our opinion of ourselves, vanity to what we would have others think of us.

Mary Bennet says this, and because Mary is Mary, everyone wants to ignore her. Unfortunately, she is correct. Terrible social experience, being right while annoying.

I declare after all there is no enjoyment like reading! How much sooner one tires of any thing than of a book! When I have a house of my own, I shall be miserable if I have not an excellent library.

Caroline Bingley says this in Pride and Prejudice while trying to impress Darcy, which is Austen's little joke: even true sentences can be fake when the motive is performance. Painfully modern.

A large income is the best recipe for happiness I ever heard of.

From Mansfield Park, because Austen never romanticizes poverty for women. Love is lovely. Rent is also persuasive.

There could have been no two hearts so open, no tastes so similar, no feelings so in unison, no countenances so beloved.

From Persuasion, her late, aching novel about second chances after people have already paid for being persuadable. This one is quieter than Pride and Prejudice, but it hurts more.

The person, be it gentleman or lady, who has not pleasure in a good novel, must be intolerably stupid.

From Northanger Abbey. Correct, no notes.

Hildegard of Bingen

German abbess, mystic, composer, healer, writer · 1098–1179

jul 28, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Hildegard was born in 1098 in the Rhineland, probably at Bermersheim or nearby Böckelheim, into a noble German family with more children than spare tenderness. She was the tenth child of Hildebert and Mechthild, and medieval parents had a habit of offering tenth things to God, including, apparently, complicated little daughters who saw light nobody else could see.

From childhood, Hildegard experienced visions: not dreams exactly, not sleep, not madness in the way her enemies might have preferred, but waking perceptions of what she later called the “living light.” She was sickly, sensitive, and strange. At around eight, she was placed under the care of Jutta of Sponheim, an anchoress attached to the Benedictine monastery at Disibodenberg. This sounds peaceful until you remember an anchoress was essentially sealed into religious life while still alive. Medieval spirituality did not believe in soft onboarding.

Jutta taught her prayer, Latin psalms, discipline, and the rhythm of monastic life. Hildegard later became a Benedictine nun, then, after Jutta died in 1136, the women of the community chose Hildegard as their leader. She was already almost forty, and for years she had kept her visions mostly private. Then came the command she could not dodge: write what you see.

what she did & why she's remembered

Hildegard became one of the strangest and most complete minds of the twelfth century: visionary theologian, composer, abbess, preacher, medical writer, natural philosopher, letter writer, political scold, and founder of convents. She wrote Scivias, Liber Vitae Meritorum, and Liber Divinorum Operum, three huge visionary works where theology arrives through cosmic images: mountains of iron, wheels of fire, female figures named Wisdom and Love, the universe shaped like a body, creation burning with green life.

She also composed more than seventy liturgical songs and the Ordo Virtutum, often described as the earliest surviving morality play with music. Her melodies climb in ways that feel almost physically ambitious, like the voice is trying to leave the body without asking permission. She invented a private language, Lingua Ignota, because apparently normal Latin was not weird enough for one Rhineland nun with a cosmic radio in her skull.

What makes Hildegard deliciously inconvenient is that she built authority out of the very thing that should have made people dismiss her. She called herself weak, unlearned, a poor little woman, then proceeded to advise popes, emperors, abbots, queens, and bishops with the confidence of a thunderstorm wearing a veil. Very “I am just a girl” while moving institutional furniture across Europe.

family life

Her birth family gave her away young, which sounds brutal now but sat inside medieval patterns of piety, inheritance, and convenience. Her deeper family became monastic: Jutta, the women under her care, scribes like Volmar who helped record her visions, and the nuns she fought to house on their own terms.

Around 1150, against resistance from the male monks at Disibodenberg, she moved her community to Rupertsberg near Bingen. This was not a cute relocation. It was a power move. The monks did not want to lose the prestige and practical benefits of having Hildegard's community attached to them. Hildegard insisted anyway, got sick during the conflict in a way she and others interpreted as divine pressure, and eventually won. Later she founded another convent at Eibingen. The woman did operations, theology, construction, fundraising, HR, and prophecy. Terrible workload. Iconic execution.

romantic life

Hildegard never married. Her life was vowed religious life, and the official romance was divine: soul and God, Church and Christ, Wisdom and creation, all the medieval symbolic machinery turned up to cathedral volume. But her writing is not emotionally dry. It is lush, embodied, fragrant, green, musical, and sometimes startlingly intimate.

Her most intense attachments seem to have been spiritual and communal, especially to the women she led and protected. She wrote about virginity, virtue, motherhood, obedience, and divine love, but always with the force of someone who did not experience holiness as bland goodness. For Hildegard, God was fire in the blood of the world. Not exactly beige church-lady energy.

life until death

In old age, Hildegard became even less manageable. She traveled on preaching tours, unusual for a woman of her time, calling clergy corrupt and urging reform. She wrote letters to Frederick Barbarossa, to popes, to abbots, to people who asked for healing, judgment, counsel, or prophecy. Her authority came partly from vision and partly from stamina. She just kept going.

Near the end of her life, her convent was placed under interdict because she allowed a man who had been excommunicated to be buried in their cemetery, insisting he had reconciled with the Church before death. The penalty silenced the community's music. Imagine punishing Hildegard by forbidding song. That is almost cartoonishly cruel. She fought back in letters and eventually the ban was lifted.

She died on September 17, 1179, around eighty-one years old. Later tradition says lights appeared in the sky over her room when she died. Whether or not that happened, the image is emotionally correct. Hildegard spent her life insisting that the world was lit from within, and then she left like a window closing.

their works

Hildegard's surviving work is enormous: visions, songs, medical/natural writings, letters, sermons, and drama. Translation wording varies, and some modern translations are copyrighted, so these excerpts are kept short or drawn from accessible older/public-facing versions. The important thing is her texture: cosmic, botanical, female, severe, and weird in the best possible way.

from scivias · the first vision

the opening image is pure medieval fever cinema

I saw a great mountain the color of iron, and enthroned on it One of such great glory that it blinded my sight. On each side of him there extended a soft shadow, like a wing of wondrous breadth and length.

Before him, at the foot of the mountain, stood an image full of eyes on all sides, in which, because of those eyes, I could discern no human form.

from scivias · the command to write

her whole public life begins with this pressure

O fragile human, ashes of ashes, and filth of filth, say and write what you see and hear.

But because you are timid in speaking and simple in expounding and untaught in writing, speak and write these things not by a human mouth nor by the understanding of human invention, but as you see and hear them in the heavens above.

o viridissima virga

one of her Marian songs, full of her beloved greenness, viriditas

O branch of freshest green,
you stand in your nobility
as dawn breaks on high.

Rejoice now and be glad,
and deign to free us, frail and weakened,
from evil habits;
stretch forth your hand to lift us up.

ordo virtutum

her musical morality play, where the soul argues with virtues and the devil gets no melody

O living fountain, how great is your sweetness,
you who do not let your face be lost
in the sight of these people,
but gather them keenly
from the fall.

Tiny theatre nerd detail: in Ordo Virtutum, the Virtues sing, the Soul sings, but the Devil only shouts. Evil, in Hildegard's imagination, has no music. Very rude. Very satisfying.

on greenness

her signature idea, usually called viriditas: spiritual life as green vitality

I am the fiery life of the essence of God; I am the flame above the beauty in the fields; I shine in the waters; I burn in the sun, the moon, and the stars.

With every breeze, as with invisible life that contains everything, I awaken everything to life.

Enheduanna

Akkadian princess, high priestess, first named author in history · c. 23rd century BCE

jul 27, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Enheduanna lived more than four thousand years ago, in the Akkadian Empire, when writing was still young enough to feel like a spell pressed into clay. Her birth name is lost. The name we know her by was a title: En-hedu-ana, often understood as high priestess, ornament, or priestess connected with the sky god An. History took almost everything ordinary from her and left the enormous parts: princess, priestess, poet, political instrument, survivor.

Her father was Sargon of Akkad, the empire-builder who united Sumerian and Akkadian lands under one dynasty. To hold that empire together, he placed his daughter in one of the most powerful religious offices in Mesopotamia: high priestess of Nanna, the moon god, at Ur. This was not decorative spirituality. It was power with ritual clothing on. Enheduanna stood where religion, administration, language, and imperial politics met.

Imagine being born into a world where kings carve their victories into stone, men claim cities through war, and a woman learns to make her own authority survive through hymns. Not poems as cute feelings. Poems as statecraft. Poems as theology. Poems as proof that she existed.

what she did & why she's remembered

Enheduanna is often called the earliest known author in world history whose name we know. Not merely the first known woman writer. The first named author, period. Before Homer, before Sappho, before the Greek tragedians, before almost everyone schools usually bother to mention, there was a priestess in Ur signing herself into literature.

She is associated with temple hymns and with major hymns to Inanna, the Sumerian goddess of love, war, sex, sovereignty, chaos, and terrifying feminine power. Inanna later merged with Ishtar, and Enheduanna's writing helped lift her from local goddess into cosmic queen. That is not small. She helped reorganize divine imagination for an empire.

Her best-known works include The Exaltation of Inanna, The Great-Hearted Mistress, and Goddess of the Fearsome Powers, along with a cycle of temple hymns. Her poetry is strange in the best way: devotional, political, sensual, angry, desperate, formal, and intensely personal. She does not sound like a dusty museum object. She sounds like someone cornered by history and still saying, write this down.

family life

Her father, Sargon, was one of the great conquerors of the ancient world. Her mother is not securely known. Her family was dynasty, not softness: brothers, royal succession, imperial pressure, gods used as legitimacy, daughters placed where politics required them. Enheduanna's office at Ur made her both a religious leader and a royal anchor in Sumerian territory.

She did not have children in the ordinary domestic sense that history records. Her inheritance was scribal. Later students copied her hymns for centuries. That is a different kind of lineage: clay tablets instead of descendants, classroom memory instead of nursery memory. Honestly, not the worst way to haunt civilization.

romantic life

Enheduanna's romantic life is not recorded, and for once the absence matters. As high priestess of Nanna, she occupied a sacred role often described in symbolic marital language: spouse or consort of the god. Her public identity was ritual, political, and theological. Whatever private human longing she had is hidden behind temple walls and broken tablets.

But her poems are not bloodless. Her devotion to Inanna has heat, fear, surrender, rage, and intimacy. She writes to the goddess like someone who knows what it means to need power outside herself and hate needing it. That emotional structure is very human, even if the official language is divine.

life until death

At some point, a ruler named Lugal-Ane rebelled and Enheduanna was forced from her office. The Exaltation of Inanna appears to come out of that crisis. She describes being driven from the sacred space where she belonged, stripped of authority, treated as if she had never lived there. That line is the knife. Exile is not only being removed from a place. It is people trying to erase your reality.

The poem pleads with Inanna to intervene, not politely, but with full divine violence. Enheduanna wants restoration. She wants the usurper thrown down. She wants the goddess to remember her. Later tradition suggests she was restored, or at least that her name survived strongly enough to outlast the men who tried to make her temporary.

We do not know exactly when or how she died. That ignorance feels almost insulting, but also fitting for the ancient world: the death record disappears, the voice remains. More than four millennia later, she is still here, because somebody kept copying the woman who wrote herself into clay.

their works

Her surviving works come through ancient copies and modern translations, so the exact English wording varies. What survives clearly is the force: Enheduanna names herself, names her office, names her pain, and turns exile into literature.

from the exaltation of inanna

the moment she names herself inside the poem

I, En-hedu-ana the en priestess, entered my holy jipar in your service. I carried the ritual basket, and intoned the song of joy. But funeral offerings were brought, as if I had never lived there.

from the exaltation of inanna · exile

this is not soft devotional poetry. this is a woman reporting political violence through sacred language

He made me walk through the thorn bushes of the mountains. He stripped me of the rightful crown of the en priestess. He gave me a knife and dagger, saying to me, "These are appropriate ornaments for you."

from the exaltation of inanna · appeal to the goddess

she turns personal humiliation into cosmic legal action

I, En-hedu-ana, will recite a prayer to you. To you, holy Inana, I shall give free vent to my tears like sweet beer.

My good divine wild cow, drive out the man, capture the man! In the place of divine encouragement, what is my standing now?

from the exaltation of inanna · restoration

the poem ends by lifting Inanna and, through her, the woman who survived enough to sing

Everyone's speech to the mistress is exalted. Praise be to the destroyer of foreign lands, endowed with divine powers by An, to my lady enveloped in beauty, to Inana!

George Eliot

English novelist, psychological realist, Victorian intellectual menace · 1819–1880

jul 26, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Mary Ann Evans was born on November 22, 1819, at South Farm on the Arbury Hall estate near Nuneaton, Warwickshire. Before she became George Eliot, before Middlemarch, before Victorian England decided she was brilliant but socially inconvenient, she was a serious little girl in the Midlands with a sharp mind, a plain face people would not stop commenting on, and a hunger for books that became almost physical.

Her father, Robert Evans, managed the Arbury estate, and through him she saw both rural labor and respectable middle-class ambition up close. Her mother, Christiana Pearson Evans, came from a prosperous farming family and died when Mary Ann was still young. The loss mattered. Eliot's novels are full of children watching adults, measuring love, noticing unfairness, and understanding too much before anyone thinks they should.

She was educated unusually well for a girl of her time. She studied at schools in Nuneaton, Attleborough, and Coventry, then kept educating herself after formal schooling ended. Languages, theology, philosophy, German criticism, music, translation, moral argument: she ate all of it. In her twenties she had a religious crisis, stopped attending church for a time, and horrified her father so badly that he threatened to throw her out. She eventually compromised outwardly, but inwardly the old certainty had cracked. That crack became her genius.

what she did & why she's remembered

Eliot became one of the greatest novelists in English literature because she treated ordinary people as morally immense. She did not need castles, curses, pirates, or convenient villains. She could take a provincial town, a marriage, an inheritance, a debt, a disappointed girl, a self-important man, and make the whole human condition crawl out from under the furniture.

She worked first as a translator and journalist, eventually becoming assistant editor of the Westminster Review, which was already an outrageous position for a woman in mid-Victorian literary London. Then she began publishing fiction under the name George Eliot. The male pen name was partly protection, partly strategy. She wanted her work judged seriously, not tossed into the decorative basket labeled "lady novelist." Very fair. Men had already been making mediocre books with confidence for centuries.

Her major novels include Adam Bede, The Mill on the Floss, Silas Marner, Romola, Felix Holt, Middlemarch, and Daniel Deronda. Middlemarch is usually treated as her masterpiece: a novel about ambition, marriage, reform, money, disappointment, and the quiet tragedy of becoming smaller than you meant to be. Eliot's true subject was moral psychology. She understood that people rarely ruin their lives with one dramatic decision. Usually they do it with tiny compromises, vanity, fear, bad timing, and the need to be loved.

family life

Mary Ann was the youngest child of Robert Evans and Christiana Pearson Evans. Her siblings included Christiana, Isaac, and twin younger siblings who died in infancy. Her brother Isaac was deeply important to her, and deeply painful. They were close as children, but when she later chose to live openly with George Henry Lewes, Isaac cut her off for years. Victorian respectability could be very brave when punishing women.

She never had biological children, but through Lewes she became emotionally involved with his sons. She helped support them, worried over them, and moved through life with the practical responsibilities of family even while polite society pretended her household did not count. That contradiction is everywhere in Eliot: the official rules say one thing, the actual emotional truth says another, and the actual truth is usually more humane.

romantic life

Before Lewes, Eliot had attachments and disappointments, including an intense feeling for the publisher John Chapman and admiration for philosopher Herbert Spencer, who did not return her feelings in the way she wanted. Then she met George Henry Lewes: critic, philosopher, messy married man, intellectual equal, and the person who changed her life.

Lewes was legally married to Agnes Jervis, but their marriage had broken down in a very Victorian disaster involving separation, other partners, and legal impossibility. Divorce was not realistically available to him. In 1854, Mary Ann and Lewes chose to live together anyway, openly, as husband and wife. Society punished her far more than him, obviously, because society loves a double standard and then calls it morality.

The relationship lasted until Lewes died in 1878. By most accounts, it was the great partnership of her life: intellectually alive, emotionally sustaining, scandalous, and useful in the best sense. He encouraged her fiction. She gave him loyalty and depth. After his death, she was shattered. In 1880 she married John Walter Cross, a much younger friend. During their honeymoon in Venice, Cross famously jumped or fell from a hotel balcony into the Grand Canal and survived, which is one of those incidents that sounds like a metaphor written by a hostile novelist. Eliot died later that same year.

life until death

By the 1870s, Eliot was famous, wealthy, respected, and still shadowed by the old scandal. Middlemarch appeared in installments from 1871 to 1872. Daniel Deronda followed in 1876. She had become a monument while still alive, but not a cold one. She was anxious, physically fragile, affectionate, exacting, often ill, and permanently interested in the moral machinery inside people.

George Henry Lewes died in 1878, and Eliot spent months editing his unfinished work and grieving him. Her marriage to John Cross in May 1880 restored her to some circles of conventional respectability, which is absurdly funny in a bleak way: after decades of being punished for loving one man faithfully, society became calmer once she legally married another. She died on December 22, 1880, aged sixty-one, after illness complicated by a throat infection and kidney disease. She was buried in Highgate Cemetery beside Lewes, not in Westminster Abbey, because respectability was still apparently taking attendance.

their works

Eliot is best read slowly. Her sentences are not trying to impress you quickly. They are trying to get under the floorboards of motive, shame, tenderness, and self-deception.

From Middlemarch

Miss Brooke had that kind of beauty which seems to be thrown into relief by poor dress. Her hand and wrist were so finely formed that she could wear sleeves not less bare of style than those in which the Blessed Virgin appeared to Italian painters; and her profile as well as her stature and bearing seemed to gain the more dignity from her plain garments, which by the side of provincial fashion gave her the impressiveness of a fine quotation from the Bible, or from one of our elder poets, in a paragraph of to-day's newspaper.
From Middlemarch, the famous ending

Her finely touched spirit had still its fine issues, though they were not widely visible. Her full nature, like that river of which Cyrus broke the strength, spent itself in channels which had no great name on the earth. But the effect of her being on those around her was incalculably diffusive: for the growing good of the world is partly dependent on unhistoric acts; and that things are not so ill with you and me as they might have been, is half owing to the number who lived faithfully a hidden life, and rest in unvisited tombs.
From The Mill on the Floss

We could never have loved the earth so well if we had had no childhood in it, if it were not the earth where the same flowers come up again every spring that we used to gather with our tiny fingers as we sat lisping to ourselves on the grass, the same hips and haws on the autumn hedgerows, the same redbreasts that we used to call "God's birds," because they did no harm to the precious crops.
From Silas Marner

In old days there were angels who came and took men by the hand and led them away from the city of destruction. We see no white-winged angels now. But yet men are led away from threatening destruction: a hand is put into theirs, which leads them forth gently towards a calm and bright land, so that they look no more backward; and the hand may be a little child's.
From Adam Bede

Falsehood is so easy, truth so difficult. The pencil is conscious of a delightful facility in drawing a griffin - the longer the claws, and the larger the wings, the better; but that marvellous facility which we mistook for genius is apt to forsake us when we want to draw a real unexaggerated lion.
From Daniel Deronda

It is one of the secrets in that change of mental poise which has been fitly named conversion, that to many among us neither heaven nor earth has any revelation till some personality touches theirs with a peculiar influence, subduing them into receptiveness.

And because Eliot could be brutally exact about human nature, here is one of her sharpest little knives from Middlemarch: "But what we call our despair is often only the painful eagerness of unfed hope." Rude. Accurate. Annoying when a dead Victorian woman clocks you from across the centuries.

Christina Rossetti

English poet, Pre-Raphaelite, devotional gothic romantic · 1830–1894

jul 25, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Christina Georgina Rossetti was born in London on December 5, 1830, the youngest of four children in a family that treated language like furniture: always present, useful, inherited, and slightly dramatic. Her father, Gabriele Rossetti, was an Italian poet, scholar, and political exile. Her mother, Frances Polidori Rossetti, came from a literary family too. Christina grew up in a house full of Italian, English, politics, religion, art, books, and the specific kind of tension that forms children who become too observant.

Her siblings were their own little weather systems. Dante Gabriel Rossetti became a famous painter and poet, one of the founders of the Pre-Raphaelite Brotherhood. William Michael became a critic and editor. Maria became a writer and later an Anglican nun. Christina was the youngest, sickly at times, intense, devout, imaginative, and stubborn in a quiet way. Not dramatic like throwing plates. More like refusing marriage twice because her conscience said no, then writing poems that make refusal sound like a locked chapel door.

When her father's health declined and the family finances tightened, the Rossetti women had to become practical. Christina's mother and sister worked as teachers. Christina herself was expected to become a governess, but illness and depression-like collapses pulled her back. She began publishing under the pseudonym Ellen Alleyne, and by her twenties she was already writing poems where innocence has teeth, desire has consequences, and death is less an ending than a room waiting quietly down the hall.

what she did & why she's remembered

Rossetti became one of the great Victorian poets, though she did it without turning herself into a public spectacle. She wrote devotional poems, love poems, children's verse, sonnets, lyrics, fairy-tale nightmares, hymns, and strange psychological miniatures that look simple until you read them twice and realize they have been quietly rearranging your bones.

Her 1862 collection Goblin Market and Other Poems made her reputation. Goblin Market is the famous one: two sisters, goblin merchants, forbidden fruit, hunger, temptation, rescue, and lines so lush they feel sticky on the fingers. People have read it as a children's fantasy, a Christian allegory, a poem about addiction, a warning about sexual exploitation, a celebration of sisterhood, and a fever dream about capitalism selling peaches with malicious intent. All of those readings fit because Rossetti knew how to make sweetness dangerous.

She is also remembered for poems like Remember, Echo, A Birthday, After Death, Song, and the Christmas carol In the Bleak Midwinter. Her gift was compression. She could make grief polite and then make the politeness more devastating than screaming. Very Victorian, very dangerous, very “I will not bother you with my pain, I will just immortalize it.”

family life

Christina's family was brilliant and heavy. Her father had fled Naples after political trouble and brought exile into the home like another relative. Her mother kept the family functioning when money became unstable. Dante Gabriel adored and overshadowed her; he illustrated her first major book, but his own fame often swallowed the room. William Michael preserved and edited her work after her death. Maria shared Christina's religious seriousness and eventually entered an Anglican sisterhood.

Christina never had children. Instead, her domestic life was threaded through mothers, siblings, nephews, charitable work, church, illness, writing, and an inner world that seems to have been both sanctuary and battlefield. She did volunteer work at a refuge for women in Highgate, including women society had already decided were ruined. That matters. Her poems are full of fallen girls, hungry girls, watched girls, dead girls, and women negotiating survival inside moral systems built by men who were very comfortable naming everyone else's sin.

romantic life

Rossetti's romantic life was mostly made of almosts, refusals, and the kind of restraint that sounds holy until you notice how much ache it contains. She was engaged to the painter James Collinson, a member of the Pre-Raphaelite circle, but broke it off after he returned to Roman Catholicism. For Christina, religious conviction was not decorative. If conscience said no, she obeyed it, even when her heart had to pay the bill.

Later she was close to Charles Cayley, a scholar and translator. He proposed. She refused again, apparently because of religious differences, but she cared for him deeply. That is the Rossetti wound: not lack of feeling, but feeling disciplined into renunciation. She did not write like someone untouched by love. She wrote like someone who had put love behind glass and could still hear it breathing.

life until death

Rossetti's adult life was marked by recurring illness. In the 1870s she suffered from Graves' disease, which altered her appearance and drained her strength. Later she developed breast cancer, underwent surgery in 1892, and endured recurrence. Through it all she kept writing: devotional prose, poems, children's verse, and late works where death becomes almost domestic, like a visitor whose knock she has been expecting for years.

She died in London on December 29, 1894, aged sixty-four, and was buried in Highgate Cemetery. After death, her reputation shifted again and again. Some readers tried to make her merely sweet and religious. Others tried to make her secretly rebellious in every line. The truth is better: she was both devout and strange, obedient and subversive, tender and severe. A woman can kneel and still be holding a knife. Literature keeps forgetting this and then rediscovering it like it's news.

their works

Rossetti's words are public domain now, which is good because she should be read directly. She has that rare gift where a line looks plain, then ambushes you three seconds later.

remember

a love poem that begins as a request and ends as mercy

Remember me when I am gone away,
Gone far away into the silent land;
When you can no more hold me by the hand,
Nor I half turn to go yet turning stay.
Remember me when no more day by day
You tell me of our future that you planned:
Only remember me; you understand
It will be late to counsel then or pray.

Yet if you should forget me for a while
And afterwards remember, do not grieve:
For if the darkness and corruption leave
A vestige of the thoughts that once I had,
Better by far you should forget and smile
Than that you should remember and be sad.

song

death, but make it quiet enough to hurt more

When I am dead, my dearest,
Sing no sad songs for me;
Plant thou no roses at my head,
Nor shady cypress tree:
Be the green grass above me
With showers and dewdrops wet;
And if thou wilt, remember,
And if thou wilt, forget.

I shall not see the shadows,
I shall not feel the rain;
I shall not hear the nightingale
Sing on, as if in pain:
And dreaming through the twilight
That doth not rise nor set,
Haply I may remember,
And haply may forget.

a birthday

rare Rossetti joy: ornate, excessive, almost medieval

My heart is like a singing bird
Whose nest is in a watered shoot;
My heart is like an apple-tree
Whose boughs are bent with thickset fruit;
My heart is like a rainbow shell
That paddles in a halcyon sea;
My heart is gladder than all these
Because my love is come to me.

Raise me a dais of silk and down;
Hang it with vair and purple dyes;
Carve it in doves and pomegranates,
And peacocks with a hundred eyes;
Work it in gold and silver grapes,
In leaves and silver fleurs-de-lys;
Because the birthday of my life
Is come, my love is come to me.

from goblin market

the opening is basically a cursed fruit stall whispering at girls after dark

Morning and evening
Maids heard the goblins cry:
“Come buy our orchard fruits,
Come buy, come buy:
Apples and quinces,
Lemons and oranges,
Plump unpecked cherries,
Melons and raspberries,
Bloom-down-cheeked peaches,
Swart-headed mulberries,
Wild free-born cranberries,
Crab-apples, dewberries,
Pine-apples, blackberries,
Apricots, strawberries;—
All ripe together
In summer weather,—
Morns that pass by,
Fair eves that fly;
Come buy, come buy.”

from goblin market · sisterhood

this is why the poem refuses to stay small

For there is no friend like a sister
In calm or stormy weather;
To cheer one on the tedious way,
To fetch one if one goes astray,
To lift one if one totters down,
To strengthen whilst one stands.

in the bleak midwinter

her Christmas poem, later set to music, has that cold devotional ache

In the bleak midwinter
Frosty wind made moan,
Earth stood hard as iron,
Water like a stone;
Snow had fallen, snow on snow,
Snow on snow,
In the bleak midwinter,
Long ago.

Mary Wollstonecraft

English writer, philosopher, feminist ancestor · 1759–1797

jul 25, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Mary Wollstonecraft was born on April 27, 1759, in Spitalfields, London, the second of seven children. She did not grow up in a tidy little house of enlightenment where everyone politely encouraged the future feminist icon. Her family started with some comfort, then slid downward as her father, Edward John Wollstonecraft, burned through money on failed schemes and became violent when drunk. Mary learned early that the family was not always safety. Sometimes the daughter becomes the guard dog.

As a girl, she reportedly placed herself outside her mother's bedroom door to protect her from her father's rages. That image explains a lot. Wollstonecraft's feminism did not come from abstract theory first. It came from watching dependence become a trap. She saw what happened when women had no money, no education, no legal power, and no socially acceptable exit. Then she spent her life making that argument impossible to ignore.

She was not formally educated like a boy of her class might have been, so she built herself out of friendships, reading, work, anger, and sheer refusal. Her closest early friend was Fanny Blood, a gifted young woman whose intelligence and gentleness Mary adored. Together with Mary's sisters Eliza and Everina, they tried to run a school at Newington Green. It was idealistic, financially fragile, and very much a woman in her twenties trying to build stability out of air. Relatable, irritatingly.

what she did & why she's remembered

Wollstonecraft worked as a companion, teacher, governess, translator, reviewer, novelist, political writer, and professional intellectual at a time when women were supposed to be decorative dependents. Her early book Thoughts on the Education of Daughters came from the hard little school of experience. She knew the scripts women were handed because she had been forced to live inside them.

In 1790, she answered Edmund Burke's conservative attack on the French Revolution with A Vindication of the Rights of Men. Then in 1792, she published A Vindication of the Rights of Woman, the work that made her dangerous. Her argument was not that women should be adored, protected, or placed on a sentimental pedestal. She hated that nonsense. She argued that women are rational human beings, and if they seem weak, vain, childish, or manipulative, society has trained them that way and then blamed them for the results. Very neat little scam, patriarchy. Zero stars.

Her feminism was sharp because it refused flattery. She did not simply say women were naturally angelic. She said women had been made artificial by dependence, bad education, and the pressure to please men. Give them serious education, economic usefulness, bodily strength, and moral agency, she argued, and they could become companions rather than ornaments. She wanted women to stop being trained like pretty pets and start being treated like citizens.

family life

Mary's parents were Edward John Wollstonecraft and Elizabeth Dixon. The household had seven children, and Mary became especially involved in the lives of her sisters. When her sister Eliza was miserable after childbirth and possibly in danger inside her marriage, Mary helped her leave her husband and child, an almost unthinkable act in that period. It damaged Eliza socially, but Mary believed survival mattered more than obedience. She was not always gentle, but she was serious about escape routes.

She later became a mother herself. Her first daughter, Fanny Imlay, was born in France in 1794 during Mary's relationship with the American Gilbert Imlay. Her second daughter, Mary Wollstonecraft Godwin, was born in 1797 after Mary married the philosopher William Godwin. That baby would grow up to become Mary Shelley, author of Frankenstein. Imagine being born into that family tree: feminism, anarchist philosophy, gothic horror, dead mothers, bad men, brilliance everywhere. A haunted library with childcare issues.

romantic life

Her romantic life was intense, messy, and deeply human. She fell for the painter Henry Fuseli, who was already married. Mary, being Mary, reportedly proposed a kind of emotional living arrangement with Fuseli and his wife. The wife said absolutely not, because even radical history occasionally has one person with boundaries.

Then came Gilbert Imlay, an American adventurer and businessman she met in revolutionary France. He registered her as his wife to protect her during the war, though they were not legally married. They had Fanny, but Imlay drifted, betrayed, and abandoned her emotionally. Mary tried to hold the relationship together with letters that are painful to read: proud woman, brilliant mind, reduced to begging someone mediocre to love her properly. Rude how common that plot remains.

After Imlay's rejection, she attempted suicide twice. Later, she found something steadier with William Godwin. Their courtship began intellectually: two radicals circling each other through conversation and letters. They married in March 1797 when she was pregnant. It was brief, but by most accounts genuinely affectionate. For once, she had a partner who respected her mind, though even Godwin would accidentally damage her reputation after death by telling the truth too nakedly in his memoir.

life until death

Wollstonecraft spent her last years writing, mothering, loving, recovering, and trying to keep the inside of herself from collapsing under the weight of disappointment. Her Letters Written During a Short Residence in Sweden, Norway, and Denmark, published in 1796, came from a journey she took for Imlay's business, but it became something stranger and more beautiful: travel writing mixed with philosophy, loneliness, landscape, politics, motherhood, and heartbreak. It made Godwin fall in love with her. He later said that if ever there was a book calculated to make a man in love with its author, it was that one.

On August 30, 1797, she gave birth to her second daughter. The placenta did not come away properly, infection followed, and after days of agony she died on September 10, 1797, at only thirty-eight. Godwin was devastated. He published Memoirs of the Author of A Vindication of the Rights of Woman in 1798, intending to honor her, but his honesty about her affairs, suicide attempts, and child outside marriage scandalized the public. Her reputation suffered for decades. Naturally, men could live like overturned liquor cabinets and remain geniuses, but Mary had to be punished for having a body and feelings.

She came back anyway. Later feminists reclaimed her as a founding thinker, not because she was perfect, but because she saw the machinery. She understood that oppression does not only lock doors. It teaches people to call the locked room natural.

their works

Wollstonecraft is best read as someone arguing with a whole civilization while trying not to bleed on the page. Her prose is formal by modern standards, but underneath the eighteenth-century structure there is heat, disgust, tenderness, and a scary amount of clarity.

from a vindication of the rights of woman · the opening argument

she begins by attacking the idea that women should be trained for charm instead of reason

I wish to persuade women to endeavour to acquire strength, both of mind and body, and to convince them that the soft phrases, susceptibility of heart, delicacy of sentiment, and refinement of taste, are almost synonymous with epithets of weakness.

Those beings who are only the objects of pity and that kind of love, which has been termed its sister, will soon become objects of contempt.

from a vindication · against ornamental women

this is the part where she basically says pretty dependency is still dependency

Taught from their infancy that beauty is woman's sceptre, the mind shapes itself to the body, and roaming round its gilt cage, only seeks to adorn its prison.

Men have various employments and pursuits which engage their attention, and give a character to the opening mind; but women, confined to one, and having their thoughts constantly directed to the most insignificant part of themselves, seldom extend their views beyond the triumph of the hour.

from a vindication · women as companions

her goal was not worship. it was partnership

Besides, the woman who strengthens her body and exercises her mind will, by managing her family and practising various virtues, become the friend, and not the humble dependent of her husband.

If marriage be the cement of society, mankind should all be educated after the same model, or the intercourse of the sexes will never deserve the name of fellowship.

from a vindication · on taught weakness

she refuses the lazy claim that women are simply naturally inferior

I do not wish them to have power over men; but over themselves.

Make women rational creatures, and free citizens, and they will quickly become good wives; that is, if men do not neglect the duties of husbands and fathers.

from letters written in sweden, norway, and denmark

this is Mary less as polemicist, more as lonely traveler with a mind that cannot stop feeling the weather

The art of travelling is only a branch of the art of thinking.

I am then going to be the first of a new genus. I tremble at the attempt.

from letters to imlay

the painful private voice behind the public steel

I have been treated with unkindness, and even cruelty; but I will not allow myself to be angry.

I have not the least expectation that I can now ever know happiness again.

That last sentence was written after one of the lowest points of her life. It is bleak, but it is not the ending. She still wrote. She still loved again. Her daughter still wrote Frankenstein. History is sometimes just one woman surviving long enough for another woman to inherit the fire.

Ada Lovelace

English mathematician, writer, first computer programmer · 1815–1852

jul 24, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Augusta Ada Byron was born in London on December 10, 1815, the only legitimate child of the poet Lord Byron and Annabella Milbanke. It sounds romantic until you remember Lord Byron was basically a walking scandal with excellent meter and catastrophic husband settings. Her parents separated when Ada was only weeks old. Byron left England soon after and never saw his daughter again.

Annabella feared that Ada might inherit her father's wild imagination, so she tried to train it out of her with mathematics, logic, discipline, and tutors. This backfired beautifully. Ada did not become less imaginative. She became imaginative with structure, which is far more dangerous. As a child she was fascinated by machines and even designed a flying apparatus after studying birds, materials, steam, and wings. She called the project Flyology, because apparently even child geniuses need a slightly unserious brand name.

Illness shadowed her youth. She had bouts of poor health, and after measles in 1829 she was temporarily paralyzed and bedridden for months. While other people might have become smaller from confinement, Ada became stranger and sharper. She read, calculated, imagined engines, and grew into a mind that wanted poetry and mathematics to stop pretending they were enemies.

what she did & why she's remembered

Ada met Charles Babbage in 1833 when she was seventeen. Babbage showed her part of his Difference Engine, a mechanical calculator, and Ada immediately understood that she was not looking at a toy. She was looking at a new kind of mind made of brass, wheels, cards, and instruction.

Years later, Babbage designed the Analytical Engine, a more ambitious machine that was never fully built in their lifetimes. In 1842, the Italian engineer Luigi Menabrea published a French paper describing it. Ada translated the paper into English, then added notes so extensive they became three times longer than the original article. Those notes are why we still talk about her.

In Note G, she described a step-by-step method by which the Analytical Engine could calculate Bernoulli numbers. This is often called the first published computer program: an algorithm intended to be carried out by a machine. But the more interesting part is not just that she wrote instructions. It is that she saw the machine's future before the machine existed. Babbage saw calculation. Ada saw symbolic processing. She understood that if numbers could represent relationships, then a machine could manipulate not only quantities, but music, letters, and abstract patterns. Very annoying of her to be born a century early and still be right.

family life

Ada's family life was never simple. Her father, Lord Byron, became more myth than parent: absent, famous, beautiful, messy, and dead by the time Ada was eight. Her mother Annabella was intelligent, strict, mathematically trained, and determined that Ada would not become Byron in a dress. Ada grew up surrounded by tutors, moral supervision, and her mother's anxious control.

Ada married William King in 1835. He later became Earl of Lovelace, making her Countess of Lovelace. They had three children: Byron, Anne Isabella, and Ralph Gordon. Motherhood and aristocratic duty interrupted her studies, but did not kill them. When domestic life allowed, she returned to mathematics with the hunger of someone who knew ordinary society had already underestimated her.

romantic life

Ada's marriage to William King was socially suitable and seems to have given her some practical support, especially compared with the total disaster buffet that was Byron's marital legacy. But Ada was not a quiet Victorian angel. She was intense, ambitious, restless, and drawn to intellectual electricity. Her correspondence with Babbage had the charge of two obsessive minds sparking around an impossible machine.

There were rumors of flirtations and possible affairs later in her life, and her gambling circle pulled her into financial trouble. But the deepest romance in Ada's story is probably not one man. It is the romance of pattern itself. She wanted what she called "poetical science": imagination disciplined by mathematics, intuition sharpened until it could cut metal.

life until death

Ada's later years were physically painful and financially chaotic. She tried to apply mathematics to horse-race gambling, convinced that a system could be found. It did not go well. There is something painfully human there: the woman who could imagine computation beyond her century still believed she could outthink chance at the track. Genius does not protect anyone from bad decisions, rude but useful fact.

She developed uterine cancer and suffered through brutal nineteenth-century treatments, including heavy doses of laudanum. She died on November 27, 1852, at only thirty-six, the same age her father had been when he died. At her request, she was buried beside Lord Byron in the Byron family vault at Hucknall, Nottinghamshire. During her life, her work received little attention. In the twentieth century, when computers finally became real, people looked back and realized Ada had been standing in the doorway before the room was built.

their works

Ada did not leave poems like Sappho or Dickinson. Her surviving masterpiece is prose about a machine that did not yet exist, and the strange thing is how alive it feels. She writes like someone watching the future assemble itself in a mirror.

from note a · on the analytical engine

this is the famous passage where she realizes the machine is not merely a calculator

Again, it might act upon other things besides number, were objects found whose mutual fundamental relations could be expressed by those of the abstract science of operations, and which should be also susceptible of adaptations to the action of the operating notation and mechanism of the engine.

Supposing, for instance, that the fundamental relations of pitched sounds in the science of harmony and of musical composition were susceptible of such expression and adaptations, the engine might compose elaborate and scientific pieces of music of any degree of complexity or extent.

from note a · the jacquard loom comparison

one of the most beautiful lines ever written about computing

We may say most aptly, that the Analytical Engine weaves algebraical patterns just as the Jacquard-loom weaves flowers and leaves.

from note g · on what machines can and cannot do

later Alan Turing called this "Lady Lovelace's objection"

The Analytical Engine has no pretensions whatever to originate anything. It can do whatever we know how to order it to perform. It can follow analysis; but it has no power of anticipating any analytical relations or truths.

from note g · the bernoulli numbers

the setup for the algorithm that became her programming legacy

We will terminate these Notes by following up in detail the steps through which the engine could compute the Numbers of Bernoulli, this being, in the form in which we shall deduce it, a rather complicated example of its powers.

The actual table she published is not pretty reading unless you enjoy Victorian assembly language wearing a corset. But that table matters because it lays out operations, variables, and sequence for a machine. The romance is in the dryness: a woman in 1843 calmly writing instructions for a computer the world had not learned to build yet.

from a letter · poetical science

the line that explains her whole brain

If you can't give me poetry, can't you give me poetical science?

Sor Juana Inés de la Cruz

Mexican poet, nun, scholar · the Phoenix of Mexico · 1651–1695

jul 23, 2026 ▼

childhood & early life

Juana Ramírez de Asbaje was born in San Miguel Nepantla, in New Spain, the world that would later become Mexico. The date is usually given as November 12, 1651, though some records point to 1648. She was born outside marriage to Isabel Ramírez and Pedro Manuel de Asbaje, which meant she entered the world already slightly outside the neat boxes society preferred. Naturally, she responded by becoming too brilliant to fit in any of them. Good for her. Extremely Scorpio behavior, actually.

As a child, she lived for a time on her maternal grandfather's hacienda. There was a library there, and the little girl treated it like contraband treasure. She hid in the chapel to read books that were not meant for girls. By three, according to early biographies, she had learned to read. By eight, she had written a religious poem. As a girl, she begged to be allowed to dress as a boy so she could attend university. Her family refused, because history is basically one long customer-service ticket filed by women against mediocre rules.

She studied Latin with frightening speed, learned Nahuatl, read philosophy, theology, logic, music, science, and literature. She was not educated in the ordinary institutional sense. She built herself by theft, hunger, discipline, and memory. Later she would write that she studied everything because everything might lead to God, but there is another truth under that: she wanted to know because not knowing felt like suffocation.

what she did & why she's remembered

As a teenager, Juana was sent to Mexico City to live with relatives, and her intelligence became public entertainment. At the viceregal court, scholars tested her like a curiosity. She answered them. She became lady-in-waiting to the vicereine, entered the bright, dangerous world of court patronage, and could have married well. Instead, she looked at marriage and saw intellectual captivity. She later wrote that she had a "total disinclination to marriage" and wanted no fixed obligation that would limit her freedom to study.

So she became a nun. Not because she was meek. Because the convent was the only place a woman could build a life around books without being handed to a husband. After a brief attempt with the Discalced Carmelites, whose severity broke her health, she entered the Convent of San Jerónimo in 1669. Her cell became less like a monk's room and more like the lair of a beautiful academic dragon: books, maps, scientific instruments, music, art, visitors, arguments.

She wrote poetry, plays, religious songs, philosophical verse, satire, letters, and courtly pieces. Her Primero sueño is a 975-line poem about the soul's attempt to climb toward total knowledge while the body sleeps. Her Respuesta a Sor Filotea defended women's right to learning with surgical politeness. Her poem Hombres necios, usually translated as Foolish Men, drags male hypocrisy so cleanly it still has bite more than three hundred years later.

family life

Her mother, Isabel Ramírez, managed property and raised children in a colonial society that gave women little formal authority while quietly depending on their competence. Juana had sisters, including Josefa María and María, and grew up close enough to family networks that they could send her to Mexico City when her gifts became impossible to ignore. Her father seems to have been absent from her daily life. There is no soft domestic legend around him, no powerful father shaping the genius. If anything, Sor Juana's life reads like a woman becoming her own authority because nobody else was going to do the job properly.
